• Accredited Entities should provide summary information in the proposal with cross-reference to annexes such as feasibility studies, gender action plan, term sheet, etc.
• Accreditations are valid for 3 years from date of issuance. Any changes to the data provided in the funding proposal. Updates to the funding proposal and/or annexes must be reflected accordingly.
• The total number of pages for the funding proposal (excluding annexes) should not exceed 20 pages. Proposals exceeding the prescribed length will not be assessed within the service standard time.
• The recommended font is Arial, size 11.

## A.19. Complementarity
Does the project/programme complement other climate finance funding (e.g., GEF, CIF, etc.)? If yes, please elaborate in section B.1.

## A.20. Executing Entity
The Government of Liberia (GoL), represented by the Environmental Protection Agency (EPA) is responsible for implementing and managing this project.

## A.21. Executive summary (max. 750 words, approximately 1.5 pages)
### 1. Liberia's capital city, Monrovia:
- Is extremely vulnerable to the climate change impacts of sea-level rise (SLR) and increased frequency and intensity of storms.
- The projected SLR of more than 30 cm by 2050 will cause coastal erosion and shoreline retreat.
- Coastal erosion is a significant contributor to accelerated coastal erosion, and along with the increasing intensity of offshore storms, it has caused the coastline to regress by 30% leading to the loss of 67 km² of land in Monrovia's coastal infrastructure over the past decade. Additionally, SLR threatens the sustainability of ecosystem services provided by mangrove forests.
- The vulnerability is exacerbated further by urban encroachment and over-exploitation of the mangroves.

### 2. Most vulnerable part of the MMA:
- West Point, an impoverished and densely-populated informal settlement situated on a narrow spit between the coast and the Mesurado Wetland with buildings built up to the shoreline.
- Coastal erosion has caused the coastline to regress by 30%, leading to the loss of 67 km² of land in Monrovia's coastal infrastructure over the past decade.

### 3. Project objectives:
- To adapt to the severe impacts of climate change on Monrovia's coast, it is necessary to change the current management approach and adopt a more integrated and participatory planning that involves the public sector, private sector, and communities at all levels of governance.
- The proposed project is requesting GCF support to address barriers to effective climate change adaptation in West Point and the surrounding coastal areas. The project will focus on four interrelated focus areas: (i) coastal protection; (ii) coastal management; and (iii) diversified coastal-resilient livelihoods. In this way, the project aims at reducing the vulnerability of approximately 10,800 people in West Point and avoiding damages up to US$47 million to the individual and communal property of West Point residents as well as securing such sites for fishing boats.

### 4. Project timeline:
- The project will address one of the most urgent adaptation needs in Monrovia by constructing a rock revetment to protect West Point against coastal erosion and storms.
- The investment was selected as the preferred solution, because it is technically feasible, cost-effective, and sustainable over time.
- However, the sustainability of this option would be limited due to the regular maintenance required with no other alternative for shoreline protection in the northern portion of the proposed revetment.

### 5. Project implementation:
- The project will address immediate adaptation priorities and creating an enabling environment for upscaling coastal adaptation policies and practices at national level.
- The project is expected to reduce vulnerability by approximately 80% over a period of 20 years, with more than half the benefits expected under a non-climate or baseline scenario.

## The project will have a significant impact on the fisheries sector.

The construction of this coastal protection infrastructure will form part of a strategic, cohesive coastal adaptation strategy using an Integrated Coastal Zone Management (ICZM) approach.

### 5.1. Planning shall be necessary for adopting an evidence-based and participatory ICZM approach across Liberia

This will be facilitated by the proposed project through initiatives to strengthen the technical and institutional capacity of the government and communities to adapt to the rapidly changing coastal landscape and to undertake long-term, climate-resilient development.

### 6. The proposed project will increase local adaptive capacity by strengthening gender- and climate-sensitive livelihoods and protecting mangroves in the Mesurado Wetland within Monrovia

Specifically, adaptive capacity building will be achieved through:

i) Strengthening the resilience of these ecosystems to climate change, through community co-management agreements between government institutions and communities, which will ensure that local communities are able to adopt sustainable practices; and ii) strengthening climate-sensitive livelihoods and supporting the uptake of climate-resilient livelihoods. This is an important element of the integrated approach because while the development of ICZMP will enable coastal adaptation in the project area, it is also critical for ensuring long-term resilience.

### 7. These investments by the GCF and the Government of Liberia (GoL) will catalyze a paradigm shift in the management of Monrovia’s coastal zone towards an integrated, transformative and proactive approach that maximizes the benefits of climate change adaptation while minimizing the risks to coastal ecosystems in adaptation efforts

This will directly benefit a total of ~250,000 people in the communities of West Point, Paynesville, and J. H. K. Town, as well as indirectly benefiting over 1 million people through the protection of mangrove ecosystems in the communities of Topo Village, Pionor and Flimahar, and Nipay Town and Jacob’s Town. In addition, the project will indirectly benefit approximately one million people through the adaptation benefits that will be realized from the coastal zone management approach being implemented focused on the Monrovia Metropolitan Area (SMA). The combination of direct and indirect beneficiaries under this project set ultimately confer adaptation benefits to one quarter of the total population of Liberia.

### 7.1. Further information on the overall strategy and sustainability of the proposed project can be found in Section B.5.
### 7.2. The project will benefit all communities within Monrovia, whereas indirect benefits will occur at the municipal scale — i.e., the population of MML, which is estimated at one million people.

B. PROJECT/PROGRAMME INFORMATION

### B.1. Climate Change Context (max: 1000 words, approximately 2 pages)

#### Context

8. Even with capital, Monrovia, has a population of over one million people, with many of the city's most vulnerable communities located along the highly exposed coast zone. These communities are largely reliant on fishery-based livelihoods and are situated in low-lying coastal areas that extend right up to the shoreline*. Although the combination of accessible natural resources and proximity to central markets makes neighbourhoods in the coastal zone popular for local residents, their exposure to climate change impacts is high due to the physical characteristics of the shoreline offering very little protection from storm surges and sea-level rise. This has made these communities extremely vulnerable to the impacts of climate change. With the physical characteristics of the shoreline offering very little protection from storm surges and sea-level rise, this has made these communities extremely vulnerable to the impacts of climate change.

9. Over the last decade, accelerated coastal erosion has caused shorelines retreat of ~30 m at the Monrovia coastline. This is a significant loss in terms of land area and has resulted in the abandonment of fishing sites that are critical to fishery-based livelihoods. The loss of dwellings and infrastructure due to accelerated coastal erosion between 2008 and 2018 is clearly visible in Figure 1. A detailed assessment of the different factors (both climate change and climate related) contributing to coastal erosion at West Point can be found in Sections 2.10 and 3.1 of the FS†.

* Further information on Monrovia's coastal communities' socio-economic conditions is provided in Annex ZA, Feasibility Study, Section 1.
† Section 3.1 of Annex ZA, Feasibility Study provides details of the two major impacts of climate change in Monrovia: flooding and coastal erosion. Section 2.10 of Annex ZA, Feasibility Study provides a more detailed account of the factors contributing to coastal erosion at West Point.
‡ Section 3.3 of Annex ZA, Feasibility Study provides details on the impacts of climate change on mangrove ecosystems in Liberia and the Monrovia coastline.

§ Further information on the two major impacts of climate change in Monrovia: flooding and coastal erosion resulting in shoreline retreat is provided in Annex ZA, Feasibility Study. Section 2.10 of Annex ZA, Feasibility Study elaborates on the biophysical factors that have resulted in coastal retreat by summarizing the factors contributing to coastal erosion at West Point. Section 3.1 provides details of the two major impacts of climate change in Monrovia: flooding and coastal erosion resulting in shoreline retreat.

## Climate change impacts

10. Accelerated coastal erosion, resulting from a combination of sea-level rise (SLR) and an increase in the frequency of high-intensity storms, poses a substantial threat to the homes, assets, and livelihoods of coastal communities in Monroviea. This is particularly concerning for the Meurado Wetland, which serves as a critical habitat for species on which many livelihoods depend. An analysis of these two climate change impacts, summarized below, has informed the design of this project. The project will support urgent coastal protection and reduce vulnerability to those impacts of climate change. Modeling of additional climate variables, related to precipitation and temperature, has also informed aspects of the project design.

11. Climate projections under Representative Concentration Pathway (RCP) 8.5 predict SLR (Figure 2) of 75 cm by 2100. This is a conservative estimate assuming no contribution from melting ice sheets, as well as an increase in the frequency of high-intensity storms resulting in an increased offshore significant wave height**. The combined effect of these two climate change impacts will result in accelerated coastal erosion and flooding, threatening local communities and coastal infrastructure (see FS Section 3.2). In addition to increasing coastal erosion, climate change will impact vulnerable coastal communities in Monroviea through **degradation of the

### Figure 3: Projected changes in sea level by RCP4.5 and RCP8.5

12. Section 3.3.2 of the FS – “near-term modeling impact of SLR, waves and sediment transport under future climate conditions” – demonstrates that five of the most vulnerable sections of Monroe’s coastline (Figure 3). West Point (delineated Section 2) is at the most risk in terms of both exposure and sensitivity*. In West Point, erosion induced by increased wave energy and storm surge will be exacerbated by sediment starvation. Increased sediment transport will result in coastal retreat of an additional 10m by 2100 (Figure 4 and Figure 5). This is equivalent to an additional 30% loss of the existing shoreline at West Point.

Between 2008 and 2018, observed shoreline regression was 30 m at West Point, with further losses up to 10m occurring in the next decade. The projected erosion rate for West Point under RCP4.5 is approximately 60% higher than that of the rest of Monroe’s coastline (note that expected changes under RCP8.5 are also provided in the FS over the same time period (see FS, Section 3.3). This translates to a loss of approximately 120m at West Point by 2100 and an additional 40m by 2150.

Given its extreme exposure and vulnerability, West Point was selected as the main target site for coastal protection measures in this project. The projected erosion rate under RCP8.5 is expected to be approximately double that of RCP4.5 (see FS Section 3 for more changes under RCP8.5) with a return period of 100 years under RCP8.5.

* Climate change degrades mangroves through inundation because of SLR and through changes in water temperature, salinity and sediment transport. The projected loss of West Point’s mangrove forest will have significant impacts on the protection of coastal communities in Liberia through protection, planning and livelihood creation – building blocks towards Liberia’s marine and coastal spatial planning.

https://www.oceanconservation.org/locally-based-sustainable-communities/mangroves/the-little-mangroves-project/

### Annex 2.0 provides more comprehensive baseline information and analysis for the mangrove components of the project. This analysis is based on the best available data, employing a combination of quantitative analysis and qualitative assessment techniques. The following are the key findings:

- Identification of degradation trends and hotspots using time-series spatial data;
- Problems and root causes of mangrove degradation; and
- Relevant past and ongoing projects, and lessons learned and best practices.

**Mangrove systems around Monrovia are important breeding grounds for various commercially viable aquatic species, including fish.**

*For further details on the importance of the mangrove ecosystems in the Mesurado Wetland near Monrovia, see Section 3.1 of Annex Z: Vulnerability Sub-assessment. For further details on the methodology and data used to produce the maps provided in Section 2 of Annex 2.0: Mangrove Sub-assessment while a summary is provided in Section 5 of Annex 2A: Feasibility Study.*

*A detailed analysis of mangrove degradation trends, hotspots, and root causes will be conducted using secondary data; proposed project activities will be revised. The revised activities will then be presented to relevant Liberia stakeholders for validation and feedback. This will be followed by a final review of the EIA. This analysis will be used to reclassify Output 3 based on the feedback received from GCF, as well as the validation described above.*

Figure E. Map showing spatial location and graph showing loss of mangrove cover (ha) over time in areas of the Mesurado Wetlands between 2002 and 2019. Data supplied by Globe Mangroves.

15. Climate change is likely to exacerbate the impacts of baseline degradation and compromise the ecosystem services provided by the wetlands. SLR (Fig. 2) presents a substantial threat to mangroves in the Mesurado Wetlands, as it is likely to increase the depth of the water beyond the threshold at which they can survive, thereby reducing the area in which they are able to grow and reproduce. The projected impacts on mangrove cover over time under future climate conditions is likely to place physiological strain on the mangrove and reduce their ability to photosynthesize and produce oxygen. This will have a direct impact on the ecosystem services provided by wetlands as described in Section 4.3 of the FS (Annex 2A). As a result of these impacts on mangroves, climate change will impact the livelihoods of people who depend on them for their sustenance. The majority of fishers and farmers in Monrovia depend on their livelihoods. Given that most fishermen are women, the loss of the mangroves will disproportionately affect women and exacerbate existing gender inequality.

**Temperature**

16. Projections indicate that maximum temperatures are likely to increase towards the end of the century. An increase in the number of hot days (where the maximum temperature exceeds 30°C) is also expected under future climate conditions. This will have a significant impact on mangrove ecosystems and other coastal habitats, including the mangroves in the Mesurado Wetland. The optimum leaf temperature for mangrove photosynthesis is 29–32°C, while the upper limit of leaf temperature for optimal growth is 40°C. Any increase above this threshold will reduce mangrove growth below this threshold. Similarly, the impacts of temperature changes on fisheries and other livelihoods are likely to be more pronounced in areas where they are economically important species (Further information provided in Section 4 of the FS). The impacts of changes temperatures on sensitive ecosystems have been considered in the design of mangrove conservation initiatives under Output 3 of the project.

Precipitation

17. An increase in the frequency of high-intensity storms is projected under future climate conditions (Figure 9). This is likely to occur as described above. Increased intensity and frequency of high-intensity storms will increase flood risk in parts of Montserrat, reducing the ability of boats to safely operate by making landing sites unsafe and decreasing the number of days where fishers are able to safely operate. The design of the coastal protection structures should take into account these changes. This may also impact the use of existing and potential launching sites for fishers. Unlike the increases in temperature, increased precipitation will not necessarily have a substantial effect on sea surface temperatures or wave heights as most of Montserrat’s coastline is sheltered from large wave facing and are partially protected from storm surges. Projections for overall trends in precipitation vary between models and scenarios. Further details of these projections and impacts are provided in Sections 3 and 4 of the FS.

19. In addition to the projects listed above, Liberia’s approved Readiness Proposal supports the Government of Liberia for its efforts in strengthening its capacity to implement NAP processes in climate sectoral actions and development of key outputs. These outputs are summarized below, along with test demonstrating complementarity with the activities proposed under this project.

**(A) Strengthening institutional frameworks and coordination for implementation of the NAP process:** The proposed project will support Liberia’s national resolution to strengthening Liberia’s adaptation-related institutions, particularly the national ministries responsible for coastal zone management, i.e., the Ministry of Mines and Energy (MINE), Ministry of Finance and Development Planning (MFDP), and the National Environment Agency (NEA). This project will strengthen Liberia's existing ICZM framework by working with relevant government institutions to develop a national adaptation plan. The project will support the development of an NAP process through a participatory and consultative process. Development of the plan will be led by a service provider with experience in developing NAPs, supported by technical assistance from the GCF. A draft NAP document will be developed through a phased approach, whereby the first phase will entail producing a high-resolution, multi-criteria analysis of coastal vulnerability to climate change and sea-level rise. The second phase will focus on building an effective implementation of the ICZM across the relevant government sectors, an ICZM Committee and Cross-Sectoral Working Group (CSWG) will be established to ensure that all relevant government sectors are aligned in terms of integration of climate risks and the alignment and conformity of the ICZM across the relevant government sectors. The CSWG will develop a detailed action plan for the implementation of the ICZM, identifying sector-specific responsibilities and timelines for implementation. This will include the development of an NAP document that will be implemented in Monrovia with support from the GCF grant financing of the project. The final phase of this project will focus on building capacity to implement the NAP across all relevant government sectors.

**(B) Expansion of the knowledge base for climate change adaptation:** Cross-sectoral capacity development and knowledge sharing are critical components of the implementation process, as they will ensure that outputs from the project can be effectively implemented. Specific aspects that address the need to expand the national knowledge base and create an enabling environment to scale up the implementation of the NAP include: Sub-activity 2.1.3 is to implement the ICZM through long-term training via a Training-of-Trainers (ToT) approach, which will be conducted in collaboration with relevant government institutions. The ToT workshops on ICZM planning and implementation will be capacity building for the CSWG and additional representatives from all relevant government sectors. As part of this process, a detailed action plan for sector-specific responsibilities and timelines for implementation will be developed. Sub-activity 2.1.4 is to develop guidelines and regulations. Capitalizing technical personnel responsible for overseeing the uptake of implementation of GCF and other climate change adaptation projects in Liberia, the CSWG will also work with the MIA as well as facilitating upscaling across the country.

**(C) Building capacity for mainstreaming climate change adaptation into planning and budgeting processes:** Systematic integration of climate change considerations is critical to mainstreaming of climate change impacts. # GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL 2/0 | PAGE 17 OF 97

## (B) Formulation of financing mechanisms for scaling: The projects main contribution to innovative finance mechanisms that create an enabling environment for upscaling is two-fold. First, the project capacity of vulnerable communities and institutions to access climate-smart financing will be enhanced through supportive credit-based products through sustainable co-management of mangrove areas and providing training to catalyse the uptake of diversified livelihoods in coastal communities. Second, the project will contribute to scaling-up the use of clean energy in the national power sector by supporting the development of a market for solar-powered cold storage facilities that can be used to manufacture and sell eco-friendly products such as energy-efficient cookstoves. To this end, Activity 3.4 is designed to provide technical assistance to the Ministry of Energy and Water Resources (MoEWR) in developing a national policy on clean energy and supporting the development of a market for solar-powered cold storage facilities that can be used to manufacture and sell eco-friendly products such as energy-efficient cookstoves.

### 2.3. Theory of change (max. 1000 words, approximately 2 pages plus diagram)

#### Project theory of change

The proposed project will address the extremely urgent climate-induced problem of accelerated coastal erosion and vulnerability of the climate-sensitive fishery industry in Monrovia, as well as mitigating the ongoing degradation of mangrove ecosystems. The project is expected to contribute significantly towards reducing exposure to SLR and the increasing frequency of high-intensity storms, which enhance the erosion potential of waves (Figure 1). In addition, the project will contribute to reducing coastal flooding by up to 50% in the next decade as expected to increase shoreline retreat during the densely-populated coast by 110% over the next 30 years, with significant impacts on human settlements and infrastructure. The projected SLR is illustrated by the loss of ~10m of shoreline at West Point between January and July 2020, according to anecdotal sources. This is consistent with the projected SLR for the region as indicated in Figure 4, which shows the Meetsuand Wetland (Annex 2.A: Feasibility Study, Section 3.), exacerbating ongoing baseline degradation and

### 2.4. Theory of change (max. 1000 words, approximately 2 pages plus diagram)

#### Project theory of change

The project is expected to facilitate a reduction in greenhouse gas emissions, the emission-reduction co-benefits of which will be enhanced through the scaling-up of clean energy products and services that can increase the potential for emission reductions. Further detail on expected emission reductions is provided in Section B.3: paragraphs 101-124.

### 2.5. Climate change impacts

Climate change will contribute to an accelerated sea-level rise along the Monrovia coastline. Anticipated changes in the future climate will result in a significant increase in storm surge and wave heights, which will lead to increased coastal erosion and destructive storm surge from enhanced storm intensity (Figure 10). These climate forces will enhance shoreline retreat and transport sediment inland, leading to increased flooding of low-lying areas and infrastructure. The projected SLR is illustrated by the loss of ~10m of shoreline at West Point between January and July 2020, according to anecdotal sources. This is consistent with the projected SLR for the region as indicated in Figure 4, which shows the Meetsuand Wetland (Annex 2.A: Feasibility Study, Section 3.), exacerbating ongoing baseline degradation and expected impacts can be found in Annex 2.A: Feasibility Study, Section 2. as well as in Annex 2.B: Vulnerability Sub-assessment, Section 4.

## Preferred solution

26. The preferred solution to the above-mentioned climate hazards is to: (i) address the immediate needs of vulnerable communities in Mozambique by protecting highly exposed areas of the Mozambican coastline from accelerated coastal erosion and SLR, and preventing the forced relocation of vulnerable peoples through the establishment of coastal defense structures; (ii) establish a long-term adaptation strategy for coastal communities in Mozambique based on a climate-resilience approach by adopting ICMW in Mozambique and Liberia to improve coordination among government agencies and stakeholders involved in coastal management activities. This will be achieved by incorporating considerations into adaptation efforts: ii) secure the livelihoods of vulnerable communities who rely on climate-sensitive resources, such as fisheries and agriculture; iii) promote community-based disaster risk reduction through community-level innovation and learning for the promotion of climate-resilient livelihoods and development of long-term adaptive capacity.

27. Securing the livelihoods of the 30,000 people* within the MMA who rely directly on the climate-sensitive fishing sector is a particularly important aspect of the preferred solution because of the relationship between the fisheries and food security. These livelihoods are underpinned by the ecosystem services provided by mangroves in the Mozambique Wetland, which provide protection from coastal erosion, SLR, biodiversity extraction and pollution. The most cost-effective approach to adapting to the impacts of climate change on these ecosystems is through the establishment of a long-term adaptation strategy for coastal communities that will reduce pressure and increase the resilience of mangroves to the impacts of climate-change-induced SLR. Similarly, empowering coastal communities to develop alternative, climate-resilient livelihood activities can strengthen their adaptive capacity.

*Further information and the impact of climate change on mangroves in the Mozambique Wetland and an assessment of livelihoods within the wetlands are provided in Annex 1: The Mozambique Wetland Margrove Sub-assessment.

29\. The plan will catalyse a paradigm shift in Liberia’s approach to coastal spatial planning and the sustainable management of coastal ecosystems that addresses current and future climate change risks through long-term, climate-responsive planning. This shift will be realised through the use of quantitative, scientifically-defensible data in the development of a national risk-informed coastal zone management plan (CZM) for ecosystem-based coastal resilience. The plan will also build on existing work that has been done in the MRA and will use it as a platform to develop public-private partnerships for coastal management. In addition, the paradigm shift in coastal management will be supported by the development of an integrated approach to coastal zone management (CZM) with specific livelihoods in the MIMA specifically.

**Barriers**

30\. There are three main barriers to addressing the above-mentioned problem statement. These are summarised below and expanded on in Section 8 of the FS:

* Limited technical and financial capacity within the public and private sector for the establishment of coastal protection infrastructure and management.
* The effectiveness of long-term adaptation planning for coastal communities in Monrovia under future climate change is limited by the lack of institutional capacity to plan, design, implement, and monitor adaptation strategies. Public and private sector actors in Liberia — however, lack the technical and financial capacity for designing and conducting such activities. This is further compounded by the fact that these communities are already settled in areas that will be severely affected by projected climate change impacts.
* Lack of access to finance and support for development also limits the financial capacity of these enterprises for managing their resources and adapting to climate change.

**Limited knowledge and technical capacity within government institutions for the adoption and implementation of Integrated Coastal Zone Management (ICZM).**

31\. Liberia has limited institutional capacity in terms of human resource, equipment, and funding to promote sustainable and climate-resilient coastal development through ICZM. Responsibility for the management of different aspects of the coast is divided among various government agencies. The lack of a single agency responsible for the management of coastal resources currently occurs largely on an ad hoc and sectoral basis in Monrovia, which in turn detracts from the sustainability of management structures and interventions and limits the potential for systematically developing integrated approaches to coastal zone management (CZM). This is further exacerbated by the climate change vulnerability of different coastal areas and no existing plan to support an integrated approach to coastal zone management. The lack of a single agency responsible for the management of coastal resources also acts as a barrier or even exacerbates by insufficient climate information systems (CIS). Limited knowledge and technical capacity within government institutions is another major challenge in Liberia that hinders efforts in order to promote sustainability of coastal settlements, food security and climate-resilient planning.

**Limited awareness about the impacts of climate change, including climate-sensitive aspects of fisheries and livelihoods, among coastal communities.**

32\. A significant barrier to building the resilience of coastal communities in Monrovia is limited recognition and awareness by these communities regarding the impacts of climate change on their livelihoods (i.e., sustainability of current extractive practices), particularly in the fisheries sector, which are responsible for 80% of Liberia’s export earnings. This is further exacerbated by the degradation of mangrove ecosystem services in Monrovia, which contributes to degradation and reduced ecosystem services

## Proposed Interventions

This project will address the climate change impacts of SLR and increasingly frequent and intense storms through a multi-faceted approach, including: (i) the protection of coastal communities and infrastructure at West Point against erosion caused by sea-level rise and increasingly frequent high-intensity storms; (ii) institutional capacity building and policy support for decision-makers to effectively manage mangroves in the context of climate change adaptation; and (iii) strengthening mangroves and strengthening gender- and climate-sensitive livelihoods to build local climate resilience in Monrovia.

### Under Output 1, coastal protection infrastructure will be constructed to protect critical sections of the coast on a long-term basis. This will include: (a) hard coastal protection infrastructure at West Point; and (b) soft coastal protection interventions). This will address the technical and financial barriers to establish coastal protection infrastructure that is compatible with the local ecosystem and can withstand extreme weather events, including SLR.

In particular - will require the construction of the "hard intervention" strategy of rock revetments to stabilize the shoreline, as opposed to "softer", ecosystem-based adaptation (EBA) solutions. Such a rock revetment will be constructed along the southern end of the site and at the northern end of the site, in order to protect against erosion caused by SLR and high-intensity storms.

2. C: Engineering Sub-assignment). These include: (i) the exposed nature of the coastline; (ii) the steep gradient of the beach slope; (iii) the presence of a large number of buildings on the coast; and (iv) the presence of mangroves, which also represents a socially sensitive element that will be sustainable beyond the project lifespan because it requires low maintenance. The proposed solution is to construct a rock revetment along the southern end of the site with rock armour - which is able to withstand extreme weather conditions and storm events -- as proposed.

The construction of this revetment will also provide an opportunity for the creation of a new, more protected environment, and the conceptual design for this portion of the intervention site consequently proposes light rock armour around the southern end of the site. The revetment will be constructed to a height that is slightly above mean high water level to account for future deepening of the area directly in front of the revetment. A six-metre wide promenade, for access and pedestrian circulation along the coast, will also be constructed on top of the revetment at the southern end of West Point. Two boat launching and landing sites are proposed as part of the design at the southern end and centre of the revetment, respectively. These two locations have been selected to create a locally safe environment and port area within the site. The northern end of the site will also be protected by rock armour, with access points where fishing boats are already launching and landing. This intervention has been developed through an integrated ecosystem approach that takes into account the needs of all stakeholders.

3. Output 2 will facilitate the paradigm shift necessary for adopting an evidence-based ICMZ approach across Liberia. The technical and institutional capacity of the government and communities to adapt to rapidly changing coastal territories is a critical component in ensuring long-term climate resilience. This output will support the implementation of ICMZ as the promotion of reliable knowledge - based on well-established practices and procedures - and good governance, which are essential for effective adaptation planning and management. The project will engage a variety of stakeholders and stakeholder interests, including private sector stakeholders; (ii) long-term climate change adaptation and planning -- with ecosystem-based adaptation (EBA), as a fundamental concept of the ICMZ approach.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V2 | PAGE 22 OF 97

## Liberia: Monrovia Metropolitan Climate Resilience Project

### Figure 11. Theory of Change

The proposed project will enhance the resilience of vulnerable coastal communities within the Monrovia Metropolitan Area (MMA) against sea-level rise and increasingly frequent high-intensity storms.

This overarching objective of the project will be achieved through three integrated outputs, which will be implemented in a coordinated manner:

- **Output 1:** Strengthening institutional capacity to manage coastal areas of Liberia at a national level. This includes strengthening the National Disaster Management Agency (NDMA) and the Ministry of Environment, Forestry and Tourism (MOEFT) (Output 1).
- **Output 2:** Implementing a robust, sustainable coastal defense solution on West Point under Output 1 as well as the institutional and community capacity to manage this infrastructure. This will be implemented in close collaboration with the MOEFT which manages its coastline at the federal level.
- **Output 3:** Implementing a resilient, gender-responsive policy framework that will apply across the whole Liberian coastline.

### Output 1: Strengthening of coastal communities and infrastructure at West Point against erosion caused by sea-level rise and increasingly frequent high-intensity storms

Coastal communities within the MMA are at risk from accelerated coastal erosion and increased incidence of extreme weather events, which has already resulted in the displacement of at least 670 households. This is disempowering women and girls who are disproportionately affected.

The proposed project will use a portion of the grant funding to construct coastal defense infrastructure — in the form of a rock revetment — to protect the West Point community against erosion caused by sea-level rise and increasingly frequent high-intensity storms.

The development of a hard revetment at West Point is very unlikely to affect water levels in the estuary. This is because the revetment will be constructed on an existing beach, which has been eroding for many years and is not currently providing any protection against wave action. The construction of the revetment will not affect the existing beach at West Point and will not impact the flood risk level function which is required for further levels in the estuary. The construction of a hard revetment on an existing beach, which has been eroding for many years and is not currently providing any protection against wave action, is unlikely to cause significant changes to water levels or sediment transport.

The development of a green promenade at West Point will have no impact on the flood risk level function in the estuary. The construction of a green promenade on an existing beach (which has been eroding for many years and is not currently providing any protection against wave action) will not affect water levels or sediment transport.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V2.0 | PAGE 24 OF 97

## Sub-activity 1.1.2: Host a validation workshop to present and validate final design and construction plan for government and communities

Under this activity, an official validation workshop will be held to present the finalized design and construction plan relevant to government and community stakeholders and explain how stakeholder concerns and input have been incorporated into the design of the project. The workshop will also provide a platform for dialogue with civil society groups such as development organizations and NGOs who will be invited to attend the validation. All consultative and workshop sessions will be gender-responsive* and include 50% women.

### Activity 1.2: Construct coastal defense structure to protect West Point against climate change-induced coastal erosion

To protect the community of West Point from the impacts of accelerated coastal erosion and extreme weather events resulting from climate change, an engineering firm will be contracted to construct a rock revetment at West Point. The revetment will be constructed using locally available materials such as granite blocks and concrete. The design of the revetment is such that three areas of beach will be retained and protected to ensure that the ocean is not eroded further, thereby protecting the community from flooding and erosion of the coastline and loss of fish. Constraints on capacity for maintaining physical infrastructure were considered in the selection and design of the revetment. The revetment will have a lifespan of 50 years with an estimated maintenance interval* every five years with a minimum lifespan of 30-50 years. To further increase the sustainability of the revetment, women will be involved in its construction and maintenance. Women will be trained to use best maintenance techniques for the revetment. Women will constitute at least 40% of waged labour on project-related activities such as construction. MWC will be the Responsible Party for this activity, which will be financed by GCF, UNDP, and GoL.

### Sub-activities under this activity include:

#### Sub-activity 1.2.1: Construct a rock revetment with a green promenade and community amenities at West Point

A firm will be procured, and construction will be undertaken according to the detailed final design and construction plan developed under Sub-activity 1.1.1. The firm implementing the protective infrastructure at West Point will engage in public consultations with women and other stakeholders on the project. A green promenade along the beach is accessible by cars because of the dense housing layout of West Point, tollbooths will be provided for the access road to the revetment. The cost of construction will be minimal and will be absorbed under construction costs. The type and location of access control infrastructure will

## B

### Sub-activity 2.1.1: Develop a high-resolution, multi-criteria vulnerability map of the Liberian coastline

#### 48. This process will result in a dynamic ICZMP that i) continuously adapts to climate and sea-level rise for the sustainable management of coastal resources; ii) provides an integrated approach for integrating climate change risks into planning and coastal resource use; iii) support integrated development planning, including through the integration of climate change adaptation strategies within sectoral plans and civil society groups through the ICZM Committee and CSW; iv) facilitate the incorporation of climate change adaptation measures in national policies and strategies related to coastal resources management on the coast zone in Liberia. To address the local gap in expertise required to develop and implement this ICZMP, the project will provide training for national counterparts and representatives on best practice for ICZM through a Transfer of Technology (ToT) activity. Activity 2.2: The BME will be financed by GCF, UNDP, and QD.

#### Sub-activities under this activity include:

##### Sub-activity 2.1.1. Develop a high-resolution, multi-criteria vulnerability map of the Liberian coastline

##### 50. To build the knowledge base for climate risk profiling in Liberia and to support the generation and implementation of an integrated coastal zone management plan (ICZMP) that will address climate change impacts on coastal areas, it is necessary to develop a high-resolution, multi-criteria vulnerability map that assesses exposure, sensitivity, and hazard parameters within coastal areas. Data collected through this activity will be used as input for the development of the map, which will have the high-resolution spatial datasets produced under Sub-activity 2.3.1. The resulting map will provide a basis for decision-making on how to address climate change factors to ensure long-term decision-making beyond the lifespan of the project. ## B

### Sub-activity 2.1.2: Develop the climate-responsive ICZMP in collaboration with stakeholders, incorporating information from the vulnerability assessment (sub-activity 2.1.1)

51 Under this activity, the project will develop the ICZMP through a participatory and consultative process. An appropriately qualified service provider will be contracted to engage with relevant stakeholders through a gender-sensitive approach. The service provider will work closely with the Ministry of Gender and Social Protection (MGSP) in the coastal zone**, the 10 relevant government institutions involved with coastal management in Liberia as well as the Ministry of Land, Forestry and Environment (MLFE). The vulnerability assessment conducted under sub-activity 2.1.1 will be considered in relation to the vulnerability map produced under Sub-activity 2.1.1 and will inform the design of the ICZMP. The service provider will develop a draft ICZMP for review by the project team, the MLFE and the ICZMP three years after the initial development. The ICZMP will be developed at a national scale but will only be implemented in Liberia’s coastal zone. The participatory process described above is expected to take 12 months. This project supports implementation on a limited scale — which will provide an opportunity for newly capacitated institutional staff to develop and implement the ICZMP. The service provider developing the ICZMP will also identify opportunities for the potential for the ICZMP across Liberia. Additionally, under this activity, the service provider developing the ICZMP will: i) define the mandate of the ICZM Committee and the CSWG based on consultations with all government stakeholders; ii) develop a work plan for the ICZM Committee and the CSWG; iii) provide guidance to the MLFE on how to mandatorily reinforce for the remitment — constructed under Activity 1.2 — to a relevant national institution, such as the Ministry of Land, Forestry and Environment (MLFE); iv) define the structure of the ICZMP.

### Sub-activity 2.1.3: Host validation workshops to present and secure ownership of the ICZMP by representatives from the 10 government institutions responsible for coastal management in Liberia

52 Under this activity, the two workshops will be held to present the newly developed ICZMP in Year 2 and the updated ICZMP in Year 4. The workshops will be co-hosted with relevant national institutions responsible for coastal management in Liberia as well as the Ministry of Gender and Social Protection. The objective of the workshops will be to launch the ICZMP, provide an opportunity for feedback from all participants on the draft ICZMP, and ensure buy-in from each institution. During the workshops, any outstanding points regarding the plan will be addressed and the membership of the ICZM Committee and CSWG will be confirmed. The ICZM Committee is expected to comprise of high-level government representatives, comprising 50% women, from different government departments. The CSWG will be mandated to strengthen cross-sectoral collaboration for ICZM and support the mainstreaming of ICZM into relevant government sectors.

### Activity 2.2: Capacitate the Cross-Sectoral Working Group to mainstream ICZM into relevant government sectors through a Training-of-Trainners approach

53 A further action undertaken during this initial validation workshop will be the nomination of government staff from each department to serve on a Cross-Sectoral Working Group (CSWG). The CSWG will include technical specialists and representatives from all 10 government institutions responsible for coastal management in Liberia. The CSWG will be established under Activity 2.2 to mainstream ICZM into their respective institutions. The members of the CSWG will also be mandated to support the implementation of the ICZMP. The structure of the CSWG, including its membership and the structure it will contribute to information flow between decision-makers and technical personnel within as well as between government departments.

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL 2 (2) | PAGE 31 OF 97

## Sub-activity 2.1.3: Procure high-resolution remote sensing data to monitor coastal erosion and mangrove ecosystem health and degradation in the MWA, to be made publicly accessible and used for the vulnerability map under Sub-activity 2.1.1.

### 6.4 Using this activity, high-resolution satellite imagery**[1]** will be procured for the MWA. First, five historical datasets (one for every second year over the past ten years) — will be procured along with a new dataset in Year 2 of the project. Additional datasets will be acquired in Years 4 and 6 of the project. The historical datasets will be used to inform the vulnerability map under Sub-activity 2.1.1, while the new data will be used for monitoring purposes. The imagery will be processed and analysed under Sub-activity 3.3.1 to assess the health and degradation of mangrove ecosystems in the MWA. The historical datasets will be made publicly accessible through the Environmental Knowledge Management System (EKMS) in Liberia.

### Sub-activity 2.3.2: Contract a specialist firm to provide an integrated, near-shore wave buoy-based data collection and processing system to support the collection of meteorological and oceanographic data for improved generation of coastal hazard maps and vulnerability assessments under Sub-activity 2.1.1.

The above-mentioned integrated solution will include: (i) four wave buoys that are capable of capturing, recording and transmitting data relating to air-wind direction, windspeed, wave height, sea-level pressure, sea surface temperature, water level, and current speed; (ii) a server-based system for the collection, processing, and transmission of data from the wave buoys; (iii) a database for collecting, processing and cataloguing data; (iv) training courses on the installation, use and maintenance of the system; and (v) on-site and off-site technical support in integrating the system with the existing weather monitoring infrastructure. The integrated solution will be based on the wave buoy system developed under the ABS/GCF project currently under development. The training courses on the installation, use and maintenance of the system will be provided by a specialist firm that has experience in installing and maintaining similar systems (including staff of the EPA, NDMA, LMS and GCDMU) benefit from technical assistance for the duration of the project. This will ensure that the system is fully operational and integrated into the system beyond the project lifespan.

### Activity 2.4: Strengthen the existing Environmental Knowledge Management System (EKMS) in Liberia as a platform for awareness-raising and sharing of climate-risk informed ICCM approaches.

The project will support the newly implemented Environmental Knowledge Management System (EKMS) in Liberia by extending accessibility to the system, improving its functionality, and strengthening its capacity. The EKMS is hosted by the EPA to act as a data link among the 10 institutions responsible for addressing climate change impacts within the coastal zone and to provide information on climate change adaptation activities. The project will support the EKMS through a partnership between the EPA and research institutions, including the University of Liberia, to develop case studies and further strengthen the system’s functionality. The project will also ensure that all relevant stakeholders are linked to these institutions and provide a platform to access and share climate information to support climate change adaptation activities in the coastal zone. This will be achieved by: (i) providing training material for GCDMU developed under Sub-activity 2.2.3; (ii) the GCDMU developed under Sub-activity 2.1.2; v) the spatial data and information on coastal erosion and mangrove ecosystem health and degradation in the MWA under Sub-activity 2.1.1; and vi) lessons learned for upscaling and replicating project activities, which will be reported to the GEF CCMF. The project will also ensure that the EKMS is updated regularly by providing training on how to update the system and to collect reports on the implementation of the ICCMP at an annual basis, which will be uploaded into the EKMS. The lessons learned from the pilot activities under Sub-activity 2.1.1 will serve as a model activity for knowledge sharing will be used for upscaling through collaboration efforts with the GEF CCMF systems to provide a platform for awareness-raising and sharing of climate-risk informed ICCM approaches in coastal zone management. The cross-project collaboration will contribute to effective coastal protection and adequate climate change adaptation.

change and disaster planning that will limit the impact of climate change hazards on vulnerable communities and reduce their vulnerability.

### B

67. To further support uptake of the system, the project will support the distribution of knowledge products to government institutions in Liberia to increase awareness of the EKMS and its benefits. The EKMS will promote awareness of the system's confluence with other systems. Improved access to the EKMS will support increased access to environmental and climate change information for all government institutions responsible for coastal management, including those in the private sector.

68. Sub-activities under this activity will include:

#### Sub-activity 2.4.1 Procure ICT equipment for each of the relevant government institutions to access and use climate and environmental information through the platform.

Under this activity, the project will procure computer equipment, remote servers and on-site data storage to facilitate access to the EKMS by all relevant government institutions responsible for management of the coastal zone and associated climate change impacts in Liberia. The ICT equipment procured under this activity will also be linked to the national service provider (NSP) to ensure that all relevant government institutions have access to the EKMS. This will allow all relevant government institutions to access a shared resource on ICOM and facilitate access to data on coastal management, climate change adaptation and disaster risk reduction in Liberia. The project will improve early warnings for extreme storm surges or other storm-related disasters that will be improved.

#### Sub-activity 2.4.2 Collect lessons learned on ICOM on an annual basis and incorporate this information into the EKMS.

A national service provider will be contracted under this activity to collect lessons learned, prepare a report and update the EKMS on an annual basis. The report will provide suggestions on best practice for effectively addressing climate change adaptation in Liberia through the use of ICOM and the EKMS. This information will be informed by project monitoring initiatives under Activity 3.3. Collecting lessons learned on an annual basis with the support of the national service provider will ensure that all relevant government institutions benefit from lessons learned within each separate institution, as well as ensure that all relevant government institutions benefit from lessons learned within each separate institution, so as to facilitate capacity development case study visits and increase understanding about coastal protection and management in Liberia.

#### Sub-activity 2.4.3 Design and disseminate knowledge products on climate-responsive ICOM in government institutions and to the private sector.

Under this activity, a national service provider will be contracted to develop an awareness-raising strategy to communicate the EKMS and facilitate greater uptake of the EKMS within government institutions and among private sector partners. Knowledge products developed under this strategy will include posters and brochures that will be disseminated in government institutions and among private sector partners, as well as information on the system. These knowledge products will be tailored to identify user-groups, which will include staff members of government institutions and private sector partners. The awareness-raising strategy and the knowledge products will be designed a gender-sensitive manner and will ensure that they are equally accessible for all groups in society. The project will also develop an awareness-raising campaign using ICOM and the EKMS for different economic activities and social groups practicing different livelihoods strategies.

#### Activity 2.5 Conduct an awareness-raising campaign for communities in focus areas on climate change impacts and adaptation measures.

A specialist media or communication firm will be contracted to design a contextually appropriate campaign using a participatory process. The awareness-raising campaign will include developing visual communication materials and

## Knowledge-sharing as well as facilitating local radio programmes and community meetings, which will serve as the primary platforms to engage communities. The communication materials — for example, billboards, posters, brochures, leaflets, etc., will be developed in collaboration with the Community Steering Committee (CSC) of Jacob's Town, Fiamah and Topoe Village — and will be erected in public spaces. These four communities have been selected as they are located within the coastal zone of the West Point (West Port), and degraded areas of the Mesuwa Wetland (Fiamah, Jacob’s Town and Topoe Village). The campaign will be designed in a gender-sensitive manner with a specific emphasis on differential impacts of climate change on women and men. It will also highlight adaptation practices that are being used or have potential adaptation practices. The development and dissemination of knowledge products under this campaign will be the responsibility of the Knowledge Sharing Group under Activity 2.0, which will serve as a repository for the knowledge products developed by the contracted firm.

### Sub-activities under this activity include:

#### Sub-activity 2.5.1: Design an awareness-raising campaign on climate change impacts and adaptation practices.
Under this activity, a media firm with experience in West Africa and expertise in communications, climate change and gender will be contracted to design an awareness-raising campaign on climate change impacts and adaptation practices that are being used or have potential adaptation practices. The firm will work closely with the CSC of each institution and will update the campaign on an annual basis based on lessons learned — collected by the knowledge sharing groups under Sub-activity 2.3.1.2. The awareness-raising campaign materials produced will prioritize the impact of climate change on mangroves and on the coastal, gender-specific climate change vulnerabilities and a range of adaptation practices, including activities that reduce pressure on mangrove ecosystems.

#### Sub-activity 2.5.2: Host radio programmes on climate change impacts, vulnerability and adaptation practices in coastal wetland areas.
Under this activity, radio shows will be hosted every three months on climate change impacts, vulnerability and adaptation practices in coastal wetlands. The radio shows will be broadcasted from the CSC offices of each of the four areas — at least half of whom will be women. The members of the knowledge-sharing groups will be nominated by their respective institutions to participate as guest speakers for these radio shows. The terms and responsibilities of the knowledge sharing groups will be set out in the constitution of the CSC and all members of the knowledge sharing groups will sign agreements with the CSC.

### Sub-activity 2.5.3: Establish, train and engage community-based knowledge-sharing groups to support on-the-ground awareness-raising and knowledge-sharing.
Under this activity, knowledge-sharing groups will be established in each of the four communities selected for awareness-raising activities under Sub-activity 2.5.1. The knowledge sharing group will consist of at least one member from each of the areas — at least half of whom will be women. The members of the knowledge-sharing groups will be nominated by their respective institutions to participate as guest speakers for these radio shows. The terms and responsibilities of the knowledge sharing groups will be set out in the constitution of the CSC and all members of the knowledge sharing groups will sign agreements with the CSC.

Sub-activity 2.5.4 Install and distribute awareness-raising knowledge products within the four communities prioritised for awareness-raising interventions.

78. The visual media and other knowledge products designed under Sub-activity 2.5.1 will be produced, installed and disseminated in the communities. Large visual products, such as billboards and posters will be installed in open public spaces and around community meeting venues. The brochures, stickers, caps and t-shirts will be distributed to women and men in the communities. Sub-activity 2.5.5 Organise community meetings to raise awareness around climate change adaptation and be the first to benefit from early warning systems.

79. Under this activity, gender and socially sensitive community meetings will be held to raise awareness around climate impacts, adaptation practices and the benefits of the interventions being implemented under the project. A total of three community meetings will be held in each of the four areas identified for awareness-raising activities. The meetings will be held at least once a month by any member of the communities and will be exclusively accessible to women to provide a forum for raising awareness on gender-specific climate change impacts, adaptation practices and benefits of early warning systems information on women’s rights, community strategies and strategies for reducing gender-based violence.

80. These community meetings will be held at venues in the communities so that they are accessible to community members. The meetings will also serve as events to distribute the small knowledge products designed under Sub-activity 2.5.1. The meetings will also serve as events to discuss actions – including setting up billboards in public spaces and disseminating information posters to schools.

Output 3. Protect mangroves and strengthening gender- and climate-sensitive livelihoods to build local climate resilience (Monitoring).

80a Under Sub-activity 3.2, the project will put measures in place to reduce the anthropogenic pressure on mangrove ecosystems in the Meswadu Wetland. By reducing this pressure, the health of the ecosystems will be improved and their resilience to sea-level rise (SLR) and the impacts of climate change will increase, so increasing the resilience of local communities. The project will also put measures in place to reduce the risk of SLR by ensuring the degradation of mangroves will increase the likelihood of their long-term survival and adaptation to climate


---
**Page 37**
---

change and mangrove migration. These mangroves are likely to be negatively impacted by climate change-induced SLR which will decrease the area of the Mesurado Wetland in which they can grow. In addition, urban encroachment into the wetlands is also a major threat to these mangroves. The loss of mangroves and associated species has resulted in degradation of the mangrove ecosystems in Monrovia[20]. In order to develop long-term climate resilience within communities dependent on mangroves there are a need to: i) support community conservation of mangroves in the MMA to secure the provision of critical ecosystem services, including expected climate change impacts; and ii) develop local climate-resilient livelihood activities and strengthen market access for these. The project will focus on four integrated approaches to achieve this: i) increasing capacity to adaptation will benefit from improved coastal protection measures being implemented under Output 1 and will integrate the lessons learned into any new planning being developed under Output 2; ii) support community uptake at both institutional and community levels.

Activities under this output will target four communities adjacent to a degraded area of the Mesurado Wetland West Point, Toppe Village, Jacob’s Town, Polorak and Flaman. Prior to the project, these communities were highly vulnerable as a result of their proximity to and reliance on the coast and mangroves for their livelihoods (Figure 13). These communities are also at risk from climate change impacts such as sea level rise, increased storm surges and flooding as they surround degradation hotspots in the Mesurado Wetlands. These adaptation will focus on four integrated approaches: i) increasing capacity to adapt by improving coastal protection measures; ii) supporting community resilience through improved livelihoods and supporting the uptake of climate-resilient livelihoods. To capitalize on the experience of NGOs based in Monrovia, the project will seek to engage NGOs — with experience in the four aforementioned themes.

Figure 13: Landscape map of Monrovia showing the Mesurado Wetland and focus areas (in bold text) for the proposed project West Point, Toppe Village, Jacob’s Town, Polorak and Flaman.


83. To ensure the safeguarding of mangrove ecosystem services a community-owned co-management agreement will be established with the support of the Community-Based Natural Resource Management (CBNRM), developed through extensive stakeholder engagement. An awareness-raising campaign will be conducted to inform the focus communities on the importance of mangroves and their role in providing resilience against climate change impacts and the importance of mangrove ecosystems. The impact of these awareness-raising activities will

be assessed through annual state-of-knowledge assessments. Finally, to reduce pressure on the mangroves ecosystems from fishway harvesting, facilities for the manufacturing of energy-efficient cookstoves will be established in West Point and other locations where there is a high demand for these stoves. These stoves will replace near major fish processing sites to reduce the need for fish smoking. The cold storage facilities will be managed by women in the community and will significantly reduce food wastage, which contributes to making fisheries more resilient against climate change. This will also provide an additional income source for women in the community to increase their income and improve their adaptive capacity. The incorporation of solar self-sustaining cold storage facilities into the cookstoves will further enhance the sustainability of this project by reducing reliance on diesel generators for electricity required prior to the loading of fish prior to its transport to the market.

### Activity 3.f: Establish a community education and innovation centre to function as a community knowledge generation and dissemination hub, with an emphasis on climate change adaptation

84. Under this activity, an education and innovation centre will be established in West Point, through co-financing to host and coordinate activities under Output 3. Because of its central location, the centre will be accessible to all communities within the project area and will serve as a hub for knowledge sharing and dissemination on livelihood development and innovation related to climate change*. An existing building on community- or government-owned land in West Point will be converted into an education and innovation centre that is wheelchair accessible and suitable for people with disabilities. Multi-purpose facilities, including cooking areas, open training areas and appropriate sanitation facilities will be established within the building. The centre will host a range of activities such as training workshops, which receives buy-in from the actively engaged local communities. Additionally, the centre will host training on the community-level maintenance of the renewable build under Output 1 (see Annex 21: O&M Plan). All activities and systems in the centre will be designed to be climate-resilient.

85. Management of the centre will be undertaken by the community knowledge-sharing groups established under Sub-activity 2.5.3 with oversight provided by the project management team and the Community Stewardship Committee (CSC) as described above. The CSC will also provide technical support for the establishment, operation and maintenance of the centre. The CSC will manage the procurement of all materials required to establish the centre on behalf of the community or government entity that owns the centre and the CSC in consultation with the MFP and MoGSCP. The financial management of the centre will be undertaken by the CSC as a separate entity from the project, established as a community-based organization (CBO). Two bank accounts will be opened by the CSC, in association with the project management team, to manage the funds for the establishment and operation of the centre. One account will be used to purchase materials for the cookstoves; and it is managed by the nominal fees from the usage of the cold storage facilities. The other account will be used to pay for all other activities under this sub-activity. These activities will be managed by the CSC and reported on quarterly to the PMU for the duration of the project. All income generated through the use of the cold storage facility will be deposited into a separate bank account.

86. Indicative estimates of the income managed by the CSC are provided in the descriptions of Activity 3.4 and 3.5 below. After project completion, the funds will also be used to continue to pay a monthly stipend to the CSC members for their continued support on the project. The CSC will also provide technical assistance to the community base for the CIC and for consultants conducting assessments under Activities 3.2, 3.3 and 3.5. The establishment of the centre is expected to have multiple benefits including: improving access to education and training opportunities for livelihoods, increasing community awareness on climate change and shifting the community's perception of the value of their natural resources. The centre will be implemented by the EPA and financed by GCF, UNDP and GIZ.

## Sub-activity 3.1.2 Establish and commission the ten person Community Stewardship Committee with representatives from each of the four areas.

88. Under this activity, the Community Stewardship Committee (CSC) will be established and registered as a community-based organization to provide oversight for the activities described in paragraphs 89 through 91 and 1. The members of the CSC will be selected every 3 years based on community nominations and each member will serve for three consecutive terms, with staggered elections so that no more than one-third of the members are replaced at any time. They will include at least 50% women and be representative of the four focus areas (West Point, Topaz Village, Jacob's Town, and Flemish and Plomker) and include the women managing the cold storage facilities in each area. The CSC will be responsible for: i) ensuring that all activities are carried out under Output 3 and community-based activities under Output 1 (investment management). The education and innovation centre will provide training to the CSC members on how to carry out their responsibilities, and the project groups to provide input and guidance. The PMU will facilitate the development of a formal constitution for the CSC which will outline its structure, roles and responsibilities, and the knowledge-sharing groups established under Activity 2.5. In addition, the CSC will be responsible for: ii) ensuring that all activities are carried out in accordance with the rules and responsibilities of the CSC and the knowledge-sharing groups established under Activity 2.5. As a further measure to ensure that the project is implemented as per the agreed terms and conditions, the CSC members will be required to sign a Memorandum of Understanding (MoU) which will be reached with the community or government entity that owns each cold storage facility. The MoU will outline any required maintenance for the duration of the project.

Sub-activity 3.1.2.5 Help in inauguration event to open the centre to the public and present the members of the Community Stewardship Committee.

89. To promote the education and innovation centre and secure community buy-in, an inauguration event will be held at this centre. This event will incorporate awareness-raising activities and during the event community members nominated by the CSC will provide a presentation on the education and innovation centre – all presentations will be presented by local officials and community authorities.

Subactivity 3.1.3 Host a collaboration workshop for government representatives, CSC members and NGOs to meet and exchange ideas and lessons learned from past experiences in education and innovation centres.

90. Under this activity and subsequent to the launch of the education and innovation centre, a two-day collaboration workshop will be organised for all members of the CSC, as well as representatives from relevant government institutions and NGOs. The purpose of the workshop is to provide an opportunity for all stakeholders involved with the project to: i) finalise and validate the constitution of the CSC; ii) define a set of guiding principles that will govern the activities under Output 3 and community-based activities under Output 1, and vi) share innovative ideas and lessons learned from past experiences. Additionally, during the workshop, guest speakers on each of the three thematic areas prioritised under Output 3 – CBRIM, local town investment development and climate change adaptation – will be invited to provide presentations.

The buildings will not be purchased under the project - instead existing community-owned structures (structures owned by community groups) will be refurbished and adapted for use as cold storage facilities. The PMU will facilitate the process of identifying suitable locations for these cold storage facilities, which will be located in each of the four focus areas. These contractual agreements between the CSC members and the EPA will apply both during and after the project period.

## Activity 3.2: Establish community-led co-management agreement to ease anthropogenic pressure on mangroves in the MMA.

### Sub-activity 3.2.1: Develop the community-led co-management agreement through a participatory, gender-sensitive approach.
Under this activity, two national consultants with experience in facilitation and natural resource management will be contracted to draft the co-management agreement. The consultants will undertake extensive consultations with communities, local government officials, and other relevant stakeholders to identify the various pressures currently exerted on mangroves, as well as the various uses of mangroves and mangrove products. These consultations will also help in identifying the key issues that need to be addressed through the co-management agreement in such a manner as to ensure that the concerns of all relevant stakeholders are taken into account and incorporated into the agreement and provide an opportunity for stakeholders to build relationships with each other, thus ensuring their commitment towards the implementation of the agreement. The agreement will be developed based on the institutional and regulatory framework for ICMZ (Output 2.1) and the initiatives targeting alternative livelihood practices under the GEF-funded World Bank project entitled “Conservation of Mangroves and Coastal Protected Areas” and the ICMP. The development of this co-management agreement will draw on lessons learned from similar initiatives elsewhere in the country, particularly those related to mangrove conservation and sustainable use.

### Sub-activity 3.2.2: Hold a three-day co-design and validation workshop for the community-led co-management agreement.
This sub-activity is critical to the success of this activity and will be facilitated by an analysis of lessons learned from similar initiatives elsewhere in the country, particularly those related to mangrove conservation and sustainable use. The national consultants will identify and confirm the key issues that need to be addressed through the co-management agreement with communities, local government officials, and other relevant stakeholders. This sub-activity is linked to Activity 3.3) and delivery of the agreed benefits will also form part of the co-management agreement.

### Sub-activity 3.2.3 Design and implement an awareness-raising campaign on sustainable Community-Based Natural Resource Management (CBNRM) in Monrovia, Liberia

#### 94. Two national consultants or service provider with experience in community awareness-raising and visual media design will be contracted under this activity to develop an awareness-raising campaign to disseminate information about the project’s objectives, activities, and expected outcomes on sustainable CBNRM practices for the conservation of mangroves. The campaign will also include information on detrimental environmental practices, such as sand mining and illegal logging, and how these can be avoided through the adoption of sustainable CBNRM practices, such as the use of energy-efficient cookstoves introduced under Activity 3.4 over traditional wood cooking stoves. The awareness-raising campaign will be designed to reach all stakeholders involved in mangrove conservation activities within Monrovia communities. It is expected that the awareness-raising campaign will also contribute towards achieving the outputs of Sub-activity 3.2.1 to ensure that the knowledge products developed (i) align with the co-management agreement and are gender-sensitive in their design, (ii) are informed by local ecological knowledge, (iii) depict the uses of mangroves by different groups within Monrovia communities, and (iv) are disseminated through community meetings and other events within communities. The knowledge products produced under this activity will include posters for each of the four areas, brochures on sustainable CBNRM practices, and a video that can be used to raise awareness about mangrove conservation within communities and at commonly-used entry points to access the mangroves, particularly in degradation hotspots. These materials will also be disseminated through community meetings and other events organized under this activity.

#### 95. Under this activity, a two-day workshop will be hosted for the CIC, relevant government institutions, NGOs and civil society in Year 3 of the project implementation phase. At the workshop, participants will receive training on information gathered from Sub-activity 3.2.1 and the experience of the relevant stakeholders. Drawing on these lessons learned, the co-management agreement will be revised and validated by the stakeholders at the workshop. Outputs of this session will also allow the revision of the CoM in Year 5 under Activity 2.1.

#### 96. Sub-activity 3.3 Conduct an assessment to evaluate the project-induced changes in mangrove degradation, community livelihoods, and awareness of climate change impacts, adaptation options and mangrove ecosystems throughout the project implementation period.

#### To assess the adaptive capacity of the four communities prioritized for awareness-raising and livelihood development activities under Sub-activity 3.2.3, a baseline survey will be conducted in Year 1 of the project phase. These assessments — building on preliminary work undertaken during the PPR phase of the project — will consider community livelihoods, mangrove degradation, and climate change impacts as well as adaptive capacity within ecosystems in the MMA as well as the different types of climate-resilient livelihoods currently being practiced within the MMA. These surveys will be conducted periodically to determine the annual change-in-state, which will help to refine project activities and outputs. The baseline survey will also include a review of existing knowledge on mangrove ecosystems in Monrovia. A series of knowledge assessments will be complemented by periodic assessments of mangrove health and degradation in the study area. The results from these surveys and assessments as well as the findings from the analysis of the reports produced from this analysis will assess the impact of the project on mangrove monrovia.
