# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL 2.0 | PAGE 48 OF 97

## Sub-activity 3.1.1: High-resolution remote sensing data procured under Sub-activity 2.3.1, conduct analyses of mangrove health and degradation in the Mecurucu Wetland to assess the impact of project interventions.

Under this activity, a report on trends in mangrove health and degradation in the Mecurucu Wetland will be produced based on high-resolution remote sensing data procured under Sub-activity 2.3.1. The first report will provide an analysis of trends in mangrove health and degradation over time (since 2005) for the entire wetland area. This report will provide a baseline for measuring project impact on mangrove protection. The baseline report will be updated based on new datasets acquired during Years 4 and 6 of the project period to assess the impact of the project on mangrove health and degradation.

## Sub-activity 3.1.2: Conduct a baseline state-of-knowledge survey on climate change impacts, adaptation options, CINMIM and mangrove ecosystems and undertake annual surveys to determine the change-in-state over the duration of the project.

Under this activity, a service provider will be contracted to conduct community-level assessments in each of the four areas prioritised under the project to develop indicators that determine baseline community perceptions and awareness levels regarding climate change impacts on CINMIM and mangrove ecosystems. Surveys carried out in the first year will be collated into a report that will act as a baseline on the state of community knowledge and awareness at the start of the project period, which can then be used to monitor changes over time during the project period to determine the effectiveness of the awareness-raising programme. Reports produced from the annual surveys will act as lessons learned and will be used by consultants engaged under Sub-activity 2.5.1 to update the awareness raising strategy (Activity 3.2) in line with the revised climate change adaptation action plan under Sub-activity 3.2.4, and by tertiary institutions to further research on coastal protection and mangrove ecosystems. The baseline state-of-knowledge survey will be conducted using a methodology through the EBMAP (Activity 2.4) to support the replication and upscaling of successful awareness-raising strategies.

## Sub-activity 3.3.3: Undertake pre-assessments of climate-resilient livelihood practices and new opportunities to improve livelihoods in communities with alternative livelihoods.

An important function of the education and innovation centre established under Sub-activity 3.1.1 will be to provide community members — particularly women — with alternative climate-resilient livelihood activities to increase their income-generating capacity, while also providing them with the skills necessary for these activities. Through a national service provider with expertise in the development of alternative, climate-resilient livelihoods will be contracted to conduct pre-assessments of existing and new livelihood opportunities within communities. Building on the preliminary information generated as part of the project PPF phase*, the service provider will identify and assess potential livelihood opportunities that can be developed by women to improve their resilience against climate change while also improving their income-generating capacity.

**benefits of reducing greenhouse gas (GHG) emission reduction**, the mitigation co-benefits of this project activity are very small. Training under Sub-activity 3.4.1 and workshops under Sub-activity 3.4.3 will have beneficial effects on the communities beyond the lifetime and geographical scope of the project. Additionally, a larger scale fish farming kiosk system is being developed in collaboration with the Ministry of Agriculture and Forestry (MAF) as part of the project. Sub-activities under this activity will include:

* **Sub-activity 3.4.1:** Develop a training programme and publicly available training materials and conduct trainings to capacitate community members to manufacture energy-efficient cookstoves.
    * In order to provide communities with immediate benefits—as well as to contribute to increasing energy efficiency in the communities—this sub-activity will be implemented immediately after the project start-up. The service provider will develop a training course and provide training on the construction of energy-efficient stoves through a training-of-trainers approach. The service provider will prepare a training manual for constructing these stoves, which will be accessible to all interested parties in the communities. In addition to this, the service provider will also organize trainings at the centre to ensure that interested community members have the requisite information and skills to construct energy-efficient stoves. This sub-activity will contribute directly to Sub-activity 3.2. The training at the education and innovation centre to capacitate community members to understand a variety of diversified livelihoods, including the construction of energy-efficient cookstoves, is an important component of Sub-activity 3.3.2. This training, and specifically the training-of-trainers approach, will contribute to the sustainability of the activities beyond the project lifespan and facilitate the replication of the activities in other communities in Maniwa.
* **Sub-activity 3.4.2:** Establish a small-scale manufacturing workshop and procure equipment and material for the education and innovation centre.
    * Under this activity, basic facilities—including open-air roofed structures—will be constructed and installed in close proximity to the education and innovation centre. These structures will be used during the training conducted under Sub-activity 3.4.1. Additionally, under this activity material inputs will be provided by the project to facilitate the establishment of a small-scale manufacturing workshop at the education and innovation centre for producing materials for manufacturing the first batch of stoves, with additional material inputs for the construction of further cookstoves to be sent from the CSG. ## Sub-activity 3.4.3: Host an annual workshop at the education and innovation centre to capacitate women's groups on business development and connect them with value chain actors to update the development of energy efficient value added products.

105. To support the uptake and upscaling of the livelihoods being supported by the project, annual workshops are planned for the women’s groups in the education and innovation centre (Sub-activity 3.4.2) on business development and networking events for women's groups, fishermen, value-chain actors and the domestic private sector, informal fishermen and other local private sector actors. These workshops will provide practical support for the development of small cohesive marketing businesses based around the project’s main products, including: i) providing training in market strategies and tools to the awareness-rising campaign under Activity 3.2; ii) and iii) facilitating networking events between women's groups, fishermen, value-chain actors and other local private sector actors.

106. The fishing industry in Micronesia accounts for a significant percentage of the food consumed by the population of the MIA, however limited access to cold storage means that most fish needs to be smoked to increase shelf life. This is particularly true for the small-scale artisanal fishermen who are predominantly women and who smoke, a significant proportion of the fish caught by communities does not make it to market (see Annex 2.0). To reduce waste and the reliance on faulness sourced from the mangroves as well as to improve food security for the MIA, the project proposes to install cold storage units in the villages where the artisanal fishermen are based at West Point. The proposed cold storage facilities under this activity are intended to be accessible to all fishers in the area and will be managed by a local community group. The units will be designed to require limited maintenance and any required maintenance will be facilitated through training provided as part of the capacity building interventions. The units will also provide access for other potential innovations and alternative livelihood opportunities, including for example, the upscaling of commercial fishing vessels like those demonstrated at the education and innovation centre (Sub-activity 3.4.2).

Activity 3.5: Purchase and install low-maintenance eco-friendly cold storage facilities near fish processing sites to reduce waste and improve food security.

107. The fishing industry in Micronesia accounts for a significant percentage of the food consumed by the population of the MIA, however limited access to cold storage means that most fish needs to be smoked to increase shelf life. This is particularly true for the small-scale artisanal fishermen who are predominantly women and who smoke, a significant proportion of the fish caught by communities does not make it to market (see Annex 2.0). To reduce waste and the reliance on faulness sourced from the mangroves as well as to improve food security for the MIA, the project proposes to install cold storage units in the villages where the artisanal fishermen are based at West Point. The proposed cold storage facilities under this activity are intended to be accessible to all fishers in the area and will be managed by a local community group. The units will be designed to require limited maintenance and any required maintenance will be facilitated through training provided as part of the capacity building interventions. The units will also provide access for other potential innovations and alternative livelihood opportunities, including for example, the upscaling of commercial fishing vessels like those demonstrated at the education and innovation centre (Sub-activity 3.4.2). This will ensure the sustainability of the units and the ability to meet their essential maintenance requirements. This activity will be implemented by the EPA and financed by UNDP.

Sub-activities under this activity include:


---
**Page 46**
---

Sub-activity 3.5.1: Develop site-specific eco-friendly, solar-powered cold storage units for the West Point fish processing site based on the needs of fishers and fishermen

107. Under this activity, a service provider will be contracted to develop site-specific designs for the cold storage units based on the needs of the fishers. The service provider will provide community engagement activities as part of the design process. The service provider will also be responsible for developing a management system, in consultation with the CSC, that aligns with the preferred method for community management and maintenance of the cold storage units. This information will be used to develop the tender documents. A stakeholder selection committee (CSC) will be selected for the CSC and will be responsible for overseeing the management of the cold storage units*. This information will be used to develop the tender documents. The service provider will also provide training on the use, maintenance, and management of the cold storage units. Stakeholder engagements will be conducted in a gender-sensitive manner and will take into consideration the needs of both women and men. A monitoring framework will be developed for this activity.

108. The design developed under Sub-activity 3.5.1 will be validated by community members at a training and validation workshop. The service provider contracted under Sub-activity 3.5.2 will also provide training on the use, management and maintenance of the cold storage facilities as well as making community members aware of the benefits of using these units.

Sub-activity 3.5.2: Install and operate low-maintenance eco-friendly solar-powered cold storage units

109. A firm with experience in developing similar solar-powered cold storage units will be contracted under this activity to construct and install two eco-friendly cold storage units at the West Point fish processing site based on the design developed by the service provider as part of Sub-activity 3.5.1. The cold storage units are designed for use by small-scale fishers, and they do not require regular maintenance. In addition to developing the units, the contracted firm will be responsible for training community members in their use and management. The service provider will also work with the CSC to support future maintenance of the units and the potential for upscaling this system across other coastal areas at the discretion of the Government.

B.4. Implementation arrangements (max 150 words, approximately 3 pages plus diagrams)

110. The project will be implemented following UNDP's National Implementation Modality (NIM), according to the Steering Committee Project Agreement (SCPA) and as per the policies and procedures outlined in the CIPAP.

111. UNDP provides oversight and quality assurance involving UNDP staff at County Offices and at regional and national levels. The project will be monitored by the National Project Steering Committee (NPSC), project oversight and monitoring functions. This will include overseeing the achievement of project management milestones that are established in the CIPAP, as well as ensuring that the project is being implemented according to the Project Management Function. As such the National Project Steering Committee cannot delegate any quality assurance responsibilities to the National Project Coordinator or another party for by the project resources. The project oversight and monitoring functions will be carried out by the UNDP Country Office in collaboration with the NPSC, UNDP is required to deliver CIPAP-specific oversight and quality assurance services including: day-to-day oversight and technical assistance, as well as oversight of project reporting.


*See Annex A2 Section 2.0.11 on delays on service provider performance.

As for the CSC, the UNDP will approve the management structure, which will be incorporated into the tender documents for the CSC and the tender documents for the service providers.

The cost of the cold storage facilities will come from charging those who use a small fee. The estimate is at US$180,000 (150 units x 12 cents).

112. The National Executing Entity (EE) for this project is the Government of Liberia represented by the Environmental Protection Agency (EPA).57 The EE is accountable to UNDP for managing the project, including the monitoring and evaluation of project activities, achieving project results, and the effective use of GEF funds. Any recipient entity that signs a project agreement with an EE must be approved by the EPA, the Ministry of Finance and Public Administration (MFPA), and the Ministry of Mines and Energy (MME). As a Responsible Party will enter into a legally binding Memorandum of Understanding (MoU) with the MME to provide technical assistance in the implementation of the project directly accountable to the EPA as outlined in their terms of agreement. Specifically, the Ministry of Mines and Energy (MME) will be responsible for providing technical support to the Project Manager in the areas of: 1.2 Construct coastal defence structure to protect West Point against coastal erosion; 2.2 Capable the Cross-Sectoral Working Group to mainstream ICBMP into relevant government sectors through a Technical Working Group (TWG); and 2.3 Establish an integrated coastal management system. The TWG will collect a selection of spatial and biological coastal information to support the implementation of the ICBMP (Table 2). The EE will be responsible for ensuring that all project activities are implemented in accordance with national laws, regulations, and policies. The EE will also ensure that the Project Manager is provided with the necessary resources—such as personnel, equipment, and funding—to implement the project.

113. The Project Board/Steering Committee will be chaired by the Director of the EPA and will be comprised of representatives from the EPA, MFPA, UNDP, and MME, including at least 30% women. The World Bank and the GEF Secretariat will also participate in the Project Board as observers. The Project Board will meet regularly with their similar investors in the country, particularly the AFDB-GCF-funded project. The Project Board is responsible for approving the project implementation plan, monitoring progress of the project activities, and ensuring that the project meets its objectives. Project Board decisions will be made in accordance with standards that shall ensure management for development results, accountability, transparency, and sustainability. If consensus cannot be reached on a decision by the Project Board, the UNDP Resident Representative (or their designee) will mediate to find a solution. If this cannot be found, it will take the final decision to ensure project implementation is not unduly delayed. The Project Board will meet at least once per year.

114. The Project Management Unit (PMU) will implement the project with support from the EPA and UNDP and will report to the Project Board. The Project Manager will be appointed to lead the PMU which will run on a day-to-day basis. The Project Manager is responsible for ensuring that all project activities are implemented in accordance with the approved implementation plan, and will end when the final project termination evaluation report and other project closure activities and documentation have been completed. The Project Manager will also ensure that all project activities are completed as per the approved schedule. The Project Manager is responsible for day-to-day management and decision-making for the project. The Project Manager's primary responsibility is to ensure that the project meets its objectives, and that all project activities are implemented in accordance with the approved implementation plan. The Project Director is responsible for the overall direction, strategic guidance and timely delivery of the Project Implementation of the project will be further supported by a technical sub-committee constituted of institutional communication and coordination between the PMU and other relevant stakeholders involved in the project for relevant project activities. # GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V2.0 | PAGE 46 OF 97

## Further Flexibility
Donor funding, including the GCF funding, will be received by the administrative agent (UNDP) on behalf of the executing entity (EPA), and disbursed to the implementing partners (Ips) based on a standard letter of agreement (LoA) signed between the donors and UNDP and an MoU between UNDP and the Executing Entity separately. As per UNDP's National Implementation policy, the LoA will specify the criteria for project assessment/determination of whether or not the project will be eligible for risk activities. UNDP will support the risk assessment and channelling of funds for high value activities that exceed a budget threshold of US$250,000 (for example, Activity 1.2) as a support service provider to the executing entity. The implementing partners will be responsible for ensuring that the project is implemented in accordance with the project workplan and that the requested payment is in accordance with the project workplan; and ii) verification that payment is made to the appropriate party (e.g., the executing entity or the implementing partner). In case of lower risk activities, funds will be advanced by UNDP to goods and service providers upon the request of the EE, and to the PMU for its day-to-day running functions. The Co-financing will not flow through the executing entity. Instead UNDP as administrative agent of the executing entity will procure and transfer funds to the relevant responsible party. In case of higher risk activities, funds will be advanced by UNDP to goods and service providers hired by the Accredited Entity and upon request of the Executing Entity. On the other hand, the Co-financing will flow through the executing entity. The implementing partners will be responsible for ensuring that payment is made to the ministry to pay procured parties and/or transfer such funds to the relevant Responsible Party. For lower risk activities, funds will be advanced to the Executing Entity by UNDP and the executing entity will procure and transfer funds to the goods and service providers hired by the executing entity.

Experience and track record of the Accredited Entity: UNDP

115. In Liberia, UNDP has had environmental protection and climate change responses through the facilitation of policy dialogue with government counterparts and other stakeholders. The UNDP’s experience in Liberia has built strong relationships with decision-makers and proven its strengths as an impartial provider of technical advice on a wide range of issues. UNDP is well positioned to support the Government of Liberia (GoL) to respond to complex issues such as climate change and green growth and is recognized as an experienced agency for policy dialogue and capacity building, bringing a long-term institutional and people-centred focus to capacity development.

Experience and track record of the Executing Entity: Government of Liberia represented by the Environmental Protection Agency

117. Under the National Environmental Policy of Liberia (2003), the GoL set up the Environmental Protection Agency (EPA) as an independent agency with a mandate to protect, preserve, and promote the environment. The EPA is currently tasked with enforcing the National Environment Protection and Management Law under the Executive Branch of Government and works in close collaboration with other government agencies, civil society organizations, and the private sector. This board of directors is required to establish Technical Committees to advise the EPA on priority issues such as climate change.

118. As the National Designated Authority (NDA) for Liberia, the EPA has been involved in the submission of several Concept Notes and Readiness Projects for the GCF. These projects include: i) The Simplified Approaches Process (SAP) Concept Note; ii) Low Carbon Tree Crop Extension Project; and iii) Liberia Adaptation Allocations (LAA). Under the EPA’s leadership, Liberia was selected as a pilot country to participate in the Global Environment Facility (GEF) between 2008 and 2018. The GEF implemented 17 GEF-funded projects with a total value of $35 million.

# B.5. Justification for GCF funding request (max. 1000 words, approximately 2 pages)

119. GCF grants are critical for this proposed project given the limited economic resources of Liberia and its vulnerability to climate change. The country has a high level of debt and is co-financing the current economic situation and borrowing profile of the Government of Liberia (GoL) is limited. Liberia has been classified as one of the world’s least developed countries, with a per capita income below $1054. According to the LDC average gross capital per capita U.S.$ 1,024*. In addition, Liberia's Human Development Index (HDI) of 0.465 ranks the country 179th out of 189 countries assessed by the UNDP Human Development Report 2018. Economic and social conditions in Liberia have been further exacerbated by the Ebola outbreak in 2014, followed by the outbreak of the Ebola virus and subsequent epidemic between 2014 and 2015. The current COVID-19 pandemic poses a new threat to the Liberian economy. External demand for Liberian exports is expected to decrease and


---
**Page 52**
---

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL 2.0 | PAGE 58 OF 97

B

may disappear altogether in the export-oriented mining, agriculture, and forestry sectors[1]. A recent study by IMF observed that macroeconomic stability in Liberia was already elusive with a faltering GDP growth and a spike in inflation to 36% year-on-year. The country’s economic performance has been further weakened by the decline of the exchange rate degrading by 26% over the year, and inflation accelerating to 28% at end-December. Growth in GDP for 2015 was 1.2%, while the projection for 2019 is a 1.4% contraction in the economy. Liberia also does not have access to concessional financing from multilateral development banks, and has limited access to capital from IMF, WB, ADB and other bilateral agencies. Due to these economic risks that persist and the IMF’s classification of Liberia as a country with high vulnerability to external shocks, it is unlikely that Liberia will be able to meet the form of concessionality to replace GCF’s grant for this proposed project. In addition, due to the public good nature of the project activities, there are no private sector beneficiaries or project beneficiaries in a predictable time period. All the activities and outputs of this proposed project result only in environmental benefits and/or co-benefits. As such, it is not possible to identify any private sector beneficiary. A financial modeling-based analysis has not been conducted and a Financial Internal Rate of Return (FIRR) has not been calculated for any of these activities.

120. This project's proposed structure includes three outputs and twelve activities[2]. However, none of the activities or outputs are expected to generate any private sector beneficiaries or project beneficiaries in a predictable time period or the project beneficiaries in a predictable time period. All the activities and outputs of this proposed project result only in environmental benefits and/or co-benefits. As such, it is not possible to identify any private sector beneficiary. A financial modeling-based analysis has not been conducted and a Financial Internal Rate of Return (FIRR) has not been calculated for any of these activities.

However, considering Liberia’s maximum concessional policy, this project's proposed activities have been analyzed from a macro-economic and government perspective to assess the need for GCF grant as the only feasible financial instrument to fund the project activities. Herein, taking into consideration the factors such as: (i) limited access of Liberia to concessional financing; (ii) high vulnerability of Liberia’s economy to external shocks; (iii) fragile economic nature of the target population and Liberia's challenging economic situation; iv) persistent inflationary risks i.e., lack of reforming programmes, and v) catalytic nature of the GCF grants, we recommend the following –


* The nature of the benefit does not accommodate repayment of capital in whatever form or servicing of a loan.
* The vulnerability of beneficiaries and the level of essentiality of the service do not accommodate repayment of capital in whatever form or servicing of a loan.
* There is no financing from GCF, and there is no incremental cost to stretch their contributions owing to budgetary and/or financial constraints.

Hence, it is recommended that in order to reduce / close the existing financing and knowledge gaps and barriers to project implementation, the following options are considered: (i) The option of using the residual funds under GCF’s approach to financing only the incremental costs of additions have been given full consideration during the preparation of this proposal. However, it is recommended that in order to ensure the sustainability of the protective infrastructure at West Point, with additional on-the-ground preparatory work[3] involving co-financing from other sources, such as UNDP and/or other development partners, the GCF grant should be used for climate change adaptation as well as the development of an ACPM, technology transfer and technical support

## The GCF's Approach to Financing

### Post-Project Operations and Maintenance (pp. 48–53)

The proposed project has been designed with a focus on an effective exit strategy that will ensure the long-term sustainability of project interventions and their associated benefits.

#### Post-project operations and maintenance

Post-project operations and maintenance have been designed to incorporate sustainability considerations from the outset, with minimal or no maintenance required beyond the project period. Through stakeholder consultations, the burden of post-project operations and maintenance has been minimized by selecting and designing interventions that are low-maintenance. The rock revetment (Activity 1.2), for example, was selected first and designed to have low maintenance requirements once installed (details in Annex C.2: Engineering Sub-assessment). For what:

#### Post-project operations and maintenance

Post-project operations and maintenance will be provided by UNDP through the development of a specific and focused EISA and associated ESAM in accordance with GCF guidance.

The reference is to Figure 4.1, which shows average coastal erosion between 2030 and 2050.

## Integrated Coastal Zone Management (ICZM) Community

The ICZM approach in Liberia is underpinned by the introduction of an integrated approach to coastal zone management in Liberia that emphasizes capacity-building, cross-sectoral coordination and ongoing monitoring and evaluation. The project will be implemented through a partnership between the Ministry of Works and Monrovia and across Liberia. This process will be informed by active engagement with local stakeholders – including communities, government representatives, CCM members and NGOs under Sub-activity 3.1.4., the specific risks and responsibilities associated with coastal zone management in Liberia are outlined in Annex 20.

The project will also build on lessons learned from the implementation of the ICZM in the country relevant to the local context. Training on the implementation of the ICZM will be also operationalized through a Trainer-of-Trainers approach that will ensure that skills and expertise are shared across the country, and can be both shared and built upon. The facilitation of the capacity-building activities will be undertaken by the MME. This will ensure the sustainability of the facilities and enable communities to host the CCM accountable for their coastal zone management.

The project will also build on lessons learned from the implementation of the ICZM in Liberia, maximizing the likelihood of effective, sustained maintenance beyond the project’s implementation period. Co-management arrangements with local stakeholders will also ensure cooperation and sustainable management of project interventions in the long term.

## Participatory approach to managing ecosystems

129. To ensure the sustainability of ecosystem management activities, the development of community-led co-management arrangements for mangroves and seagrasses will be undertaken with the close involvement of communities bordering the Mwemba Wetland. Their needs and concerns will be carefully considered, facilitating buy-in at all levels. The project’s approach to managing coastal ecosystems in the Mwemba Wetland will be underpinned by: (i) the existing land-use management framework; (ii) the strengthened institutional and regulatory systems; and (iii) the development of a community-based co-management system. This will ensure the importance of mangrove ecosystems and how it sustains marine life, as well as its role in climate change mitigation and adaptation. The project’s approach to managing coastal ecosystems will be underpinned by: (i) the existing land-use management framework; (ii) the strengthened institutional and regulatory systems; and (iii) the development of a community-based co-management system. This will ensure the importance of mangrove ecosystems and how it sustains marine life, as well as its role in climate change mitigation and adaptation.

### Implementation of the mangrove co-management agreements

129. The implementation of the mangrove co-management agreements will be incorporated into the revision of the ICZMP. Embedded within this process is a community-based approach to managing coastal ecosystems that will provide support for mangrove conservation and management beyond the project period through the established institutional and regulatory systems. Conversely, incorporating lessons from community-based implementation of ICZM into the planning phase of the project will ensure that the co-management agreements are embedded in the revision of the ICZMP.

### Establishment of a Community Stewardship Committee

129. The establishment of a Community Stewardship Committee (CSC) will promote community involvement and engagement with the project, as well as provide a platform for the exchange of information between communities that operate, in the interests of ensuring that the committee is self-sustaining beyond the project lifespan. Increased knowledge about mangroves and seagrasses will be shared among communities through the CSC, which will help to value mangroves, highlighting the link between mangrove conservation and health, livelihoods and food security.

### Small income streams

129. This will also provide a platform for the exchange of information on best practices in coastal management. In addition, the small income streams developed through the cold storage facilities (Activity 3.5) and eco-friendly livelihood products (Activity 4.6) will enable the community facilities and the CSC to operate beyond the project lifespan.

## Climate-resilient, gender-responsive and sustainable community livelihoods

129. The project activities are designed to support the communities of Mwemba in achieving their own communities’ resilience to climate change and to generate gender-responsive, sustainable livelihoods. In particular, the long-term benefits of sustainable livelihoods will be promoted as an alternative to limited extraction-based mangroves. This will have significant positive impacts on the lives of women and men living within the project area and will be essential for project implementation.

### Project activities are predominantly targeted at women

129. Project activities are predominantly targeted at Mwemba’s coastal fishing communities, approximately 60% of whom are women. The long-term benefits of project activities will therefore inherently contribute to sustained gender equality. In addition, all communities established during project implementation will promote the inclusion of women in decision-making processes and ensure that they have access to resources which will contribute to a shift in gender relations, which will additionally reduce inherent gender-based vulnerability in future. It also means that the benefits from the project activities will be shared by both men and women across generations and will contribute to the sustainability of project interventions.

### Scaling up and continuation of best practice

129. The project will continue to incorporate sustainability considerations from the outset, including the potential for scaling up and continuation of best practice. Information generated through the development of the Community Stewardship Committee (CSC) and the revision of the ICZMP in Mwemba will be shared widely and used to scale up project interventions across all coastal counties in Liberia. During the implementation period, collaboration between development partners – which have been selected for their experience with similar projects in other countries such as Indonesia, Malaysia, and JICA. These partnerships and experience will provide a robust platform for the implementation of other projects


---
**Page 56**
---

In the upcoming years, extensive engagement with the private sector during the development and implementation of the ICZMP will strengthen public-private partnerships for coastal management, increasing support for future coastal protection projects. To ensure that these partnerships are sustainable beyond project completion, it is essential to adopt a replicable approach used in this project. This ensures their implementation will continue successfully post-project implementation.

In addition, activities promoting uptake of climate-resilient livelihood activities — including the manufacturing of energy-efficient cookstoves and solar lanterns, as well as the establishment of small-scale enterprises such as fish farming and aquaculture — are crucial for facilitating the development of small businesses. To enable the upscaling and replication of these alternative livelihoods, the following actions will be taken:

i) Facilitating relationship building between potential community entrepreneurs and value chain actors;
ii) Providing training on business management and marketing to ensure successful market entry (e.g., through microfinance loans or grants);
iii) Supporting market analysis through the livelihood assessment under Activity 3.3 and marketing through the awareness campaign under Activity 4.2, with a focus on promoting local products; iv) Using education and innovation as platforms for testing new technologies and innovations in the local community.

# D. EXPECTED PERFORMANCE AGAINST INVESTMENT CRITERIA

This section refers to the performance of the project against the investment criteria as set out in the GCF's Initial Investment Framework.

## D.1 Project Performance (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

### 134. The proposed project will contribute to the achievement of the GCF’s Paradigm Shift objective of increased climate resilience and adaptation by: A.2.1 Reducing exposure to climate change impacts; improving climate-resilient coastal planning; strengthening local livelihood practices and promoting the sustainable use of mangrove ecosystems to reduce the climate vulnerability of coastal communities in Monrovia, Liberia; and B.2.1 Strengthening institutional capacity for climate resilience through the GCF Performance Measurement Framework, including Fund-level Impact.

#### Project Outcomes

- **A.5 Increased access to climate-resilient livelihoods and opportunities across communities and regions**
- **A.6 Increased resilience of infrastructure and the built environment to climate change**

### 134. The project will contribute to the achievement of two GCF Fund-Level Outcomes (as per the Performance Measurement Framework) that will be used to assess the contribution of this project to climate-resilient development.

#### A.5 Strengthened institutional and regulatory systems for climate-responsive planning and development

- **Transition from a focus on disaster risk reduction to climate change adaptation**: The project activities (Activities 2.1–2.2) will enhance the technical capacity of the GoL, to plan for and respond to the impacts of climate change.
- **Specific Climate-Sector Working Group (CSWG)**: The CSWG will be established and trained to facilitate coordination among relevant government agencies and other stakeholders in Liberia’s coastal provinces to facilitate climate-responsive planning.

#### A.6 Reduced exposure to climate risks

- **Reduction in Monrovia communities’ exposure to climate risks**: The coastal protection measures implemented through this project will reduce Monrovian communities' vulnerability to climate risks.
- **Reduced risk of loss and damage associated with exposure to climate risks**: The risk of loss and damage from storms and associated damage to infrastructure and houses at West Point community will be substantially reduced.

### Adaptation impact

- **1.3 million people are expected to directly benefit 250,000 people (69%) women in the target communities of: i) West Point (through coastal defense and enhanced livelihoods); ii) Topside Village; iii) Pokpori and Fiamara; and iv) Nipry Village. The project will also indirectly benefit approximately 1 million people (25%) men in the target communities, who are expected to benefit from improved access to climate-resilient livelihoods. It is also expected to indirectly benefit approximately 1 million people (26%) in Monrovia (49% women). This will be achieved by: i) reducing exposure of vulnerable coastal communities and infrastructure to climate risks; ii) strengthening the adaptive capacity of Monrovian’s citizens who reside within and alongside the Mesurado Wetlands by strengthening local livelihood practices, promoting sustainable use of mangrove ecosystems, and supporting the update of diversified climate-resilient livelihoods (Output 3). When counted with the West Point coastal defense project, this will also contribute to reducing exposure of vulnerable communities in the areas prioritized for interventions; iii) many of whom are involved in fishing and fish processing (10%). The entire population of the Mesurado Wetlands is estimated at approximately 25,000 people. This will be achieved by: iv) the establishment of climate-resilient ICZM practices and related capacity development (Output 2) in the MMA.

### D.2 Paradigm shift potential (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)


---
**Page 62**
---

The project will catalyse a paradigm shift in the approach to coastal management in Liberia, shifting from a focus on disaster risk reduction and post-disaster planning to incorporating medium- and long-term climate considerations into coastal development planning. This shift will be realised through the construction of robust coastal defence measures in targeted one of Liberia’s most vulnerable communities (Dukpu 1), the development of a climate-smart ICZMP for the entire county, and the implementation of an improved community fish farming system at Dukpu 2. All shall act as novel approaches to address the impacts of climate change on livelihoods in the Monrovia Metropolitan Area (Section 6.1 of Annex A2). The project will also support a series of integrated coastal zone management (ICZM) assessments for the proposed project will strengthen the case for adopting the ICZM approach in Liberia and the strengthening of the framework for implementing effective ICZM and coastal adaptation will be strengthened through: i) the development of a national ICZM strategy, ii) harmonisation of policies and regulations across sectors to support implementation of the national ICZM strategy, iii) the establishment of a multi-stakeholder platform and institutional investments in coastal zone management through a T+G approach. This strengthened framework and approach will be used as a model for replication in other vulnerable communities in Liberia.

The project will also contribute to the development of an improved community fish farming system at Dukpu 2, which will serve as a model for replicating this process in other coastal communities in Liberia. The successful establishment of the shoreline at West Point will form the basis for replicating this process in other vulnerable communities in Liberia.

# Contribution to the creation of an enabling environment

141. The project will create an enabling environment for climate-resilient coastal planning, the sustainable management and governance of coastal resources, and the implementation of ICMCPs and other tools and methodologies under Output 2. This will be achieved through a series of activities under Output 3 that have been specifically designed to create enabling conditions for the integrated approach to coastal management and governance that are necessary to undertake long-term, climate-resilient planning in vulnerable coastal areas.

142. The project will make a strong contribution to the development of ICMCPs in Liberia and assist the country to meet its legislative requirement of developing an ICMCP and updating this plan every three years. This will be achieved through a series of activities under Output 3 that have been designed as enabling activities that result in the ongoing development of diversified and climate-resilient livelihoods, as well as contributing to sustainable use of mangrove ecosystems.

Overall contribution to climate-resilient development pathways consistent with relevant national climate change adaptation strategies and plans.

145. The project will contribute to the protection of ecosystems and biodiversity in Monrovia through strengthening and promoting climate-resilient livelihoods (Outcome 3). This will yield the environmental co-benefits listed below:

- Reduction in GHG emissions from burning fossil fuels for cooking, by facilitating the production of energy-efficient cookstoves. These co-benefits are described below:

- Reduction in replacement costs of economic assets damaged by erosion and storm surges — including industry, transport and housing — will benefit the city through inter alia circumventing loss and replacement costs.

## Improving mangrove management in the Mesurado estuary will benefit the fishing sector as these ecosystems act as critical breeding habitats for economically important fish species. The economic value of mangroves is estimated at $15 million per year.

### Social cohesion
The project will contribute to social development and cohesion by building the resilience of vulnerable coastal communities and their livelihoods in the impacts of climate change. This will yield multiple socio-economic benefits, including:

- Enhancing coastal defence infrastructure (Outcome 1) will protect coastal communities, reduce mortality and injuries from flooding and erosion, and improve access to markets for fishers.
- The project will also provide a range of other services to communities with coast zones — including homes, schools, clinics and roads, as well as livelihood assets such as boats and fishing gear. These benefits will be particularly important in the context of climate change.

### Gender-sensitive development
The project will contribute to the implementation of both Liberia’s Gender Policy 2009, which aims to reduce gender inequality, and the Climate Change Gender Action Plan for Liberia (2012), which promotes gender-sensitive adaptation strategies. The project will achieve this by:

- Improving access to climate information through the implementation of gender-sensitive activities, including: i) gender-responsive training for the management of coastal defence infrastructure; ii) the development of a gender action plan for the design and implementation of coastal adaptation options and alternative livelihood opportunities; and iv) increasing the resilience of Mesurado’s fishing communities by providing access to climate information and services to women.

### D.4 Needs of recipient (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)
#### Vulnerability of the country and beneficiary groups
Liberia is ranked as one of the most vulnerable countries in terms of impacts of climate change, ranking 8th in the 2017 Climate Change Vulnerability Index[3]. This vulnerability is largely linked to the country’s exposure to SLR and extreme weather events. The projected increase in sea level rise (SLR) due to climate change is expected under future climate conditions, and the socio-economic impacts of such events, coupled with SLR and the resulting coastal erosion, are likely to be exacerbated by Liberia’s high population density and associated concentrations of economic and social assets in its coastal zone as well as its low adaptive capacity. The projected increase in extreme weather events will also have a significant impact on the livelihoods of the population living in low-lying coastal zones — mostly concentrated in the capital city of Monrovia, which is located within the Mesurado estuary[4]. This makes Liberia’s coastal zone one of the most vulnerable communities to SLR and extreme weather events in West Africa. The Mesurado Wetland, making it one of the most vulnerable communities to SLR and extreme weather events in Liberia, with approximately 10,800 people — 49% women — projected to be directly at risk from coastal retreat by 2050[5].

[3] Aspinall R., Kates G., Gomes D. de, Semenov T., Chilingir Y., Milne D.E., Nyantama A. and Flitcroft C. 2014. Carbon pools and multiple ecosystem services in the Mesurado estuary, Liberia: a case study of climate change vulnerability. In: Climate Change Vulnerability Index.

[4] Annex B.2. Vulnerability Sub-assessment (page 5)

**Page 66**

190. The projected increase of Monrovia’s population — at 3% per year — coupled with the ongoing shoreline loss is leading to an increase in the density of the city’s population, putting considerable pressure on the environment and communities that depend on coastal resources for their livelihoods. This will result in a further decline in Liberia’s GDP and employs at least 37,000 people in Monrovia alone. The vulnerability of these livelihoods is being exacerbated by the impacts of SLR and the increasing frequency of extreme weather events along the coast of West Point. These mangroves are also important for supporting coastal artisanal fisheries of the Grand Bassa communities who derive livelihoods from coastal waters and fish stocks. The mangroves in the Monrovia Wetland serve as a buffer against storm surges, reducing erosion and protecting the shoreline. They also provide habitat for the health of mangrove ecosystems. In addition, these mangroves are an important source of foodstuff and other resources that support the livelihoods of coastal communities. The increasing frequency of extreme weather events and extreme weather events is threatening the supply of these resources, including nutrient-rich fish which serve as the most prominent protein in the diet of many people living along the coast. This has a direct impact on their food security of these communities, and it revenues from fisheries — especially for women, as 60% of persons who are involved in fishing activities are women.

151. The proposed project addresses these vulnerabilities by (i) building a coastal reinforcement of 1,050 m of coastline at West Point — including landings for fishing boats (Output 1); ii) improving the management of Liberia’s coastal resources and increasing their productivity to ensure food security and income generation for climate-resilient livelihoods and improving the resilience of climate-sensitive livelihoods in Monrovia (Output 3).

### Domestic financial, social and economic status quo

Since 1990, Liberia has been classified as a Least Developed Country (LDC) with a GDP per capita of US$285. The Human Development Index (HDI) is 0.465 ranks the country 17th out of 189 countries assessed by the UNDP Human Development Report. However, Liberia’s economic performance has been severely curtailed by the outbreak of the Ebola virus and subsequent epidemic between 2014 and 2015. The Department for International Development (DFID) estimates that Liberia’s GDP contracted by about 38% in 2015. Figure 16 shows that, compared to other LDCs, Liberia has a lower GNI per capita index equivalent to only 30% of the average figure for LDCs.

| 47.2 | 0.51.8 | 941.00 | 6,120.00 |

Figure 15 shows that in 2015, Liberia is was more economically vulnerable than the average figure for LDCs.

133. Liberia is disproportionately reliant on its primary production sectors for economic growth and development with the agriculture, forestry and fishing sectors accounting for approximately 70% of GDP in 2018[1]. Reliance on these sectors makes Liberia extremely vulnerable to global commodity price fluctuations and market shocks. This economic vulnerability in turn compromises the ability of the GoL to deliver basic services such as water, housing, transport and energy to its citizens. The impact of climate change is expected to further exacerbate this situation by increasing poverty levels, reducing food security and health – means that funding climate change adaptation interventions would protect vulnerable communities from the adverse impacts of climate change.

134. Liberia’s National Adaptation Plan (NAP) 2015-2025[2] identifies five priority areas for action: i) improving urban planning in government institutions for incorporating climate change considerations; ii) increasing access to information and knowledge management systems as well as to disseminate information products to coastal communities; iii) training and capacity building, integrated planning and the development of infrastructure.

135. Liberia’s National Climate Change Action Plan (ccCAP), 2012 prioritises the mainstreaming of gender equality into national climate change policies, programmes and interventions. The project contributes to one of the ccCAP’s six priority areas: i) improving urban planning in government institutions for incorporating climate change considerations; ii) increasing access to information and knowledge management systems as well as to disseminate information products to coastal communities.

136. This will be achieved by: (i) protecting vulnerable homes through establishing hard infrastructure, it incorporating

### Institutional capacity needs

134. To date, the GoL has established several laws, policies, strategies and institutions to address the increasing vulnerability of its citizens to climate change impacts. However, limited institutional capacity within most government departments is a significant barrier to the implementation of climate change adaptation interventions in Liberia. The GoL’s ability to implement climate change adaptation interventions is also hampered by: i) limited human resources; ii) lack of technical and financial resources for implementing climate change adaptation interventions resulting in insufficient: (i) work facilities, (ii) access to technical equipment; iii) training to use and maintain technical equipment; and iv) data collection, analysis and management. Furthermore, there is limited technical capacity within the GoL’s institutions to develop and implement climate change adaptation interventions.

### D.5 Country ownership (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

136. The National Climate Change Action Plan (ccCAP), 2012 identifies the impacts of extreme climate events to be a high priority, as evident from the development of several national laws and plans and the commitment of the GoL to prioritising climate change adaptation within its GoL forance for Oiit of the proposed project, as well as the work plan of the Ministry of Mines and Energy. The GoL has also developed several other policies and strategies beyond the project implementation period. The proposed project’s alignment with these plans and policies is described below.

137. The Pre-Po Agenda for Prosperity and Development (PAP), 2018[3] is Liberia’s five-year National Development Plan, which sets out a clear vision of how to achieve prosperity in the country by 2025. The PAP contributes to three of the four pillars of the PAP: namely; i) Power to the People by contributing to lifelong education and training for all Liberians; ii) Economic Growth and Prosperity by contributing to the protection of climate-sensitive livelihoods; and iii) Governance and Transparency by building the capacity of the GoL for effective governance.

138. The National Policy on Response Strategy on Climate Change (2018)[4] is the central policy underlying Liberia’s NAP 2015-2025[5]. The NAP identifies the sectors most vulnerable to climate change impacts and names five existing pillars to support the safeguarding of these sectors. These include: i) urban planning; ii) infrastructure development; iii) institutional coordination, capacity-building; integrated planning and the development of infrastructure.

139. The ccCAP[6] is a multi-sectoral national plan that identifies climate change as one of its five priority areas for action along with addressing coastal erosion in vulnerable areas of the MWA. The project further aligns with Liberia’s NAP by: supporting: i) improved urban planning capacity in government institutions for incorporating climate change considerations; ii) increasing access to information and knowledge management systems as well as to disseminate information products to coastal communities.

## Stakeholder Engagement

155. The project was developed following an extensive stakeholder engagement process, beginning with initial consultations and engagements during the first phase of project design in 2016, prior to the initiation of the PPF-financed Feasibility Study. The majority of the first-phase engagements focused on identifying broad needs and priorities for climate change adaptation and resilience building. Subsequent stakeholder engagements were also organized with relevant government departments to validate the design of the project and ensure its alignment with national policies.

156. Further stakeholder engagements focused on beneficiary communities were conducted in 2019 as part of the second phase of development. In total, over 40 community stakeholder engagements were conducted from February to May 2019 at various local ownership of the project and to capture the needs of communities more directly. In this context, stakeholder engagement was primarily structured to:

- **The EPA** was established under the National Environmental Policy of Liberia (NEP) as an independent authority for the management of its resources.
- Further details on the stakeholder engagement process are presented in Annex 7.

167. Final institutional stakeholder engagements and validation were held during the concluding phases of project development, between July and September 2019. These engagements included a three-day workshop in Monrovia that was attended by representatives from government agencies, civil society organizations (CSOs), local communities, as well as several consultants and technical experts involved with the development of the project. The workshop provided an opportunity for stakeholders to review and validate the draft project design document and provide input. The final project design was validated at a workshop held in Monrovia on the 5th September 2019 and approved by the Ministry of Mines and Energy (MINE), Ministry of Public Works (MPW), Ministry of Finance and Development Planning (MFDP) and the World Bank.

#### D. Detailed Project Description (max. 800 words, approximately 1 page)

168. The proposed project is requesting grant financing from the GCF to address the climate change impacts of SLR, accelerated coastal erosion, and increasingly fierce and intense storms on vulnerable coastal communities. The grant funding requested by the project will be reinforced with additional co-finance from UNDP and the GOL.

### Incorporation of best practice

169. The project incorporates best practices and lessons learned from relevant ongoing interventions and international experiences in the design of the project, particularly those that have been implemented in Liberia, but also internationally, particularly two GEF-funded projects in Monrovia (refer to Section B.1 for further detail). Best practice and lessons learned are incorporated into the project design with respect to: i) the selection of appropriate coastal protection measures; ii) the construction of coastal protection measures – including labour practices appropriate for the Monrovian context, as well as the use of locally available materials where possible; iii) the development of livelihoods directly or indirectly on mangroves and/or for marine resources; and iv) the development of a conservation strategy that addresses specific aspects of best practice that have been incorporated into the design of the project are summarized below.

- **Designing the coastal protection measures to have minimal maintenance requirements to ensure their sustainability**
- **Incorporating the use of locally available materials where possible, including beach profile data, bathymetry and sediment dynamics into local planning and management of coastal activities**
- **Adopting an Integrated Coastal Management (ICM) approach to coastal zone management by applying a systemic and coordinated approach in the identification and management of risks in coastal areas**
- **Improving governance in ICM planning through establishment and management of the cross-sectoral working group for coastal zone management at national level**
- **Capitalizing on the wealth of local ecological knowledge held by civil society by establishing and sustaining community-based monitoring systems to ensure that these are integrated into project design, implementation and M&E**
- **Collaborating with development partners** working in Liberia through the design and implementation of the project

170. The project also incorporates the principles of adaptive management by making provision for iterative design and revision under several activities. For example, the ICZMP (Activity 2.5) will be revised three years after it is initially developed to reflect lessons learned from the initial implementation phase of the coastal community awareness campaign (Activity 2.5) and state-of-knowledge assessments (Activity 3.3) will be updated annually for the duration of the project. This ensures that the design, implementation and M&E processes of the project interventions by ensuring that they are specific and responsive within the Liberian context.

# Cost-effectiveness

172. The base estimate of the expected net present value (ENPV) of the project is USD17.73 million using a 10% discount rate, with an economic internal rate of return of 21%. In addition to the above damages estimated, benefits also include an increase in income growth and the social benefit of the project through improved infrastructure.

173. Of the USD59 million of project costing, 24% of the necessary budget resources or USD14 million is contributed in the form of Co-financing with majority funded by GCF, in spite of its economic and budget constraints mentioned above, bringing in co-finance resources — in the form of grants and in-kind contributions — to the project demonstrating that the project has a strong case for inclusion. This is also more responsibility to ensure the resilience of its population.

173. This project proposes the implementation of coastal protection measures covering approximately 1,000 m of exposed shoreline with mangrove planting as well as measures to protect and enhance the climate-resilience of the livelihoods of ~25% of Morrovo's population. The cost of these measures is USD104 / beneficiary. This is a reasonable cost, especially given, the fact that the indirect beneficiaries are excluded.

## Engineering Sub-representation (Area C.2)
This detailed design and construction plan will identify all required inputs and outputs for the project, procurement procedures, contract terms, and gender-sensitive considerations. The design and construction plan will be validated by community and government stakeholders prior to implementation.

### 1.2 Coastal Community Defence and Revetment at West Point against climate change-induced coastal erosion.
An engineering firm will be engaged to conduct a vulnerability assessment and green space analysis for the design and construction plan for revetments at West Point. The revetment will protect the West Point community from accelerated coastal erosion and provide access to the community space as well as protection for fishing boats.

### 2.1 Develop an Integrated Coastal Zone Management Plan for Liberia.
Develop a high-resolution, multi-criteria vulnerability map of the entire coastline of the Liberian coastlines in consultation with relevant government and stakeholders. This will be done through:
- Producing a multi-criteria vulnerability map of the Liberian coasts;
- Developing an ICSMP (Integrated Coastal Sectoral Working Group (ICSWG) to establish an ICSMP plan; and iv) Revising and updating after three years to incorporate lessons learned.

### 2.2 Capacitate the Cross-Sectoral Working Group.
The Cross-Sectoral Working Group (CSWG) will be trained in the Climate Smart Water, Energy, and Transport (CSET) approach to increase its capacity for developing an ICSMP. This will include training workshops for members of the CSWG and representatives of relevant government sectors.

### 2.3 Host a validation workshop.
Host a validation workshop to present and validate the design and construction plan to relevant community stakeholders.

## 1.2.1 Coastal Community Defence and Revetment at West Point against climate change-induced coastal erosion
- **Revetment (coastal protection):** Construction of approximately 1,000 m of revetments on promenade and community amenities at West Point.

## Strengthen the asset base and technical capacity of the ICM Unit (ICZM Unit) to support the implementation of the ICZMP

### 2.3 Strengthen the asset base and technical capacity of the ICM Unit (ICZM Unit) to support the implementation of the ICZMP

The capacity of the ICM Unit (ICZM Unit) for the collection, processing, and dissemination of spatial, meteorological and oceanographic data relevant to the implementation of the ICZMP will be strengthened. This will include:

- i) The development of high-resolution remote sensing data for mangrove ecosystems health monitoring.
- ii) The development of a vulnerability map for Sub-Metropolitan areas.
- iii) The development of a database for the collection and processing of the same data, with support for technical staff to use and maintain the system.

### 2.4 Strengthen the existing Environmental Knowledge Management System (EKMS) to improve environmental awareness-raising

The newly implemented EKMS will be capable of improving its accessibility and functionality by integrating ICM into the system. ICT equipment will also be provided for the 10 government institutions.

## Community-led co-management agreement to ensure sustainable management of mangroves in the MMB

### 3.2. Establish community-led co-management agreement to ensure sustainable management of mangroves in the MMB.

A co-management agreement will be developed through a participatory process involving government stakeholders and local communities, taking into consideration: (i) governance structures; (ii) general social groups; and (iii) gender issues. The agreement will define community responsibilities for the conservation and sustainable use of mangroves, including the management of fishing activities.

### 3.3. Conduct annual assessments to evaluate the project-induced changes in mangrove extent and condition in the MMB using high-resolution data from Sub-Activity 2.5.1. An initial assessment will be conducted during Year 1 and repeated annually throughout the project implementation period to assess the impact of project interventions.

Spatial analyses of the change in mangrove extent and condition in the MMB will be conducted using the high-resolution data from Sub-Activity 2.5.1. The analysis will assess the changes in mangrove extent and condition over time, including the impact on relevant government, conservation, research, and CSG members.

### 3.4. Host a workshop to discuss findings of Activity 3.3 during Year 5 based on findings from Activity 3.3.

E.7 Monitoring, reporting and evaluation arrangements (max. 508 words, approximately 1 page)

### 174. Project-level monitoring and evaluation will be undertaken in compliance with the UNDP POPP and the UNDP Evaluation Policy.

The primary responsibility for day-to-day project monitoring and implementation rests with the Project Manager. The Project Manager will develop annual work plans to ensure the efficient implementation of the project. The Project Manager is responsible for ensuring that all project activities are implemented as per the approved implementation, including the implementation of the M&E plan, so that the appropriate support and corrective measures can be taken in a timely manner.

The UNDP Office will provide a high level of transparency, responsibility and accountability in monitoring and reporting project results.

### 175. The UNDP Office will support the Project Manager as needed, including through annual supervision missions. The UNDP Country Office is responsible for complying with UNDP project-level M&E requirements as outlined in the UNDP POPP and the UNDP Evaluation Policy. A detailed project monitoring plan (M&EP) will be provided by the UNDP Regional Technical Adviser as needed. The project target groups and stakeholders including the NDA Focal Point will be involved as much as possible in project-level M&E.

### 176. A project inception workshop will be held after the UNDP project document has been signed by all relevant parties. The purpose of this workshop is to: a) review the project document, particularly in the overall context that influence project implementation; b) discuss the roles and responsibilities of the project team including reporting requirements and timelines for reporting; c) discuss the project monitoring plan (M&EP); d) discuss reporting, monitoring and evaluation roles and responsibilities and finalize the M&E plan; e) review financial reporting procedures and processes; f) review the project implementation plan and schedule Project Board meetings and finalize the first annual work plan. The final inception report will be completed by the UNDP Regional Technical Adviser and approved by the Project Board and other stakeholders.

### 177. A project implementation report will be prepared for each year of project implementation. The Project Manager, the UNDP Country Office, and the UNDP Regional Technical Adviser will provide objective input to the annual GCF ACC. This report should include: a) an overview of the project progress; b) an assessment of how well the project is performing against the approved work plan; c) an evaluation of the project’s performance in terms of its impact on poverty reduction and sustainable development, as well as any lessons learned from the project implementation process.

### 178. An interim evaluation will be undertaken annually well in advance of the GCF APR submission deadline. Progress against the gender action plan, stakeholder engagement, social and environmental safeguards updates, challenges and delays must also be reviewed at this stage to ensure that these are addressed before the final evaluation report is submitted to the Project Board and other stakeholders.

### 179. An independent interim evaluation will be undertaken, and the findings and responses outlined in the management response will be incorporated as recommendations for enhanced implementation during the final phase of project implementation. The interim evaluation report will follow the standard templates and guidance available on the UNDP Evaluation Resource Centre. The final evaluation report will be completed by the UNDP Regional Technical Adviser and will be approved by the Project Board. The final evaluation report will be available in English.

### 180. An independent final evaluation will take place when project activities have concluded. The terms of reference, the evaluation process and the final evaluation report will follow the standard templates and guidance available on the UNDP Evaluation Resource Centre. The final evaluation report will be completed by the UNDP Regional Technical Adviser and will be approved by the Project Board. The TE report will be available in English.

**Page 89**
---

181\. A household survey for MRRV for the project is an independent impact assessment which will be commissioned to a third party. While the detailed scope of the assessment will be further developed, it is expected that the assessment will involve a representative sample of households in the four prioritized communities in Monrovia. These surveyed households will include both the project target and non-target beneficiaries with a comparison of project impact at various levels. Adoption indicators are measured as test sites to improve access to alternative livelihoods and reduce poverty in targeted households such as use of mangroves and settlement planning. The execution of activities such as livelihood diversification, improved agricultural practices, and increased income generation could possibly permit investigation of impacts of these three interventions independently as well as in combination (the beneficiaries will be selected based on vulnerability criteria). This assessment is expected to take place over a period of one year. Moreover, this assessment will make use of satellite data to assess the changes in settlement patterns, the efficacy of the investment and reduced rate of mangrove degradation etc. before and after interventions.

182\. The UNDP Country Office will include the planned project evaluations in the UNDP Country Office evaluation plan, and ensure that all relevant documentation is available in English and the management response to the public UNDP Evaluation Resource Centre (ERC) (www.erc.undp.org).

183\. The UNDP Country Office will retain all M&E records for this project for up to seven years after project financial closure in order to support ex-post evaluations.

184\. A detailed MEE budget, monitoring plan and evaluation plan will be included in the UNDP project document.

185\. Monitoring, reporting and evaluation arrangements will comply with the relevant GCF policies and Accreditation Master Agreement signed between GCF and UNDP.

## West Point

### Mitigation Measure(s)

- The revetment at West Point has been designed to protect the vulnerable shoreline and fix in place.
- The revetment will use porous materials, a permeable design and include a drainage system to enable water to drain through the structure and into the river.
- During the development of the detailed design and construction plan under activity 1.1, a site-specific hydrological study was conducted to assess the flood risk in West Point. The study identified that the revetment will help reduce existing localized and broader flooding and mitigate this risk by enabling the design of adequate drainage capacity.

### Other Potential Risks in the Horizon

- Current events (domestically and internationally) could negatively influence the project's budget, including implementation costs.
- This may result in: i) suboptimal and incomplete implementation; ii) a reduction in the number of beneficiaries.
- Project development might be delayed by the COVID-19 pandemic as national and regional responses to the pandemic are likely to impact the availability of resources for project activities. The Government of Liberia has issued regulation mandated by the Government of Liberia in response to COVID-19. Project staff will take additional precautions to ensure that stakeholders and beneficiaries are not exposed to and that project activities do not in any way contribute to the spread of COVID-19.

### G. GCF POLICIES AND STANDARDS

#### Environmental and social risk assessment (max. 750 words, approximately 1.5 pages)

- The environmental and social risks associated with the project were evaluated in accordance with GCF's Environmental and Social Framework (ESF) and its corresponding ESF Implementation Guidelines (EIG).
- The project is considered to be Category B or Medium Risk (GCF risk) and Moderate Risk (UNDP risk category). This early assessment is based on the Environmental and Social Assessment Report (ESAR), which was developed for the project subject to this categorization. The ESAR complies with the broad ESG requirements of the GCF, UNDP, and other relevant international standards.
- It is important to note that the ESAR is an interim report in order to comply with national legislation, an Environmental and Social Management Plan (ESMP) will be developed for the project subject to this categorization. The environmental authority in Liberia — prior to the implementation of Activity 1.2: The primary environmental and social risks associated with the project — as identified by ESAR — are presented below.

#### Environmental Impacts

- Overall, the project is likely to result in some negative environmental impacts.
- These impacts are, however, expected to be mitigated through the application of appropriate mitigation measures.
- During the construction phase of Output 1.2, substantial earthworks will need to take place along the West Point beachfront. Potential impacts include: i) sedimentation and erosion; ii) changes in water quality; iii) loss of coastal vegetation; iv) changes in water quality in the short-term as well as a shift in sediment transport patterns in the long-term.
- Additionally, there are risks associated with the construction of the embankment. These risks include: i) potential impacts on fishery resources that have, however, been accounted for in the ESAR and ESMP; and appropriate risk mitigation procedures will be included in the Terms of Reference for contractors.

#### Social Impacts

- The social risks were identified during the Feasibility Study as a result of the construction of a revetment at WestPoint — and an associated loss of beach areas — both of which have been mitigated through an adaptive management approach.
- These impacts are expected to be mitigated by: i) providing alternative access points for the local community land used for domestic purposes, bathing and socialising; and ii) fishing livelihoods, which will occur because of the construction of a new jetty at WestPoint. The design of the jetty includes an area that can be used as a commercial fish market.
- The final design for the revetment includes a green promenade with associated amenities that will be available to be used as community spaces and recreational areas, including a children's playground and a sports court.
- The project is also expected to have positive social impacts on a local scale at a result of an existing pedestrian/bicycle pathway associated with the port of Monrovia.

#### Other Social Impacts

- The ESAR has identified other social risks as a result of the other activities under the project, which are expected to yield a range of social benefits, including strengthened livelihoods, food security and mangrove areas as well as the protection of homes and infrastructure.
- It should be further noted here that these impacts have been assessed and any potential impacts on Indigenous Peoples have been investigated and will not occur under the project.

#### Grievance Redress Mechanism

- The Grievance Management (GRM) and Complaints Register (CR) have been developed for this project and are detailed in the ESMP.
- These two mechanisms are compatible with Liberian legislation and provide a means by which affected parties can raise their concerns or grievances to the project team, who will then agree/given to communicate their concerns and/or grievances through an appropriate process. The GRM and CR are designed to ensure that all complaints are addressed in a timely manner and to provide a response to concerned stakeholders, especially any vulnerable groups who may lack access to formal legal systems of support.
