### G.2 Gender assessment and action plan (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

A gender assessment and action plan— in line with the objectives of the GFCS’s Gender Policy and gender guidelines—is being developed by UNDP to inform the design of the proposed project (Annex B). The document was based on a review of data as well as consultations with government officials, civil society organizations (CSOs), donor agencies and multilateral development banks.

The gender assessment shows that gender inequality persists in Liberia, largely due to patriarchal norms that maintain women’s subordinate status. Liberia ranks 178 out of 185 countries on the UNDP’s Gender Inequality Index (GII). The GII is a composite measure of three dimensions: reproductive health; empowerment and education; and economic security. Liberia scores 0.492, which means that women in Liberia are 49% less likely to have equal opportunities as men. Liberia ranks 178 out of 185 countries on the UNDP’s Gender Inequality Index (GII). The GII is a composite measure of three dimensions: reproductive health; empowerment and education; and economic security.

Gender gaps in human development are significant, with women having lower levels of education than men. Women have an average of 6 years of schooling compared to 10 for men. Women’s literacy rate is 42% while the male literacy rate is 73%. Women also earn less income than men: USD $77 per woman and USD $95 per man; and levels of adult HIV are 2.4% among women, compared with 1.6% for men.

Women in Liberia face significant challenges in their daily lives. The high level of domestic violence against women is imposed upon women through the social cultural allocation of gender roles restricts their livelihood opportunities. Women’s limited access and appreciation of women are employed in the informal sector (30%). This high proportion of women in the informal sector limits their social and economic empowerment.

As a result of these gender roles and inequalities, women in Liberia are more vulnerable than men to coastal erosion which causes the destruction of infrastructure, loss of property, displacement, decreases in income, as well as many other negative impacts. Women’s limited access to land and resources exacerbates existing gender inequalities by exacerbating the challenges women already face such as family care and food insecurity. For example, women are more likely than men to be affected by climate change because they are responsible for water collection and cooking. In addition, women in Liberia are more vulnerable to climate change impacts due to their limited access to resources and information.

From the findings of the gender assessment, a gender action plan (GAP) for the project was developed to ensure gender is mainstreamed in project design and implementation. In particular, the GAP recommends that: 1) Women’s participation in decision-making processes be increased; 2) The project should increase women’s skills and economic opportunities. By increasing the climate-resilience of Monrovia’s fishing community, the project strengthens women’s livelihoods and increases their resilience to climate change impacts.

Stakeholder consultations under the project will be conducted in a gender-sensitive manner and encourage women’s participation. This will ensure that women are not only involved as beneficiaries but also as groups to pursue climate-resilient and sustainable livelihoods in the Mesurado mangroves, as well as through women’s organizations. The project will also mainstream gender into all aspects of the project, including climate-sensitive activities and stakeholder consultations, which will contribute to reducing the gender gap of social, economic and environmental vulnerability to climate change.

On the project implementation side, a Gender Specialist will be posted to the Project Management Unit to provide advice and quality assurance oversight, conduct capacity building and to monitor the implementation of all gender-related components. The Gender Specialist will work closely with the Ministry of Health, the Environmental Protection Agency, the Center for All Disaster Management, the Ministry of Gender, Children and Social Protection, and local NGOs will create the cross-sector technical perspectives required to ensure the sustainability of project activities.

# Secretariat’s assessment of FP160

## Overall assessment of the Secretariat

The funding proposal is presented to the Board for consideration with the following remarks:

| Strengths | Points of caution |
| --- | --- |
| The project addresses shoreline regression at West Point, where dwellings and public infrastructure are under threat, including basic services such as water supply and sanitation. | Risk of maladaptation needs to be carefully assessed and mitigated during planning and finalizing the engineering design of the revetment structure. |
| The Government of Liberia contributes to a considerable extent in co-financing despite its budgetary constraints. |  |

2: The Board may wish to consider approving this funding proposal with the terms and conditions set out on the respective term sheet and addendum XVI, titled "List of proposed conditions and recommendations".

## Summary of the Secretariat’s assessment

### Project background

Monrovia, the capital city of Liberia, is vulnerable to the climate change impacts of sea-level rise. The projected increase in sea level will exacerbate existing threats from coastal erosion and shoreline retreat. SLR also threatens the sustainability of ecosystem services provided by the West Point Marine Area (WPMA) and the Greater Monrovia Metropolitan Area (GAMA), exacerbating the already vulnerable mangroves that are under threat of over-exploitation and urban encroachment. These changes have resulted in change of habitat for economically important species, such as fish, which will negatively impact the fishery-based livelihoods of Monrovians.

The West Point Marine Area is located on the western coast of Monrovia, near West Point, one of the most densely populated and poorest areas on the Monrovian coast and the section most vulnerable to climate change-induced erosion of coastal zones. Near West Point, the shoreline has regressed by 30 meters in the last decade, with a corresponding loss of mangrove forest cover and boat launching sites that are critical to fisher-based livelihoods. It is forecast that coast erosion resulting from SLR will continue unabated for at least another two decades.


---
**Page 105**
---

diverse climate-resilient livelihoods. In this way, the proposed project will build the long-term climate resilience of coastal communities in Liberia by both addressing immediate adaptation priorities and creating an enabling environment for upscaling coastal adaptation initiatives at scale.

6. The total project cost is USD 25.6 million, all in the form of grants; GCF financing amounts to USD 7.3 million, most of which will be contributed to the revetment construction along the West Point coastline (USD 4.1 million), with additional contributions from Liberia (USD 6.8 million in form of grant and in-kind contributions) and UNDP (USD 1.6 million grant).

7. The environmental and social safeguards classification of the project is category B, as informed by the screening performed by UNDP.

### Component-by-component analysis

#### Output 1: Protection of coastal communities and infrastructure at West Point against erosion caused by climate change and sea-level rise (total cost: USD 25.6 million; GCF cost: USD 7.3 million or 28 per cent)

Under this output, GCF grant funding will be used to protect the vulnerable community at West Point from coastal erosion using revetment structures. This revetment structure was selected as the preferred solution because while a "soft solution" in the form of beach nourishment could also be considered, its long-term maintenance and sustainability is questionable as the required regular maintenance is not considered feasible in the local context. The revetment will be constructed using locally available materials and labour. As per common good practice, appropriate drainage will be included in the detailed design of the structure to allow run-off and prevent localized flooding behind the structure.

- GCF funding will cover 28% of the total cost (USD 7.3 million) for construction and the remaining costs are borne by co-financers, which will cover the cost of finalization and validation of the project design and construction plans, and rock material for construction of the revetment at West Point.

Structural measures for coast protection may cause changes of wave patterns which leads to increased erosion in other areas. The Liberia Coastal Zone Management Authority (CZMA) has confirmed that through detailed modelling it is evident that no effect on the wider coastline will take place around West Point due to stabilizing the coastline with marine boulders.

#### Output 2: Institutional capacity-building and policy support for the implementation of Integrated Coastal Management Plan (ICMP) across Liberia (total cost: USD 18.4 million; GCF cost: USD 1.6 million or 80 per cent)

This project will develop a national scale high-resolution multi-criteria vulnerability map and associated coastal zone management plan for the Coastal Zone Management Plan with consultation with relevant stakeholders, including the private sector. This output will also support policy development to ensure that Liberia has an enabling environment for climate-resilient coast management. Implementing ICMP in the MMA, and across Liberia, will transform the existing coastal management paradigm and reduce the vulnerability of coastal communities to extreme weather events such as hurricanes, floods, and increased incidence of extreme weather events.

Output 3: Climate change adaptation through gender- and climate-sensitive livelihoods to build local climate resilience in Manzan (total cost: USD 2.2 million; GCF cost: USD 5.7 million or 30 per cent)


---
**Page 106**
---

12. Under this output, the proposed project will put measures in place to reduce the anthropogenic pressure on mangrove ecosystems in the Mersaoud Wetland. By reducing this pressure, the health of the ecosystems will be improved and their resilience to sea-level rise and the impacts of climate change will increase. This will contribute to the long-term sustainability of communities in Monrovia that rely on these ecosystems for their livelihoods. In addition, reducing the degradation of mangroves will increase the likelihood of their long-term survival and adaptation to climate change.
13. In addition, urban encroachment into the mangrove forests and the harvesting of mangroves for firewood and charcoal are contributing factors in the rapid degradation of the mangrove ecosystems in Monrovia. In order to develop long-term resilience within vulnerable, mangrove-dependent communities there is a need (i) to support community conservation efforts that will help to reduce anthropogenic pressure on mangroves in the face of observed and expected climate change impacts; and (ii) develop local climate resilient livelihoods for these communities by supporting alternative enterprises engaged in these activities. These community-level approaches to adaptation will benefit from being integrated with the long-term adaptation planning developed under Output 2, to support transformative change at scale.
Output 4: Mangrove conservation (total cost): USD 1.2 million; co-financing: USD 0.8 million or 75 per cent
Total project management costs (PMCs) amount to USD 1.2 million, to be financed by GCF and UNDP. The total PMCs and the split between GCF and co-financers comply with the GCF Policy on Fees for Accredited Entities.
### Assessment of performance against investment criteria

#### 3.1 Impact potential
Scale: Medium to high
15. Following a feasibility study funded through the GCF Readiness facility, the proposal has established that the projections based on the Representative Concentration Pathways (RCP) for both RCP2.6 and RCP8.5 show significant impacts on Liberia's coastlines over a range of climate change scenarios. These impacts are expected to have adverse effects on communities, their livelihoods and the broader coastal infrastructure in the form of: (i) degradation of the mangrove ecosystems on which their livelihoods and food security depend and (ii) increased vulnerability to sea-level rise and associated erosion, flooding and socio-economic spaces and amenities such as fish markets.
16. Historically, the coastal zone around the city of Monrovia has become highly vulnerable to climate-induced coastline erosion and shoreline retreat as a result of sea level rise and increasing frequency of high-intensity storms and off-shore waves (coastline retreat is estimated at 20-30 meters per year). This vulnerability is expected to increase in these impacts, in addition to over-exploitation of its resources for community livelihoods and asset generation. The coastal zone around Monrovia supports the largest and most important fishery-based livelihoods of 55,000 Monrovians, of which 46 per cent are women.
17. The project expects the outcomes will contribute to the achievement of the GCF objective by contributing to the following outputs: (i) reducing anthropogenic pressure on mangroves; (ii) actively protecting infrastructure against climate change impacts; (iii) improving climate-responsive coastal planning; and (iv) strengthening local livelihood practices and promoting the


---
**Page 107**
---

# 3.2 Paradigm shift potential

## Scale: Medium

Liberia is endowed with significant natural resources but without external support its ability to effectively manage them will remain a challenge. In addition, climate variability and change are expected to exacerbate the existing challenges in managing these resources, especially as climate change gathers pace. A more integrated and comprehensive approach to management of coastal ecosystems (including mangroves) is required that combines Integrated Coastal Zone Management (ICZM) and disaster management strategies and their effective operationalization to manage the scale and intensity of the climate risks described in relation to the impact potentially caused by sea level rise, increased storm frequency and intensity, flooding, heatwave, storms, lighting, that could impact on the lives and livelihoods of the communities.

### 3.2.1 Potential for paradigm shift

The proposed project builds upon a robust coastal vulnerability assessment underpinned by extensive stakeholder consultation. It seeks to:

- (i) Under output 1, protect the coastal communities and infrastructure at West Point through the construction of a seawall and other protective measures.
- (ii) Under output 2, develop and implement an integrated national coastal management framework and coordination for implementation of the national adaptation plan (NAP) through existing institutional structures such as the National Environment Management Agency (NEMA), Ministry of Mines and Energy, Ministry of Finance and Development Planning and the World Bank. This will be achieved by:
    - Strengthening the capacity of NEMA to lead coastal management.
    - Building on the existing knowledge base for scaling up adaptation through cross-sectoral capacity development under output 1.
- (iii) Under output 3, develop a national coastal zone management plan and the integration of ICZM into relevant by-laws and regulations as well as mainstreaming climate change considerations in disaster risk reduction strategies and approaches and (iv) promoting sustainable use of mangrove resources through management and improved financial mechanisms under output 3 to support the local fishery-based livelihoods. This includes training and facilitation for value addition, manufacturing and selling of coconutswood to reduce pressure on the mangrove ecosystems.


---
**Page 108**
---

3.3 Sustainable development potential Scale: High

The proposal seeks to strengthen adaptive measures to help minimize impacts of climate variability and change through awareness and building institutional and community capacity for sustainable coastal management. This will be achieved by installing a series of revetments and beaches, to protect against rising sea level, storm surge and beach erosion.

A cost-benefit analysis was conducted with both protective and adaptive measures has revealed the vulnerability analysis and damage assessment under the most likely scenarios for five sections of the Monrovia coast with a return period of 100 years under RCP2.6 and RCP8.5. The total cost of the proposed revetments is estimated at $3,479,000. This project is projected to prevent 150 m of shoreline retreat by 2050 and 360 m by 2100.

For West Point, the cost of inaction will lead to damage of US$38–40 million by 2100. The intervention will provide a positive impact on the health and food security for about 3,000 fisherfolk and 9,000 inhabitants in Monrovia. The support provided for manufacturing eco-friendly boats (e.g., K-Boats) supported by local communities will reduce the burden on the mangroves.

The proposal is expected to have a positive impact on the economy by reducing coastal erosion and sedimentation, and ultimately improving access to nearby wetland areas.

The proposal presents evidence that the interventions are likely to improve the health and food security of the affected communities. The proposed revetments will also provide that the intervention may save some residential areas in the area from inundation and erosion.

The proposal also indicates that it will provide the affected communities with additional education and training opportunities, however, it does not indicate that these will necessarily create new employment benefits within the proposal's scope or target area. While this is possible it is not specifically indicated.

There is expected to be an increase in physical infrastructure as a result of the proposal. However, there are no specific details provided on how this will be achieved and who will benefit from the fisher folk who use the beach at West Point that during and following the installation of the revetment there will always be sufficient room for them to launch and retrieve their boats and clear and clean access.

There are clear efforts to ensure equitable gender participation in the contribution to the expected benefits. However, it is not clear how this will be achieved or what role women will play in the construction phase of the revetment but this is likely to be much higher than the current level of female participation in the earthmoving and (heavy) construction industries in Liberia.

### Needs of the recipient Scale: High

Liberia is classified as a least developed country (LDC) which is vulnerable to climate change ranking fourth in the 2017 Climate Change Vulnerability Index. It is particularly vulnerable due to its low adaptive capacity, and impacts of a number of climate change scenarios have been well presented in the proposal.


---
**Page 109**
---

3.5 Country ownership

The proposal will work with the nationally-determined contribution, national adaptation plans, Sustainable Development Goals, Sendai Framework for Action, National Disaster Management Strategy and related national development plans.

The Government of Liberia has a long history of responding to climate change. The Government of Liberia considers the management of coastal erosion and the impacts of extreme climate events to be a high priority. They have developed several national policies and plans in this regard and demonstrated their commitment by allocating resources for the implementation of these policies. This proposal will build on that experience.

The Climate Change Response Strategy on Climate Change (2018) is the central policy underpinning Liberia's response to climate change and serves as the basis for all future adaptation and mitigation planning. It identifies the sectors most vulnerable to climate change impacts and outlines strategies for addressing these vulnerabilities. The proposal will contribute to four of these pillars, specifically cross-sectoral coordination, capacity building, institutional strengthening, and public awareness.

UNDP is the accredited entity of the project and will provide a qualified team of national and international experts equipped to assist the Government to respond to complex issues such as climate change adaptation. UNDP has extensive experience in this area and will ensure that the project adopts a comprehensive, multi-sectoral approach that is centred around results-based activities and that includes capacity building for key government officials and other relevant decision-makers, and that UNDP has a proven track record as an impartial provider of technical advice and support.

3.6 Efficiency and effectiveness

The project seeks to address an urgent adaptation need in Monrovia by the construction of a rock revetment wall on the West Point coast. The proposed solution is expected to be a preferred solution compared to beach nourishment with granite due to lower maintenance costs and environmental impacts. The project will construct approximately 1,505 metres of seawall to stabilise coastline and enhance resilience of the livelihoods and homes of over 23,000 members of Monrovia's population at a cost of USD 104 per beneficiary.

Applying the UNDP internal rate of return (IRR) methodology, it is estimated to be USD 17.73 million based on a 10 per cent discount rate, and an economic internal rate of return of 21 per cent. The co-financing ratio of about 38 per cent shows a strong commitment of the government despite the challenging financial situation.

The project incorporates best practices and lessons learned from relevant ongoing projects in Liberia that are focused on coastal erosion management and restoration of degraded land and ecosystems in the coastal zone - particularly two Global Environment Facility (GEF)-funded projects in Monrovia. The Government of Liberia has identified the material for revenue construction, including gravel, concrete, and other materials required for construction and protection. The Government's commitment improved the co-financing ratio from initial design and strengthened the project's financial sustainability.

The project will be implemented by UNDP with technical assistance provided by the Ministry of Works and Community Development. These efforts by the Government will enable the remaining four sites identified and extensively

### Assessment of consistency with GCF safeguards and policies

#### Environmental and social safeguards

##### 4.1 Environmental and social safeguards category and safeguard instruments.

The project aims to provide adaptive interventions such as institutional development, capacity building, awareness raising, environmental management planning, support for regulations enforcement; and protective interventions such as the construction of a seawall along the West Point coast and other coastal protection measures in vulnerable section of the coastline from climate change induced erosion in the coastal area of Monrovia. The project interventions will generally target the Mesurado River and St Paul River estuaries and marine areas, including the mangrove forest and its associated biodiversity.

The AIE has prepared an Environmental and Social Assessment Report (ESAR) and a Stakeholder Engagement Plan (SEP). The ESAR provides a detailed description of the environmental profile of the project area, the country's regulations relevant to assessing and managing environmental and social risks and impacts of the project activities, and a risk mitigation/management plan.

The AIE has assigned an environmental and social risk category (ES Risk) of Category B for the project. Category B is defined as having significant environmental or social risks. The ESAR confirms that the project's ES Risk category is within the level by which the AIE is accredited to manage.

##### Compliance with GCF’s environmental and social safeguards (ESS) standards

The project will be managed in accordance with Liberia’s Environmental Protection and Management Law (EPML). The ESAR recommends a number of additional surveys and detailed plans as part of the ESS, including a Reclamation Plan, an Environmental Impact Assessment (EIA), and a Social Impact Assessment. Additional information, including updated ESMPs and sub-plans based on these additional data and information.

##### Based on the ESAR review

The key environmental and social risks of the project include: (i) potential for land acquisition and involuntary relocation of residential structures along the alignment of the seawall; (ii) potential for loss of livelihoods among coastal communities, particularly fishing communities along the West Point coast and the small-scale/subsistence salt extractors; (iii)

construction-related occupational and community health and safety risks, including potential labor management issues, and [vi] possible unintended long-term adverse impacts to adjacent unprotected ecosystems and the estuarine due to likely changes in coastline dynamics, including erosion, sedimentation, and increased turbidity, which may affect water quality and sediment distribution. The detailed ESS process that will be conducted will revisit these risks and propose site-specific management measures to avoid and/or mitigate these risks.

### 4.3.2 Project Organization and Staffing

The project organization and staffing for the ESMM will require hiring of staff and engagement of contractors. The ESAS has projected about seven expanded positions, including a safety officer, to manage the increased number of local workers. The project and contractor's staff and workers will potentially be exposed to occupational health and safety (OH&S) hazards at construction sites, quarry sites and construction areas. The ESAS has identified these risks as part of its OH&S risk assessment and included unaddressed ordinance [10] in the area. There is also potential risk of worker discrimination including gender-based violence.

### 4.3.3 Occupational Health and Safety Management

The ESMM in the ESAS has already mandated strict compliance with OSH standards at construction sites and cities for the presence of UDI prior to any civil works. Project control over OH&S will be achieved through a combination of measures, including commitment to equal opportunity hiring, social inclusion, and adherence to the prohibition of child labour in accordance with ILO Convention 182. The ESAS has also identified additional OSH compliance measures that will be fleshed out in more details in the ESSA. The ESAS will further assess OH&S compliance at construction sites and cities for the presence of UDI prior to any civil works, including special labour contract arrangements that might violate labour standards and update the ESMM accordingly.

### 4.3.4 Resource Efficiency and Pollution Prevention

The risk of release of air and water pollutants, and hazardous wastes to the environment is assessed as negligible and limited to construction activities at the quarry site only. The project will also generate dust and noise may also be generated at the quarry, batching plant, hauling routes and construction sites. To mitigate these risks, the ESAS has recommended that appropriate dust suppression measures and hauling arrangements. The project may also cause short-term erosion and sedimentation of water bodies, including the beach during construction. In the long term, the project will have a positive impact on the environment by reducing the use of fossil fuels. The ESAS has recognized this risk and has indicated that additional investigation and research will be conducted to assess the potential environmental impacts which may need to be addressed.

### 4.3.5 Community Health, Safety and Security

The project will expose the local communities to health and safety hazards during the construction period. There is also the risk of exposure to noise pollution from the quarry site and construction sites. Conversely, there is a risk of spreading communicable diseases may be exacerbated by the crowded conditions at the construction sites. The project may also cause short-term erosion and sedimentation of water bodies, including the beach during construction. In the long term, the project will have a positive impact on the environment by reducing the use of fossil fuels. The ESAS has recognized this risk and has indicated that additional investigation and research will be conducted to assess the potential environmental impacts which may need to be addressed.

### 4.3.6 Environmental Safety Management Plan (ESMP)

The ESMP of the ESAB advocates for "strong adherence to standard construction site processes of work segregation from public access, traffic safety measures, and standard health and safety practices." The ESAS has recommended that these practices should be followed as a matter of standard good practice. Due to the situation in the area, the project should ensure that contractors and workers are provided with adequate training on the ESMP and site-specific risk which would include community safety management measures, installation of proper lighting and warning signs, construction traffic management, among others, as well as require compliance with the relevant local laws and regulations. The project management team should also adopt a simple disease surveillance protocol for the community and workers.

A further safety risk relates to the possibility of . The project should be organized in such a way as to ensure equal access for all residents to adequate services and care for equal treatment of groups, and should respond immediately for any perceived unfairness in the allocation of benefits and the social and economic costs burden between groups.

### ESS 3: Livelihoods

The livelihoods of the residents will be affected by a range of physical displacement (i.e. loss of dwellings) and economic displacement (i.e. loss of productive assets and entire livelihoods). The proposed design for the revetment wall with promenade, including its access road and quarry site can potentially affect the assets and livelihood of the current occupants of the area. All indicated that "the design of the revetment wall with promenade is not appropriate" and that "a more sustainable alternative with physical relocation of communities (housing) is required."

### ESS 4: Social Cohesion

The social cohesion of the residents in the area since the revetment and promenade may display small informal businesses. The main livelihood of the residents in the West Point is also fishing which requires immediate access to gently sloping beaches to allow for easy launching and retrieval of boats. The proposed design for the revetment wall with promenade will constitute a barrier between the settlements and the beach. Moreover, the promenade will be located on the landward side of the existing recreational area. To address this, the design provides for two beach fish landing sites, one in the main settlement and another at the end of the promenade. The project team explained that "early construction of new canor storage will be required to enable fishers to relocate their boats from the current location on the shoreline to a more secure location near the shoreline." Close liaison with community and traffic management during construction will be required. To maintain access for vehicles, the design also provides a total width of m for a promenade along the top of the revetment is accounted for in the design.

### ESS 5: Environmental Sustainability

A detailed assessment should be made to evaluate the inevitable revetment impacts of the revetment wall, access road and quarry site. Based on their actual alignments should be made and once detailed engineering designs become available. A relocation action plan (RAP) will also need to be developed for the affected residents. The potential impact on businesses and livelihood should this be identified in the assessment. The potential impacts of project construction and operation on the environment, including biodiversity conservation through alternative stakeholder consultations for possible mitigation measures such as finding of alternative locations for quarry sites, should be considered. A RAP will also need to include financing support, among others. The ESAS indicates that the RAP will be prepared and implemented in close consultation with affected residents.

### ESS 6: Biodiversity conservation and sustainable management of living natural resources

The project clearly aims to conserve the marine and estuarine ecosystems as a means of sustaining livelihoods for the local population, particularly those who depend on the marine ecosystem and estuary most especially the Mesuralo Wetland. Among the project interventions are the establishment of a protected area in the Mesuralo Wetland, implementation regulations on improved urban waste management, regulations to stop encroachment into the coastal mangroves, fish harvesting, fishing methods such as catch regulations for endangered marine fish species, and protection of the short finned pilot whale and other cetaceans and marine mammals such as the sea whale, the blue whale and the Atlantic humpback dolphin. However, it is important to note that the project should not interfere with the management of marine and estuarine ecosystems since these should not be managed well. The revetment wall for instance will have long-term unintended consequences for the morphology and ecosystem of wetland area. The access road and quarry site may also cause direct damage to water mixing. These may have long term unintended consequences for the morphology and ecosystem of wetland area.


---
**Page 113**
---

natural habitat if not properly located. Any unintended adverse impacts should be minimized or managed.

### Direct impacts of the proposed revetment wall to the Mesurado Wetland Ecosystem are not expected to be significant, if any. However, impacts on specific areas of the coast, such as the index of biodiversity and the potential for habitat loss, should be avoided by ensuring that the project is carefully designed according to the prevailing dynamic processes. To further gain knowledge of the ecosystem dynamics, a baseline study will be conducted in the Mesurado Wetland Ecosystem under the project as a basis for interventions. Biological surveys must be conducted in the potential disturbance areas and the results used to inform the design of the revetment wall. Areas with high aquatic sites, and areas of high biological diversity or diversity must be avoided. The design of the project's interventions must take into account any early coastal process reactions that will result from construction activities on the coast wall. Guidance for the protection of threatened species should also be agreed with the fishermen. The ESMP, which will be finalized after the EIA has been completed and approved by the Ministry of Environment, Forestry and Climate Change, will be developed in consultation with stakeholders using well-established scientific methodologies.

### GCF Indigenous Peoples Policy and ESSE 7: Indigenous Peoples. Liberia has a multi-ethnic population. If the project site is located, the AEI screen identified that there are no group or groups that are identified as "indigenous" based on the definition of the acculturation index (AI) of 0.5 or less. However, the ES should consider the indigenous people trace their origins elsewhere: the Krua are originally from Maryland and Stone County, the East from Liberia, and the West from Sierra Leone. The Krua have been regarded as a minority, but they are integrating rapidly through proximity and intermarriage with other ethnic groups in the region. The Krua who live on the coast living in the affected community is socially excluded by factors such as tribal origin, language spoken, historical or cultural background. Nevertheless, the project should ensure an approach inclusive of all groups. The ESMM will include a Free Prior and Informed Consent (FPIC) procedures in the event of indigenous peoples being encountered in a project of the project-affected area. The ES should also consider the need to consult with the Krua community during the conduct of the environmental and social assessment (ESA). The consultations shall continue throughout the project implementation phase, including the monitoring stage with the SEP. The ESAA should further assess the impacts and benefits of the project to each of these groups. The ES should identify those various groups and make sure that they will be represented during consultations.

### ESSE 8: Cultural heritage. There are a number of historically important sites and landmarks in Liberia, including the remains of the Portuguese fort at Cape Palmas, where slaves who resettled to Liberia in the early 1800s; the oldest cotton tree; the abandoned Hotel Africa which has been identified as a potential archaeological site; and the Fort St. George itself which a major Portuguese trading post. None of these sites however will be affected by the physical construction of this project. The ESAA does not indicate any cultural heritage site present in West Point coastal section area. The ESMM of the ESAR contains a number of provisions to quickly identify and protect cultural heritage during the project implementation phase, including the requirement for discovery of archaeological artifacts and sites.

### Gender policy

The AE has provided a gender assessment and gender action plan and therefore complies with the requirements of the gender policy of the fund.

- The Liberian Constitution provides for equal treatment in the pursuit of gender equality in Liberia.
- Liberia has a relatively strong legal and regulatory framework to support the pursuit of gender equality. It is party to the Convention on the Elimination of All Forms of Discrimination Against Women (CEDAW) and the Protocol to the African Charter on Human and Peoples' Rights on the Rights of Women in Africa, which prohibits discrimination against women.
- The Constitution grants equal ownership rights to land and non-land assets to both men and women. However, customary law prevails under customary law, women can only access land through their husbands and are unable to inherit or sell it without their husband's consent.
- In the case of divorce, women have no legal right to alimony or maintenance payments. Women who engage in governance of the state, several efforts to pass a bill mandating legislative quotas have not yet been successful due to lack of political will and opposition from some political parties and civil society groups.
- Under customary law, women can only access land through their husbands and are unable to inherit or sell it without his consent. Women also face discrimination in accessing credit due to low literacy rates and limited access to financial services such as banks and microfinance institutions.
- Gender inequality in Liberia varies according to age, status, means of income, country, rural/urban location, and ethnicity. These inequalities continue to impact women's livelihoods, health, education, labor market participation, political representation, public life, land property and justice are more limited for women. These inequalities contribute to women's lack of access to care on women, maintain unequal power relations in the household and economic disempowerment.
- Women have limited access to basic services such as healthcare, education, water and sanitation, housing, transport, and communication services. They also face barriers in accessing justice due to their low participation in decision-making processes.

Despite the enabling environment, the assessment indicates that though the Constitution grants equal ownership rights to land and non-land assets to both men and women, under customary law, women can only access land through their husbands and are unable to inherit or sell it without his consent. Women also face discrimination in accessing credit due to low literacy rates and limited access to financial services such as banks and microfinance institutions.


---
**Page 115**
---

### Making gender spheres and institutions

The situation for adolescent girls is critical, with high school dropout due to poverty, teenage pregnancies and socio-cultural norms. Rates of sexual and gender-based violence, harmful practices, female genital mutilation, child marriage and teenage pregnancy are alarmingly high in the region. Members of the LGBTIQ community have limited access to basic rights, services, social freedom, social justice, social economic empowerment and access to health services, and suffer from denial of their human rights.

### Gender action plan

The gender action plan proposed by the AU fulfills the requirement of the gender policy. It is in line with the AU’s strategic framework for gender equality, which sets out clear timelines and budgets. The gender action plan proposed by the AU considers some of the key barriers such as limited access to credit, raw materials, training, marketing, etc., absence of an enabling environment, lack of awareness on women’s rights, inadequate access to information resources; limited access to decision-making positions in public service; limited participation in most private sector activities; gender stereotypes and biases that continue to prevent equal access to control over productive assets.

### The AU will hire a full-time gender expert

The AU will hire a full-time gender expert to mainstream gender throughout the project, and gender expert consultants for specific activities where necessary. At least 20 per cent of project resources are allocated towards gender equality and women’s empowerment in planning and implementation. The Project Steering Committee and other management bodies will be composed of at least one-third women. A Gender Working Group will be established to ensure women have easy access to report grievances and incidents of gender-based violence. Women are targeted as beneficiaries for capacity building, awareness raising on issues such as unequal gender relations, gender disparities, inequalities and promote women’s empowerment. Activities are designed to contribute to changing norms and behaviour through a number of area specific activities including: providing training in the use of appropriate services and technologies for climate-smart livelihoods; and improving coordination and institutional capacity at all levels. In addition to these activities, the project will also provide support to engage women in the design of the coastal defence structures and provide training on gender equality and women’s empowerment. As part of this, a specific module on support equal access to employment, such as provision of childcare, will be implemented. The project will also provide training for women on climate change adaptation through Coastal Management Plan and the Training of Trainer’s curriculum which will include social and gender dimensions in coastal management planning and implementation at the Coastal Zone Management. Activities are designed to ensure women are trained in monitoring of spatial and biophysical coastal information, and that gendered messaging is included in awareness-raising programmes. The project will also provide training for women on business measures to ensure that women have equal access to a community education and innovation centre that will be established at the project site. In addition, the project will be linked to develop training and to provide capital for women to establish small-scale manufacturing of coastal defence structures. As part of this, the project will purchase and install cold storage facilities near fish processing sites to support fishermen, of which 60 per cent are women.

### UNDP confirmed that it has risk-based due diligence requirements for partners and private entities that it intends to engage with. When developing a new project, UNDP ensures that risks are identified and assessed at an early stage in order to determine whether adequate and effective measures to prevent, monitor and address such risks are put in place.

### UNDP advised that a Partner Capacity Assessment (PCA) was undertaken using UNDP’s PCA Tool to assess the capacity of the implementing partner to manage project implementation activities, which is the largest cost item for this project. The overall risk level assessed for this project is moderate.

The result of the assessment recommended UNDP to continue working with the Environmental Protection Agency (EPA) as an implementing partner (UNDP terminology equivalent to a “partner” in the PCA Tool). The PCA included a detailed environmental assessment component for projects involving construction. The result of the construction assessment in the PCA rated as moderate, which is consistent with the overall risk level that has been put in place in project design to keep the risks low. These provisions are that EPA will technical assistance and training on environmental management practices provided by qualified government ministries (Ministry of Mines and Energy and the Ministry of Public Works) to ensure compliance.

The PCA is used in conjunction with the Harmonized Approach to Cash Transfer (HACT) Framework. UNDP’s HACT provides a common operational (harmonized) framework for transferring cash assistance to vulnerable populations. The HACT framework ensures alignment of development aid with national priorities and to strengthen national capacities for management of cash transfers. It includes a harmonized approach to project design, national implementation (NIM) audit, spot checks, and programmatic visits. The micro assessment and spot checks are undertaken by an independent third-party technical consultant.

While the HACT framework requires a macro-level assessment every four years, project-specific assessment and checks are more frequent (regular spot checks are carried out at least once per year). UNDP has conducted two such assessments to date for this GSP project. UNDP has undertaken a HACT micro-assessment of the EPA to assess the risks associated with the entity as an implementing partner/exiting entity in Liberia.

The PCA and HACT micro-assessments cover the following areas: managing partner’s programme, financial and operations management policies, procedures, systems, and internal controls. It includes:

- Reviewing the implementing partner's legal status, governance structures, and financial viability;
- Programme management, organizational structure and staffing, accounting policies and procedures, fixed assets and institutional reporting, monitoring, and procurement; and
- Compliance with policies, procedures, regulations and institutional arrangements that are relevant to the Government and the implementing partner.

The risk areas covered by the assessment include programme management, organizational structure and staffing, accounting policies and procedures, fixed assets and institutional reporting, monitoring, and procurement. The overall rating is “moderate” across all the risk factors.

In addition to UNDP’s PCA micro-assessment, and in part of this project, the UNDP Country Office will undertake the following actions to detect and address any issue related to risks identified in the project:

## Legal assessment

*(Term Sheet for the proposed project has not yet been reviewed nor agreed.*

In accordance with Clause 6.01 of the accreditation master agreement, all funding proposals submitted by GCF accredited entities must be accompanied by a term sheet that sets out the key terms and conditions relating to the proposed fund activity. This is a preliminary legal document subject to revision based on the results of due diligence.

The Accreditation Master Agreement (the "AMA"), was signed with the accredited entity on 5 August 2016 and became effective from that date.

The accredited entity has provided a legal opinion/certificate confirming that it has obtained all internal approvals and it has the capacity and authority to implement the project.

The proposed project will be implemented in the Republic of Liberia, a country in which GCF is not protected against litigation or expropriation. As such, the Republic of Liberia and GCF are not protected against litigation or expropriation in this country, which risks need to be further assessed by the Board. The Board may require additional information for the negotiation of an agreement on privileges and immunities.

The Heads of the Independent Redress Mechanism (IRM) and Independent Integrity Unit (IIU) have been informed that GCF is not responsible to undertake their redress activities and/or investigations, as appropriate, in countries where GCF is not provided with relevant protection against litigation or expropriation. Any claims or complaints by GCF are made only after GCF has obtained satisfactory protection against litigation and expropriation in the country concerned, which may be provided with appropriate privileges and immunities.

In order to mitigate risk, it is recommended that approval by the Board is made subject to the following conditions:

Project/programme size: Small


## Assessment of the independent Technical Advisory Panel

### 1.1 Impact potential Scale: Medium to high

Libera's capital, Monrovia, is extremely vulnerable to the climate change impact of sea level rise (SLR), which is combining with changes in nearshore wave conditions and sediment flux in the deep water channel. The city has experienced a combination of erosion with additional rapid coastal erosion during extreme storm events, which have become more frequent over time. Monrovia's coastline has retreated by 10 metres since 2005. Coastal retreat has been recorded in five vulnerable hotspots, with the worst damage on the West Point peninsula, where approximately 300 houses were lost and 400 people displaced when the coast retreated by 30 metres. Using a combination of climate and wave models to understand likely future trends, the project proponents indicate anticipated shoreline retreat in these five hotspots will be between 15-20 metres by 2050 (Figure A). Monrovia's coastline at West Point is likely to experience the most rapid acceleration as a result of climate change.

![](image.png)

To adapt to the severe impacts of climate change anticipated on Monrovia's coast, the project seeks to change the current approach from a focus on short-term solutions to long-term integrated planning that is climate risk-informed and involves the public sector, private sector


---
**Page 123**
---

# Adaptation impact

The project is considered medium to high risk. The project will address one of the most urgent adaptation needs in Monrovia by constructing a rock revetment to stop the West Point shoreline from regressing further. (The revetment was selected as it would provide the best protection against coastal erosion and storm surges.) Shoreline nourishment with an associated grove was considered technically feasible, but the sustainability of this option would be limited, with the high level of regular maintenance required not seen as feasible in a context where resources are scarce. The revetment will protect homes, artisanal fishing livelihoods, and infrastructure of approximately 10,800 people in West Point and surrounding communities from coastal erosion, protecting residential property, and securing climate-proof launch sites for fishing boats. The project is expected to directly benefit over 25,000 people (including those living within the high-risk coastal defense and enhanced livelihoods), as well as Tupper Village, Plankton and Fiamah, and Upper Town and Lower Town (through enhanced livelihoods and improved protection of mangrove ecosystems).

The construction of the coastal protection infrastructure will form part of a strategic, cohesive approach to climate change adaptation in Monrovia. The project will use an Integrated Coastal Zone Management (ICZM) approach, creating pathways to scale for adapting to climate change along the country's whole coastline. By addressing the most pressing needs and demonstrating how to protect mangroves and promote fuel-efficient cooking and fish-smoking will help to support the ICZM efforts. The Joint GCF/Government of Liberia investment at West Point will serve as a demonstration project, showcasing best practices in coastal protection infrastructure design, which will be used to leverage future investments in coastal protection infrastructure for the most vulnerable communities along Monrovia's coastline. A comprehensive technical assessment was used to inform the design of the West Point revetment and provide guidance on how to address other climate change adaptation needs. This will help develop local capacity for construction, inspection and maintenance. The independent Technical Advisory Group (TAG) has reviewed the project and concluded that it is a good example contributing to an integrated “green-grey” coastal protection system for the city, and that this concept could be utilized within the ICZM approach and explored further during project implementation.

A possible limitation on the project's impact stems from the difficulty of achieving effective management of mangroves in Monrovia. The project will contribute to a more robust Integrated Coastal Zone Management - both in the urban coastal context of Monrovia with ongoing in-migration and spread of unplanned internal settlements, and in rural coastal contexts where there is a high level of artisanal fishing activity and associated illegal logging activity (a directly anthropogenic contributor to coastal erosion outside the metropolitan area). In response to a question on this, the accredited entity (AE) highlighted the existing land-use

### The Impact of Sea-Level Rise on Monrovia

The impact potential of the project will be clearly demonstrated if the climate rationale is fully articulated in the funding proposal, using more of the material contained in the Feasibility Study and Vulnerability Assessment to convey the complexity of the climate change parameters. This includes conveying the observed trends in sea-level rise and increased frequency of intense storm events—data not just do justice to the complexity of the processes involved, but to the work done by the project proponents in understanding these trends and their implications for Monrovia’s coastal zone.

As exhausted all possibilities for seeking historical data on observed trends in order to validate the use of modelled projections, it is important that the funding proposal clearly articulates this. For these reasons that a condition is presented at the end of the document, involving the development of a summary report by the project proponents. This will provide a clear summary of observed and predicted changes along Monrovia’s coastline in relation to the following climate change parameters: sea-level rise; increased frequency of intense storms; and high-intensity storms, also showing how these parameters are being affected by climate and non-climate factors such as sediment transport and land use. The report will also facilitate the potential replication of this important project in other coastal countries in West Africa experiencing similar climate change vulnerabilities.

The Feasibility Study and Vulnerability Assessment provide a wealth of information about conveying the complexity of the climate change parameters. The funding proposal identifies the primary climate change parameter as sea-level rise, with increased frequency of intense storms being linked to the secondary impact and key hazard of coastal retreat. The Feasibility Study and Vulnerability Assessment, however, indicate a more complex situation—with coastal retreat due not just to sea-level rise but also to sediment transport and land use changes (related to ongoing gradual structural retreat (related to SLR, sediment deficit and “regular” storm erosion), as well as the increased frequency of intense storms. The return period for extreme events (whose return interval has become shorter) is predicted to continue to decrease with ongoing climate change. The funding proposal should therefore clearly articulate how these three interlinked factors causing the underlying gradual coastal retreat could have been explained more fully in the feasibility proposal.

A. A habitat vulnerability profile due to sea-level rise (Basis Effect).

(1) Coastal erosion due to sediment deficit caused by longshore sediment transport and sediment exchange with rivers and estuaries; and

(2) Storm surge.

Paragraph 12 of the funding proposal does indicate that the Feasibility Study undertook modelling of the impact of SLR, waves and sediment transport under future climate conditions. The volume of sediment available for longshore transport is likely to be affected by climate change on wave conditions, run-off and temperature, and portrays a fairly complex picture, as conveyed in the Executive Summary of the Feasibility Study and Vulnerability Assessment funding proposal, for example stating in the Executive Summary that “Libertia’s capital city, Monrovia, is extremely vulnerable to the climate change impacts of sea-level rise (SLR) and the increasing frequency of intense storms. ## Paradigm shift potential: Scale: Medium

### 1.2 Innovation

#### 1.2.1 Innovation

The paradigm shift involved in the proposed project is rated as medium overall. The project utilizes an approach to coastal zone planning and management which is innovative in that it leverages private sector investment, expertise, and technology. Historically, traditional public sector collaboration has not been employed in the coastal zone, where communities' capacities are often limited by lack of resources, technical skills, and knowledge, and where long-term planning for climate risk management has not been widely undertaken. The project creates a new model that integrates private sector investment with science-informed planning, involving state and community actors, and embarking on the process of private sector engagement in coastal zone management. This is achieved through best practice technology to construct coastal protection works to reduce vulnerability of a priority coastal stretch in the capital city. In response to questions about implementation TAP, the project proposes that the first phase would be taken as a demonstration or learning phase for the "green promenade" to be constructed along the kilometre-long rock revetment, which will be used to demonstrate the technology and its benefits to artisanal fishing communities' socioeconomic needs in the context of evolving climate risks.

#### 1.2.2 Potential for knowledge and learning

The project has a strong orientation to knowledge management and learning. This includes (i) building capacity through training and expertise of technical officials from 10 government institutions, to be trained as trainers for synthesis and analysis of beach profile data; coastal protection works; and the development of systems of coastal protection infrastructure; (ii) establish a system for data collection, analysis, and dissemination relating to coastal dynamics and ecosystems, helping to increase the evidence base to support ICM decision-making processes in the capital city; (iii) develop an information management system to incorporate information on climate change adaptation and ICZM with targeted knowledge products developed and disseminated; and (iv) establish an education and innovation centre to


---
**Page 127**
---

function to promote knowledge generation and learning hub, especially for the manufacturing of energy-efficient technologies. The project will also support sharing of lessons learned and best practice to inform learning and best practice across community, subnational and national level institutions such as the West Africa Biodiversity and Climate Change WA BiCC programme.

### 12.3 Contribution to the creation of an enabling environment

The proposed project will undertake significant work towards creating an enabling environment in relation to coastal sector, though it could do more in relation to the private sector. Component 2 is focused on strengthening public sector capacity for mangrove management and governance that are necessary to undertake long-term, climate resilient planning and implementation. This includes developing a national Integrated Coastal Zone Management Plan and capacitating an ICRM Committee and Cross Sectoral Working Group (CSWG) at the national level. The project will also be improving coordination and policy harmonization among government institutions, and building sector capacity for mangrove management and governance at the subnational and local levels.

The mangrove or management activities under Component 3 will also be embedded in the strategy to create an enabling environment for coastal communities to enable systematic improvements in mangrove conservation efforts. The funding proposal states that there will also be a focus on strengthening public-private partnerships (PPPs) through the development of a national PPP framework, and increasing private sector involvement and support for coastal adaptation in Liberia in future. The livelihoods support work in Component 3, which is currently being taken up under a market-driven approach using demonstration sites, takes as a wider market-driven approach to leverage project resources to catalyse further private sector investments.

The project co-finance from UNDP and Government of Liberia will be used for the establishment of a Community-based Small-scale Marketing working group (CSMG) in each of the three target communities. The CSMG will meet once per year, one to community members on manufacturing and selling coconuts, and one to women's groups on marketing fish. In addition, the project will also support the development of an independent TAP; the design was adjusted to also include a demonstration of a larger scale fuel-efficient stoves in each of the three target communities. This is particularly relevant in Liberian contexts, with potential to be used by emerging women's Collaborative Associations in Liberia.

Similarly, project co-finance will be used for the establishment of a Community-based Small-scale Marketing working group (CSMG) in each of the three target communities. The CSMG will meet once per year, one to community members on manufacturing and selling coconuts, and one to women's groups on marketing fish, with an exit strategy that sees the CSMG continuing to run the facilities post-project by charging a small fee for use. This is particularly relevant in Liberian contexts, where it can be achieved through the annual workshops seems rather ambitious, as one-of events can surely have limited impact on long-term development and capacity to finance, and access to higher value markets for fishmongers.

The emphasis in Component 3 is on the use of project funds to test up-demonstration operations and technologies. However, it does not appear that there has been any form of market value chain analysis that get to the bottom of the barriers to dissemination of these technologies. More than likely, this will be achieved through the demonstration activities and off-take/off investment - contributing to market transformation at scale on the adaptation content. In response to the need for a more comprehensive approach to climate change adaptation, it is important to maximize the potential of Activity 3.3.3: "Undertake periodic assessments of climate resilient livelihood practices and new opportunities to inform the design of new options and determine their uptake or scaling up in the target communities. This will also provide an opportunity for testing of adaptation to climate change. These include technologies related to non-climate drivers of coastal ecosystem changes, such as stimulating the Compressed Dried Earth Blocks (CDEB)

# Contribution to the regulatory framework and policies

## 1.2.4 Contribution to the regulatory framework and policies

The project will enable the development of KCMZ policy in Liberia and assist the country to move from a pilot phase to full implementation of the KCMZ policy, including updating every three years, through the development of two iterations of the Plan during the project lifespan, as well as efforts to achieve the harmonization of policies and regulations on KCMZ with government counterparts. The project will also address the independent TAP and the difficulty of achieving effective land use planning and management in the Liberian context, where much development is occurring without adequate planning or management. The proposed Plan will be based on and support the implementation of the Land-Use and Management Framework developed by the Government of Liberia (GoL) with technical assistance from USAID/Mexico and elsewhere. In relation to Component 3, they explained that "embedding participatory ecosystem-based coastal zone management into the existing national policy framework for mangrove conservation and management beyond the project period through the established institutional arrangements in Liberia is critical. Conversely, incorporating lessons from community-based implementation of KCMZ into a dynamic framework will increase the effectiveness and applicability of the KCMZ going forward."

## 1.2.5 Scalability and replicability

The project has potential to leverage additional investment in coastal protection works for adaptation purposes during its project lifetime, enabling replication at other vulnerability hotspots along the coastline of Monrovia and Liberia as a whole. Information generated through development of the Plan will be shared with other countries in the region. The independent TAP sees this as a good start, but notes that additional efforts will be needed by government counterparts to ensure that the KCMZ policy is fully implemented and scaled up and unlock investments in specific coastal protection projects. It is appreciated that a reference has been made to the potential for collaboration with other donors and development partners initiated during project development: "will be consolidated, including between USAID Liberia and USAID/Mexico (through the USAID/USAID Mexico Regional Cooperation Agency). These partnerships and experience will provide a robust platform for the implementation of other projects in the coastal zone." The funding proposal notes that, "Extensive management of mangroves is not possible without strong institutional arrangements. This will strengthen public-private partnerships for coastal management, increasing support for future coastal protection and adaptation initiatives," though potential private sector partners have not yet been identified.

# Sustainable development potential

## 1.3 Sustainable development potential

Scale: Medium to high


---

### Environmental co-benefits

The project’s sustainable development potential overall is rated as medium to high. In terms of environmental benefits, the project is expected to have some benefits in contributing to the protection of ecosystems and biodiversity expected through Component 3. This involves a small reduction in sedimentation in the mangrove forest due to the construction of breakwaters; promoting the manufacture and sale of energy-efficient livelihood products; and making a modest contribution towards the conservation of marine resources by establishing a sustainable fishery management system, thus supporting mangrove functioning in (1) stabilising sediments; (ii) providing breeding habitats for a range of marine and terrestrial species; (iii) improving and maintaining water quality; and (iv) reducing coastal erosion. It is noted that the AE recognises that the mangrove co-management agreements will not include explicit enforcement of legislation to prevent overfishing, but rather will focus on promoting sustainable fishing practices and greater protection of mangroves, as with the successful agreements concluded through Conservation International in other parts of the country. As such, it is noted that the AE describes the goals of this work as deliberately limited in ambition: “to be a pilot for future engagements with communities; to support the development of more complex conservation frameworks (in Figure 2).”

### Social co-benefits

The project interventions to enhance coastal defence infrastructure (Component 1) will protect coastal communities, reduce mortality and reduce damage to the dense concentrations of resources that are found in the fishing grounds. This will benefit a wide range of users, which clinics as well as livelihood assets such as boats and fish processing equipment. The coastal defences will also provide protection for other types of infrastructure, thereby avoiding significant social disruption that would occur without the project. The adaptation measures proposed by the TSP will ensure that the fishing grounds are not closed off in any way of life centred around the artisanal fishing trade. At the Independent TSP’s suggestion, the project proponents have made sure that the detailed design of the promenade along the riverment will include space for small-scale fish processing facilities, sheds and huts to accommodate a number of social functions previously utilising the beach.

### Economic co-benefits

Through the coastal protection defence interventions to be implemented at West Point, the project will reduce the long-term costs of economic and social assets that would otherwise be damaged by erosion and storm surges. In addition to this direct benefit, several economic co- benefits are expected through Component 3. These include: (i) improved livelihoods for the protection of climate-resilient livelihoods (Component 3). Improved management of mangroves in the Mearuara Basin will benefit the fishing sector economically, since these ecosystems act as critical breeding grounds and nursery areas for fish species. Economic benefits are also likely to be generated in the form of short- and long-term work opportunities for the construction operations involved in the project; (ii) improved livelihoods through Component 2, as well as through the Community Stewardship Committee (CSC). Installing cold storage facilities in fishing communities will provide economic benefits by increasing the value of fish sold to markets. The establishment of fishery-based livelihoods without increasing demand on fish stocks. Furthermore, establishing facilities for the manufacture and sale of energy-efficient livelihood products will provide income-generation opportunities for community members.

### Gender-sensitive development impact

Clear efforts have been made to promote gender-responsive livelihood development in the project design, both by providing direct opportunities for women in waged labour – as part


---
**Page 130**
---

of the construction of the West Point rock revetment - and by supporting livelihood enhancements for female fishmongers and other women. In response to a suggestion from the independent TAP, to enhance not only women's access to fuel-efficient stoves (for household sites) but also their participation in the project, the Government has agreed to introduce these replace open fires, reduce the fuelwood collection burden, and take pressure off mangroves and forests. The introduction of these stoves is part of a wider strategy for improving energy efficiency by introducing larger scale fuel-efficient kilns (such as the Food and Agriculture Organization's Thalassery model) in order to improve the quality of fish products and increase their market value. Such technology could also be introduced as a pilot technique for a pilot women's cooperative structure supported through the education and innovation centre, if the AE and Government wished to do so.

25. In response to questions from the independent TAP and the Secretariat about whether it is realistic to have a target of 30% female participation in the construction projects on the revetment site, in accordance with the Panel's position that "The 40% increase in women in the wage labour contracted under Activity List A2 is recommended as part of the Gender Action Plan based on the experience gained from the New Kru Town project", it should be noted that there are now two women in Liberia in roles some in the construction industry (for example, civil engineer and architect). The Government has also appointed a female Project Manager. Similar projects, like the construction of the revetment at New Kru Town, have successfully employed a quota system to ensure that 30% or more of the labour force is women. This is part of the project to provide equal opportunities to women under this activity as it is a direct income-earning and skills-building opportunity for women in Liberia who are currently unable to work on construction projects.

Examples of non-engineering wage labour activities these women can participate in – as stated in the Gender Action Plan – include supervision, security, unskilled labour and catering. The Government has also taken steps to address gender norms that prevent women from engaging in manual labour, and similar occupations."

### 1.4 Needs of the recipient

**Scope: High**

#### 1.4.1 Vulnerability of the country, vulnerable groups and gender aspects

26. The needs of the recipient for the proposed project are rated as high. Liberia's population is extremely vulnerable to the impacts of climate change, ranking 4th in the 2017 Climate Change Vulnerability Index (CCVI). This vulnerability is due to increasing exposure to sea-level rise and extreme weather events along its 568 km coastline. The predicted impact of climate change on coastal communities will be exacerbated by the high population density and associated concentrations of economic and social assets in the low-lying coastal areas, as well as the dependence of the population on climate-sensitive livelihoods. Liberia ranks fifth in the world for vulnerability to sea-level rise, with 10% of its total land area at risk from flooding, majority concentrated in the capital city of Monrovia. The target site for coral protection works, West Point, is located within the coastal wetlands and mangrove forests of the Grand Bassa Wetland, making it one of the most vulnerable communities to shoreline erosion, which advances at a rate of 10-25 m per year in some areas. This area is home to approximately 18,000 people's homes, livelihoods and infrastructure directly at risk over time. This includes 49% per cent of the population are women, including many senior fishmongers whose livelihood depends on fishing and selling what they catch along West Point.

#### 5.2 Economic and social development

27. The growth of Monrovia's population at a 3 per cent per year, coupled with the ongoing shoreline loss, is leading to an increase in the density of the population of West Point and the city more widely, putting considerable pressure on the environment and ecosystems on which

### Alternative of alternative sources of financing

Since 1990, Liberia has been classified as a least developed country (LDC) with a GDP per capita of USD $74 – significantly lower than the LDC average GDP per capita of USD $1,645. According to the United Nations Human Development Index (HDI), Liberia ranks the country 176th out of 189 countries assessed by the UNDP Report 2018. Economic and social gains made by Liberia in the post civil-war period have been undermined by a series of shocks, including the Ebola outbreak and subsequent epidemic between 2014 and 2015. According to the United Nations Secretariat (UN/DESA) World Urbanization Prospects: The 2018 Revision, Liberia is classified as an LDC in terms of the Economic Vulnerability Index; had a Gross National Income index equivalent to only 39 per cent of the average for all LDCs and 54 per cent of the average for all countries figure for LDCs. The country is disproportionately reliant on the agriculture, forestry and fishing sectors, which are highly vulnerable to climate change impacts. In addition, Liberia’s economy results in the economy being extremely vulnerable to global commodity price fluctuations and market shocks. This economic vulnerability in turn compromises the ability of the Government of Liberia (GoL) to provide adequate funding for adaptation measures that can be implemented in a sustainable manner. The necessary emphasis by the Government on basic development needs – including food security, health care, education, infrastructure development, etc., has limited the funding of climate change adaptation interventions to protect vulnerable communities, despite the urgent need for such investments.

### Need for strengthening institutions and implementation capacity

The GoL has established several laws, policies, strategies and institutions that are intended to address the needs of people in Liberia. However, the lives and livelihoods of people in Liberia, but limited technical and financial capacity within most government departments and agencies to effectively implement climate change adaptation measures, including climate risk-informed BCR in particular. Where adaptation measures have been initiated, financial constraints have often prevented their implementation due to: lack of access to technical equipment; lack of training to use and maintain technical equipment; and/or a lack of institutional capacity to manage the equipment. There is also limited technical capacity within government institutions and NGOs at both the national and local levels to effectively use and enhance knowledge management systems as well as to disseminate information on climate change adaptation measures, including those that address climate change. The Environmental Protection Agency (EPA) does, however, have a track record of implementing climate change adaptation projects in Liberia through its sector-specific Technical Committees, including those on climate change as well as marine and coastal
