1. **Country ownership of the proposed project is rated as high overall**. The project has strong alignment with Liberia’s climate change strategies and adaptation priorities, as well as broader national development plans. These include: the National Policy and Response Strategy on Climate Change (2015), the National Adaptation Plan (NAP) for 2016-2030 (approved in June 2023), and the Republic of Liberia Agenda for Transformative Steps Towards Liberia Rising 2018. Through its focus on coastal resilience, sustainable livelihoods, and climate-smart agriculture livelihoods, the project will contribute to the achievement of the objectives of the Liberia National Adaptation Programme of Action (NAPA, 2006), the National Strategic Action Plan for Climate Change (NSAPCC, 2013) and the Climate Change Strategy and Implementation Plan (ccGAP, 2012). In relation to the Paris Agreement, the project will align with one of the three adaptation priorities identified in the Nationally Determined Contribution (NDC), namely the construction of coastal defences to reduce the vulnerability of urban coastal communities.

2. The project was developed following an extensive stakeholder engagement process, beginning with initial institutional and community engagements during the first phase of project development. This phase included a scoping mission, a desk review, and a conceptual feasibility study, identifying broad needs and vulnerabilities within the Monrovia Metropolitan Area (MMA) and West Point communities. The scoping mission identified key issues to be addressed by the project, including coastal erosion, flooding, and access to basic services such as water supply and sanitation. The desk review highlighted the need for a comprehensive approach that addresses both environmental and social factors affecting the MMA and West Point communities, in which the trade-off between coastal protection and changes in access to the beach and sea was discussed in depth. This included consultations to identify a range of potential project sites within the MMA and West Point communities. The scoping mission also identified key stakeholders who would be involved in the project design phase.

3. The detailed design phase will provide a further opportunity for community input on and validation of the proposed solutions, as well as opportunities for public consultation on the development of public space on the promenade.

4. The national executing entity (EE) for the project is the Government of Liberia, represented by the Environmental Protection Agency (EPA). As the GCF national designated authority for Liberia, the EPA has been involved in the submission of several Concept Notes and Requests for Proposals (RFPs), including the current RFP for the West Point Coastal Resilience Project. The project is a key component of the Liberia National Adaptation Plan (NAP) and will be implemented under the African Development Bank approved at B2, and has a strong track record in implementing projects with the support of the Global Environment Facility. The Ministry of Mines and Energy (MME), as a Responsible Party, will enter into a legally binding memorandum of understanding (MoU) with the EE to assist in successfully implementing the project.

### Efficiency and effectiveness

#### Scale: Medium to high

The project’s overall efficiency and effectiveness is rated as medium to high. The effectiveness of the project in achieving its goals by GCF was assessed at 80 per cent. The project has been effective in applying best available practice in coastal engineering, and later innovations in techniques. The selection of the approach was based on detailed analysis of the vulnerability assessment results, including hydrodynamic models and morphological models, with a vulnerability assessment covering the anticipated impacts of sea-level rise and storm surges. The technical design for the project’s main engineering work considered the alternatives and motivated for the revetment arrangement, on the basis of the most recent scientific evidence available at the time of planning and maintenance. The project designs for the recommended rock revetment and promenade, as well as the associated drainage system, were found to be effective in reducing the risk of coastal flooding and unimpeded storm damage causing erosion of sediment into scree-holes.

The project’s cost-effectiveness could be maximized by follow-through on the implementation of additional conservation measures. The project has established new partnerships for financing additional coastal protection measures in vulnerability hotspots, especially mangrove forests. These partnerships will also ensure that an early start is made during project implementation to leverage further investment in the community mangrove conservation agreements, and in small business development for technologies that take pressure off mangroves. This will be achieved through a series of workshops. In response to questions from the independent TAP about engagement of the domestic private sector, GCF has received a letter of commitment from the government of Timor-Leste for private sector participation in consultation with the 10 government institutions. These private sector partners will be targeted as key stakeholders in the development and implementation of climate change adaptation to their businesses.

The total project cost is USD 254 million, including GCF grant financing of USD 98 million (38 per cent) and co-financing of USD 76 million (30 per cent) to contribute to the overall construction at West Point. The remaining USD 80 million (34 per cent of the total project cost) will be borne by the Government of Timor-Leste, with a significant contribution from the World Bank (USD 159 million in grants). The government co-financing is considered to be significant, given that it allows for the construction of the West Point rock revetment. This represents an important contribution of USD 4.5 million worth of rock rubble to be used as a major input to the construction of the West Point rock revetment. GCF has also received a letter of commitment from the government of Timor-Leste that it will provide co-financing in the amount of USD 10 per year for the ongoing OKM at the West Point works, the community education and innovation centre, and the mangrove conservation agreement. The project is expected to generate FGD made in the context of coastal adaptation generating largely public goods, and not generating revenue from private sector participation.

The economic analysis conducted for the project indicates a conservative estimate of the expected net present value (ENPV) of the project at USD 17.3 million, using a 10 per cent discount rate. The ENPV is based on the following assumptions: (i) that the project activities for damages avoided, benefits also include an increase in income growth and the social benefit of the project through improved infrastructure. ### Overall remarks from the independent Technical Advisory Panel

(b) Further strengthens the project's climate rationale by summarizing clearly the observed and predicted changes along Monrovia's coastline in relation to the following parameters: sea level rise, sediment transport and exchange, wave conditions, coastal erosion, and land use. The report should also explain how these factors are being affected by climate and non-climate drivers, and how they interact to produce shoreline regression - both historical and anticipated.

# Introduction

The Green Climate Fund (GCF) recognizes the central importance of gender considerations in terms of both impact and access to climate funding and requires a Gender Assessment and Gender Action Plan to be submitted as part of the project funding proposals that it assesses. The main objective of the Gender Assessment is to provide an overview of the gender situation in Liberia, with particular emphasis on coastal communities within the Monrovia Metropolitan Area (MAMCP). Therefore, this gender assessment aims to provide an overview of the gender situation in Liberia, with a focus on coastal communities and their vulnerability to climate change.

Specifically, the assessment will identify gender issues in Liberia that are relevant to the project and examine how these issues may impact women's access to opportunities. Given the fact that gendered climate vulnerability is at the center of the project, the information and design considerations in this Annex should reflect a gender perspective throughout all stages of the project.

This Gender Assessment is based on the Feasibility Study, Stakeholder Engagement and Environmental and Social Management Framework (ESMF) Annexes.

The assessment was based upon conducting field consultations, gathering information from available data from studies conducted by the Government of Liberia, UNHCR and civil society organizations, donor agencies and multilateral development banks. Lessons learned from past studies and assessments on gender in Liberia were also considered to ensure that all perspectives are engaged, men, women and youth alike. This will help to ensure concerns that will be integrated as the project progresses, including activities identifying women as beneficiaries including as leaders and decision-makers.

Gender mainstreaming in this project aims to be both gender responsive, by integrating a gender perspective into the design of the project, its implementation, monitoring and evaluation; but also gender transformative, in the sense that it deals with root causes of vulnerability and structural barriers to climate resilience, and challenges the norms around the gendered distribution of labor and constraints in relation to access to resources.

The proposed GCF-funded project not only intentionally targets and benefits women, but also considers the intersectional vulnerability: there are some categories that face additional forms of marginalization and stigma due to their gender and other factors such as age (e.g., older adults), youth and children, the elderly, people with disabilities and marginalized groups such as those with HIV/AIDS and LGBTI living in the target areas.

The project design recognizes that SLH and coastal erosion have immediate and long-term impacts on women's access to resources. The gender assessment is based upon information gathered in the current gender analysis. In addition, the project recognizes the effects of changing environmental conditions by supporting climate resilient livelihoods, and the importance of integrating gender into the project through the use of Gender-Sensitive Zone Management (GSZM) and into local value chains, in which women are already playing a growing role.

The Gender Assessment intends to provide additional information on the national and local gender context, particularly regarding women's access to resources, their role in decision-making and the


---
**Page 139**
---

# Annex VIII Gender - Assessment & Action Plan

## GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL

Gendered aspects of local livelihoods. It provides the basis for, and lessons on which, the Gender Action Plan (which is reflective of the overall project design) has been built.

Overall, the project objective is to enhance the climate resilience of the most SLR-impacted MIMA communities that are constantly exposed to storm surges, flooding, and salinity ingress through the installation of climate-resilient livelihoods. This will be achieved by empowering women - and their families - and communities who are rendered the most vulnerable.

Specifically, GCF resources will be used to enable all relevant institutions including the Minister of Gender, Children and Social Protection, the Ministry of Agriculture, Forestry and Food Security, and other concerned women to become leaders in the adoption of climate change resilient livelihoods and the management of the QZM options, ensuring that women are involved in the ownership, operationalization and management of these options. The GCF resources will also be used to provide training for women from concerned institutions. Resources will also be used to promote climate resilient livelihood options and participation in their associated value chains for women whose existing livelihoods are particularly vulnerable to SLR impacts.

The project measurable benefits that will be realized as a result of the GCF investment includes:

- Provision of assistance to communities in the MIMA, with a special focus on women, youth and other vulnerable groups, to pursue climate resilient livelihoods and facilitate their access to associated market linkages, with investment in assets, tools, and training.
- Change in attitudes and perceptions towards gender equality and women empowerment to induce a transformative change within the communities to improve gender relations at the family and community levels.
- Inclusion of gender at all the national, local and community activities of the ICZM, at management and operational levels.
- Strengthened capacity, coordination and knowledge sharing of national institutions and local communities intervening in the project.

## Existing Gender inequality and social exclusion in Liberia

Women play a crucial role in Liberia. Not only do they compose almost half of its population, but they also play an important role in the economy as well as in the household at all levels. The Liberia Revised National Gender Policy (2018-2022) states that "Gender inequality and the marginalization of women in Liberia are perpetuated by socio-cultural perceptions, practices, and stereotypes that support gender roles and norms." Women's socialization is a key factor in perpetuating these inequalities through socialization of girls and boys, social norms and customs, which carries into schools, the community and the workplace.

Gender inequality is not unique to Liberia. It varies according to age, status, means of income, county rural/urban areas and traditions, but it, in general, access to education, healthcare, land, property, and justice is limited to women as compared to men. Gender imbalance causes such as exclusive burden of


---
**Page 140**
---

# Annex VIII Gender - Assessment & Action Plan

## GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL

care on women, literacy unequal power relations in the household, lack of access to basic services, economic disempowerment, low participation in decision making in public spheres and institutions and in the household, lack of access to legal and judicial services, vulnerability to Human Immunodeficiency Virus (HIV), gender-based violence, early marriage, child labor, limited access to education, training, and opportunities; the situation is worse for adolescents girls that are often school dropouts because of poverty and that the issue of teen-pregnancy.

Gender inequality is a major obstacle in achieving sustainable gender indices

Through the years several indices have noticed to quantify the concept of gender inequality. The United Nations Development Programme uses the Gender Inequality Index (GII) and Gender Development Index (GGI). GII is a composite measure that shows inequality in achievement between women and men on three dimensions: reproductive health, empowerment and the participation of women's resources in human development in three areas: health, education, and command over economic resources. The GGI measures gender gap in life expectancy at birth, school enrollment rates, and income. Despite the ratification of the Maputo protocol, its domestication has remained inadequate. With strong political will and commitment to implement the protocol, the situation can be improved.

The Gender Inequality Index (GII) is a composite measure that shows inequality between women and men on three dimensions: reproductive health, empowerment and the participation of women's resources in human development in three areas: health, education, and command over economic resources. The GGI measures gender gap in life expectancy at birth, school enrollment rates, and income. Despite the ratification of the Maputo protocol, its domestication has remained inadequate. With strong political will and commitment to implement the protocol, the situation can be improved.

The Gender Inequality Index (GII) of 0.631 ranks Liberia out of 177 out of 185 countries assessed, a decline from earlier years[1]. Gender gaps are evident in mean years of education (3.5 vs. 8.1), income (US$277 vs US$797) and life expectancy at birth (60.4 vs. 67.5). The GII is calculated as the average of three sub-indices: reproductive health, empowerment and economic resources. Liberia's score on the sub-index of violence against women during the civil wars has been normalized[2]. Almost 14 percent[3] of the population aged 15 years or older have experienced sexual violence in their lifetime. The prevalence level of adult HIV prevalence was at 2.1 percent[4](2.4 percent among women, 1.8 percent among men), indicates underlying policy and health system deficiencies and unequal gender relations.

The Global Gender Gap Index (GGGI) of the World Economic Forum compares the gap between men and women in four areas: education attainment, economic participation and opportunity, educational attainment, health and survival, and political empowerment[5]. Out of 149 countries, Liberia's rank based on GGGI in 2018 is given below:

## Priority

After emerging from years of conflict, the Ebola crisis and the resultant economic downturn[1], Liberia faces many economic challenges. According to the 2018 UNDP/LIBERIA Common Country Assessment[2] these economic challenges are: high inflation, declining global commodity prices, growing foreign debt, and import dependency. These factors have led to a decline in government revenue and reduced capacity for delivery of essential services. Acute poverty and vulnerability in Liberia are a result of entrenched social, and economic inequalities and exclusion – which resulted in years of conflict[3] and continue to permit discrimination against women.

Liberia’s Human Development Index (HDI) has been declining, with the HDI value for 2017 at 0.435, placing Liberia at 131 out of 189 countries.[4] More than half of the country's population[5] live below the poverty line and 60% are unemployed or underemployed. The most recent Human Development Index experienced multi-dimensional poverty – 33.2 percent severely poor while 20.4 percent “vulnerable” to slipping back into poverty.[6]

Weak administrative procedures and limited human and institutional capacities[7] have led to delays in closure, access to justice, especially in cases of human rights violations and sexual and gender-based violence.

Despite the central economic roles of women in households and in the economy in general, women still face some constraints in order to participate effectively in the economic life of the country because of discriminatory social norms and practices and unequal policies, such as:

### Limited access to credits, raw materials, training, marketing, etc.
- Absence of enabling environment to contribute effectively to the economy and limited access to productive resources

It is important to note that women are disproportionately affected by climate change. Women are more disproportionately affected than men. For example, women may be forced to spend savings during climate shock events, and this loss of financial resources can have a long-term impact on their ability to recover from the shock. This is also where women (as customers) tend to collapse. Small businesses predominantly owned by women were largely affected during the economic downturn in Liberia because they were selling perishable food items and many lost their assets.

## Social protection

The main goal of the PAPD is to improve protection of the poorest and most vulnerable population segments from poverty, deprivation, and hunger, and enhanced resilience to risks and shocks through a multi-sector approach. The PAPD aims to reduce vulnerability by providing social safety nets and increase employment readiness of extreme poor, youth, and women through increased opportunities for economic empowerment.

Therefore, the situation in SLR affected communities cannot be ignored. Stakeholder meetings held in MIM from March to April 2019 revealed that with affected women involved in drying and selling fish along the river banks, they are not only vulnerable but also face a lot of challenges such as harassment and women are frustrated at being neglected, particularly given that the erosion caused by climate change rapidly left most of them homeless. While they do not feel protected at home, there are social measures to protect them from further harm.

However, social protection needs for women and men may vary. Women and men face different risks and vulnerabilities, some specific to their gender and others exacerbated by gender inequalities and discriminatory practices. The PAPD recognizes that women in Liberia face certain gender-related constraints, including barriers to women's economic advancement.

In the absence of specific data in relation to gender and social protection, it is clearly understood that women’s financial contribution is more important within household survival strategies. However, because women are disproportionately affected by climate change and have greater risks and vulnerabilities (e.g., health risks, interrupted or incomplete employment), they have either less or little-to-no resources to cope with the shocks and stresses of climate change. Therefore, social security measures are restricted to the small, male-dominated section of the workforce employed in the formal state and private sectors. Social protection is limited for those in the very informal employment sector where women dominate.

### Gender and Employment

Liberia Demographic Health Survey (2013) provides the following figure 53% of women in contrast to 74% of men were employed at the time of the survey. Geographically, by country, women in Gbarpolu (78%) and Bong(72%), were more likely to be employed than women in other counties (80-90%). Lofa (13%), Woinjokri (15%), and Nimba (16%) were the least likely to be employed. Men, on average, are 4 times more likely to be employed than their female counterparts in other countries (58–87%). Women (59%) and men (82%) are equally likely to be employed in rural areas compared with urban areas where women are less likely to be employed (60%) and men (84%) in the lowest quintile are engaged in agricultural activities (LDSH, 2013). The urban and rural differentials show that urban women were concentrated in sales and marketing (60%), construction (57%), and transport and communications (59%). Urban men were more likely to be employed in manufacturing (48%) and agriculture (42%). Women (22%) in urban areas were employed in sales and marketing, in the rural areas, 60 percent of women and 14 percent of men are engaged in agricultural activities.

By category of employment, women are mostly found in sales and services (49%), followed by agriculture (42%), in contrast to men who were more likely to be employed in agriculture (48%), unskilled laborers (37%) and construction (35%). Women are less likely than men to work in the professional categories. 4 per cent of women compared to 10 per cent of men had professional, technical or managerial occupations. According to the LDSH(2013), women and men with no education were more likely to be employed in agriculture (68% for women and 75% for men) than those who have completed secondary school (49% for women and 58% for men).

Education is also a hindrance to women's employment in the formal sector, so men are disproportionately represented. Women with no education were more likely employed in informal sector compared to 30% of working men. Men are more than three times likely to be employed by the civil service, NGOs, international organizations or public corporations, in mining, in banking, and in the transport and communications sectors only in agriculture and fisheries that men and women are employed on an equal basis -1 to 1 ratio.

High-participation of women in the informal sector is a consequence of the care economy as the burden of home and family care imposed upon them through the socio-cultural allocation of gender roles. They are more absent in small and medium enterprises due to limited education, lack of capital, credit and land.

While all men and women in the informal sector face similar problems such as absence of social insurance schemes, low pay, poor working conditions, lack of access to finance, inadequate or unsafe work environment, limited finance and business development services, women are more negatively affected by these factors. Women also face a number of gender-specific challenges due to information gap on migration and customs regulations, making women in cross border trade more vulnerable to exploitation, due to limited knowledge about their rights and the laws that protect them.

The Civil Service Reform Strategy (2008-2011) focused on mainstreaming gender and establishing mechanisms for addressing gender issues. The strategy aimed at eliminating sexual harassment, a nationwide harassment policy, and the establishment of Gender Focus Points (GFPs) in each Ministry Agency, and Commission Corporation (MAC). The Decent Work Law passed in 2016 assured the right to minimum remuneration of women domestic workers. It guarantees entitlement of women and men


---
**Page 144**
---

without distinction, exclusion or preference to receive equal remuneration for work of equal or comparable value**.

It is hoped that the introduction of a new National Employment Policy; the review and revision of the Liberian Labor Code, the transformation of the Liberian Emergency Employment Program (LEEP) into the Liberia Employment Program (LEP); the establishment of a Gender Equality Unit within the MOAS; the launch a Women's Entrepreneurship Program involving MSMMS for business skills development, and provision of access to microfinance and functional literacy will make a big difference in Gender and Employment in Liberia.

## Health and Reproductive Rights

Liberia faces a few health challenges but more specifically gender norms, roles and relations can influence women’s ability to affect the attainment of personal, physical and social well-being. Fortunately, gender equity is a guiding principle of the NHPWP (2011-23) and improving health outcomes among women and girls is critical to the overall goal. The three objectives of the National Health Policy and Program are to improve access to quality health services; promote gender equality and the health status of women and girls; To increase access and utilization of services, especially for women and girls in rural areas and those living with disabilities; and to bring health services closer to where people live. Coupled with investments in strengthening human resources, the target is to increase facility-based deliveries by a skilled birth attendant from 22 to 80 percent.

To make services for women and girls more responsive, the cornerstone of (2011-23) NHPWP is the Essential Package of Health Services (EPHS). The EPHS includes family planning, maternal health linkages to the community and household, as well as general specific health messages. The target is for 90 percent of families in rural areas to have access to at least one facility providing essential reproductive health services with a background of ensuring social protection through a combination of prepayment schemes and free services for priority interventions that directly affect the health of women and girls.

Legislation does not restrict the right of women to access contraception and other reproductive health services. However, many women in Liberia do not have access to these services due to lack of reproductive health services**. Over 25 percent of adolescents age 15-19 have already had a child. Vaccination rates for infants under one year old are low (40%). The maternal mortality ratio is 638 per 100,000 live births; the mortality rates in Liberia are slightly higher for boys than girls. The same is true of infant mortality rates.

High maternal mortality and systemic inequities — especially related to health service access and quality – have a major impact on women and girls. The high maternal mortality rate is linked to a high fertility rate, low levels of education among women, and the low utilization of health facilities for delivery, compounding the risks associated with complications.

Other factors which also contribute to maternal and newborn mortality are delays in diagnosing problems, deciding to seek medical care, accessibility and affordability, inadequate supply of drugs and equipment, poor nutritional status of pregnant women, and high fertility rates. Some women who survive pregnancy complications often suffer from long-term disabilities such as obstetric fistulae or infertility. Teenage mortality is high among girls who have complications during pregnancy and give birth before the age of 15 years. In Liberia, there are no facilities for emergency obstetric care in most areas; there are no medical facilities nor skilled birth attendants to address complications that may arise. Additionally, there are no postpartum services available within a reasonable distance from where women live or work after childbirth and in the postpartum. The national contraceptive prevalence rate for family planning is 20% and less than 14% for adolescent girls aged 15–19 years. The unmet need for family planning has virtually remained the same with no significant progress made over time.

In terms of family planning, there is limited private income to buy it. Access to oral knowledge about Sexual and Reproductive Health (SRH) varies between men and women. According to the LHDs (2013), 91% for men while only 58% for women have access to SRH information. Teenagers of both sexes either are not aware or do not practice birth control methods, even though family planning is free. Some of the reasons given for this include cultural misconceptions about family planning, lack of knowledge and skills on how to use contraceptives, fear of side effects, hesitation on promiscuity by using family planning, religious background that prohibit family planning, affordability and lack of access to SRH services.

If this situation is not improved, women will continue to bear the brunt of continue to be vulnerable to emerging health issues/diseases with the lack of or limited access to social protection programs described above.

The delivery of water and sanitation services in Liberia is a key concern in the affected communities visited during consultations. This process affects women and girls’ dignity, safety and physical and time burden. The majority of households do not have access to safe drinking water as most sources are far away, which affects the well-being of women and girls and the way of life in communities. The unavailability of clean water also leads to poor sanitation practices such as open defecation, public toilets and nonfunctioning public common toilet facilities are of great concern. Community practices open defecation and pollute the beaches. Water and sanitation-related illnesses challenge their health and add a burden on women who have to spend more time in fetching water than men due to economic sectors due to the fact that women and children pay high prices from vendors in MMSA.

Liberia’s coastal communities also face several psychosocial challenges: in addition to economic vulnerability and stress arising from displacement, they are also affected by social isolation caused by enforced emigration. The lack of means of getting that are going to be put in place by local authorities is a burden. This is even worse for single female-headed households. More than men, the affected women also revealed that they have no access to any form of shelter or accommodation and are forced to lodge with friends and relatives in the other communities in proximal areas. The use of improper and unsafe plumbing systems in their homes increases the risk of waterborne diseases such as cholera, typhoid fever, dengue fever, dysentery, diarrhea, hepatitis A, hookworm disease, leishmaniasis, malaria, meningitis, polio, schistosomiasis, tetanus, trachoma, tuberculosis, and typhus. Furthermore, this displacement is connected to teenage pregnancy as well as drug and substance abuse by young men and women which were also rampant phenomenon discussed.

# Annex VIII Gender - Assessment & Action Plan

## GREEN CLIMATE FUNDING PROPOSAL


After years of civil war and the breakout of EVD, Liberia is still in the process of rebuilding its education infrastructure and workforce. The issue of youth education is serious. Based on the Liberia Household Income and Expenditure Survey data collected in 2014, the national literacy rate is estimated to be 56 percent. However, there are significant disparities between urban and rural areas: 78 percent of females living in cities are more likely to be able to read and write than those from rural areas; 76 percent versus 50.1 percent. The gap between males and females is also significant: 92.4 percent of males aged 15 years or older can read, while only 54.8 percent of females know how to read and write. Therefore, women lack the necessary skills and resources that can provide them with more types of opportunities of their male counterparts.

Liberia Demographic Health Survey (LDHS, 2013) reveals that access to education has increased for both girls and boys since 1998. The net primary school enrollment rate in 2011 was 64 percent for girls in 2011, up from 56 percent in 2007 to 66 percent for boys in 2013 (LDHS, 2013). However, there are still significant disparities between urban and rural areas. The variations can be seen by literacy rates by ages and by completion rates, and variations in rural and urban areas. For example, Grand Bassa County has the highest rate of girls who have never been to school (58 percent) while 100 percent of girls in Montserrado County have never attended school. These variations persist even though gender disparities in primary school enrollment have been significantly reduced. The issue of girls dropping out of schools is of concern. Reasons for this include early marriage, poverty, lack of access to education, and cultural norms such as early marriages, poverty, boys to girls' preference to education, as well as gender roles stereotyping in educational settings.

The Government recognizes the need to close this gap and to promote a drastic and rapid increase in school attendance in rural areas, particularly for girls. This situation was alleviated by providing universal free primary education (UPE) under the Education Reform Act of 2011, which came into effect on April 4th.

The Government has also prioritized girls' education through the National Girls' Education Policy to ensure equal access and opportunities in education. The passage of the Liberia Constitution and the Primary Education Law is another important step towards women's and girls' equality in education. The Education Reform Act of 2011 aims to enhance access to education from a gender perspective by increasing the number of schools, providing more teachers, and improving the quality of teaching and learning.

The situation becomes critical at the coastal communities like new Hotel community, where most adult women and girls are illiterate and lack many of the basic skills to meet up with the challenges of life and empower themselves better. Stakeholder meetings and interviews with communities indicated "motherhood" as a major reason for dropping out of school. The high cost of education is also being progressively away the public schools' infrastructures and sea level rise and subsequent flooding are a threat to young kids during the raining seasons.

### Political participation and governance

Despite Liberia producing the first female president in Africa, women continue to face several challenges in political participation including lack of violence and intimidation, hostility in their speaking in public, and a lack of funds to run for office. Participation of women in politics and governance remain

# Annex VIII Gender - Assessment & Action Plan

## GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL

### Local Government

In local government, from general town chiefs to county superintendents, women account for fewer than 6% of leadership positions. In the national legislature, women make up around 11.5%. In the Cabinet, women hold only one ministerial position and in the judiciary, there is just one woman with only five people on the court. In the decision-making structure in the judiciary, such as circuit court judges, women total about 12.7%.

The Local Government Act enacted in 2018 **^** affirms the commitment of the Government of Liberia to further the unity of the Republic by providing equal opportunity for all its citizens to engage in the governance of the state through the devolution of certain administrative, fiscal and political powers and institutions. The Act also provides that women should hold at least 30% of seats on local councils. A mandating legislative quotas have been introduced but are yet not be successful. The Equal Participation in Politics Act (2018) requires that all political parties must reserve a minimum of one-third of the will of political parties to initiate affirmative action measures, this can significantly increase the number of women in elected positions. However, the lack of resources for implementation and enforcement measures fall short of achieving the desired target. The Laws, and related dispensations need to be made explicit and enforceable.

In addition, the Local Government Act includes a gender equity measure which reserves 2 seats in every county council for women. To address the persistent pattern of discrimination against women, gender inequity and marginalization of women in the public sector, the Government should develop and adopt policies at all levels of government and governing agencies to ensure that gender equities, including codes of conduct and non-discrimination policies. Although the increase in women’s representation is critical, there is also needed to develop a robust gender capacity building program for the different actors at national and local levels.

### Decision making

Women's equitable participation and representation in decision-making strengthens commitments to women's empowerment and gender equality. It also makes for better outcomes related to sustainable development and poverty reduction.

The 2013 Liberia Demographic and Health Survey, covering all of Liberia, found that "most currently married women (58.61 percent) reported that each of these household decisions is made jointly with their husbands. Sixteen percent of women reported that they alone make decisions about their health care and 47% said that they alone decide on the use of family planning methods. Thirty-two percent make their own decisions about major household purchases. More specifically, 30 percent of currently married respondents report that they alone decide how much money is spent for food and other expenses are used, while 54 percent say they decided jointly with their husband. Only 15 percent of women generally report that they alone make independent decisions on their earnings. Further, 32

Women in coastal communities are empowered by hardship; as they are not waiting for their partners to attend to domestic care and other family related needs, they are part of the decision in the household. ## Labor force/ Employment and livelihoods

Libertarian employment law prohibits discrimination based on gender[8]. In addition, the "Decent Work Bill" passed into law on June 25, 2016. The bill explicitly excludes discrimination against women in working conditions and provides for equal pay for work of equal value. It also includes a paid maternity leave,[9] and employers are responsible for paying 100 percent of maternity benefits.[10] But these regulations only apply to women working in the formal sector, and according to the 2008 report issued by the International Labour Organization (ILO) and the United Nations Conference on Trade and Development (UNCTAD), 90 percent of women are employed in the informal sector[11]. The World Bank considers that only 47 percent of women can be employed.[12]

As of 2014, 47 percent of the total labor force is female,[13] although not all those who work are included in the labor force including, for example, unpaid workers, family workers, and students. The labor force size also tends to change seasonally.

## Access to resources, land tenure and property rights

According to data from the MICS[14], there are 367 Women Entrepreneurs who are employed in the trade sector. This is a very small number of women having access to the capital or assets necessary to begin to expand their businesses. This is in addition to 5,600 women who are listed as employees in small businesses and 1,500 women involved in agricultural entrepreneurship, according to the 2009 MICS.

Women are the key actors of informal Cross Border Trade (CBT), making up approximately 70 percent of the informal cross border traders. Women operating in cross border trade are operating under unfavorable conditions because exposed to fraudulent customs charges, extortion, discrimination, and at times sexual harassment[15]. They also face difficulties when entering and exiting when crossing the borders.

Title 29 of Liberia's Property Law and Article 12 of the Constitution grant equal ownership rights to land and non-land property, including customary land tenure and customary practice. The Land Rights Policy approved by the Land Commission on May 21, 2013 also states that: "The Constitution gives all Liberians the right to own and inherit land and other property. This means that land and other property is permitted for all Liberians regardless of their identity, whether based on custom, ethnicity, tribe, language or religion." The Land Commission has been working towards the implementation of this policy in every part of the Land Commission's policy recommendation. Furthermore, the Government established a Land Commission in 2009 to address conflict over land sales, secure men and women's land tenure, and modernize the Liberian land law.

While Liberia has a dual legal system, based on written law derived from statutes and case law, and on customary law, customary tenure systems generally prevail. Under customary law, women can only access land through their husbands; they cannot themselves inherit land and any disputes concerning land are resolved in favor of men. It is hoped that women's formal access to and control over farmland will increase via the passage in 2018 of the Land Rights Act which ensures women's access to land in order to increase farming and other livelihoods, especially for women who have been displaced from their homes or whose productive use of land reduces to poverty and vulnerability, especially owing to its recognition of customary communities as owners of their own land which presents a unique opportunity for disadvantaged Liberians. The Land Commission has been working towards the implementation of this policy in every part of its proceedings. Accelerating the process of accession to the provisions of the Act and ensuring the full functionality of the Land Authority will therefore be a major focus under economic governance.

The government sees women as important partners of development, especially in view of their role in the overall management of a household's resources and in accordance with the 2013 Demographic and Health Survey. The DHS indicates that the percentage of women who do not own land

house is highest in Monterrandro (83 percent) out of all the country's counties. By county, the percentage of men who do not own a house is also highest in Monterrandro (98 percent). 14

Women and men have equal rights to access financial services, including credit and bank loans. However, in practice, women often have difficulty accessing credit due to low literacy rates, and/or because they cannot provide collateral for the loan. Women are also more likely than men to be excluded from savings by NGOs and the government and women are the main beneficiaries. However, most women have found a different way of saving money through Savings Groups (SGs). These groups are formed in a traditional form of banking and resource mobilization system widely used in Liberia and mostly by low-income earners, including many who have MSMEs. In these savings clubs, a group of people agree to pay a certain amount each week or month into the club's account. The total amount collected is given to an individual to expand his or her business or to take care of other basic needs, in return for which he or she agrees to repay the loan and interest at a later date. Women involved in petty trade, find daily "Susu" clubs to be an effective way to save towards better opportunities (either expanding business or investment).

## Gender-Based Violence

Rates of sexual and gender-based violence (SGBV), harmful practices (HPs), female genital mutilation (FGM) and access to adequate coverage are all high. While access to sexual and reproductive health rights (SRHR) is low.

Women and girls are the main victims of harmful traditional practices affecting their health, often to the point of permanent physical, psychological and emotional damages, and sometimes even death. These include Female Genital Mutilation (FGM) and Gender-Based Violence (GBV). Health facilities remain insufficiently equipped to deal with GBV cases. The Liberian Ministry of Health and Social Welfare has not yet developed a national plan for the elimination of FGM and HPs. Women affected by these practices are often subjected to different forms of GBV confronted with the challenge of inadequate access to appropriate counseling and psycho-social support. The prevalence of GBV including the harmful traditional practice such as FGM, is a major public health concern on women and girls and it also often a cause and consequence of HIV among women and girls.

The Liberia EUIU Spotlight Initiative® provides a good situational analysis of the SGBV and Harmful Traditional Practices (HTPs) situation in Liberia. However, there are gaps in data collection by related Institutions, Presentation and Social Norms, the current response and the inaccuracy and insufficient data about the nature and the extent of GBV. These are summarized in the in the following sections.

The Liberian Ministry of Health and Social Welfare (MoHSW) has made some progress on VAWG; implementation is weak or non-existent and significant legislative and regulatory gaps remain. For example, the 2015 National Action Plan for Ending Violence Against Women and Girls (NAP-VAWG) failed to ban FGM despite intense debate, and even though then the President issued Executive Order No. 92 prohibiting FGM in January 2018, the practice remains widespread. While the Penal Code criminalizes rape, there is no prohibition of marital rape. In addition, the sexual and

There is also an urgent need to address the structural gaps in laws and policies through legislative reforms, amendments and repeal of obsolete laws. There is also a need for expeditious enactment of pending legislation on domestic violence, child marriage, and other related issues including sexual rights. Some of the major ongoing legal and policy interventions include the anticipated amendment and implementation of the Family Code, which will replace the current Family Law Act 2014, as well as the Public Health Law and National Family Planning Strategy and the Reproductive, Maternal, Newborn, Child and Adolescent Health Policy; and planned revision of the 2011 National Youth Policy. In addition to the above, there is a need for strengthening the capacity of law enforcement agencies under the Public Health Law and National Family Planning Strategy and the Reproductive, Maternal, Newborn, Child and Adolescent Health Policy; and planned revision of the 2011 National Youth Policy.

The lack of trust in government structures and the public sector to provide or resolve formal justice systems, especially when the perpetrator is a family member of the victim or is a relative, guardian, parent or other member of the community.

Current institutional capacities and mechanisms are significantly weak due to lack of efficient implementation of laws and policies. There is also an urgent need for planning, implementing and monitoring interventions to prevent and respond to SGBV, HIV and SRHR-related issues at both national and subnational levels. The most significant challenge in addressing these issues is the lack of a comprehensive framework to meet the gender-based needs of women and men, boys and girls. As a result, there is considerable public mistrust in the delivery of appropriate services to effectively address the structural causes of the high incidence of SGBV, HIV and SRHR-related issues.

The Ministry of Gender, Children and Social Protection (MoGSCP) has not received enough funding to prevent and respond to SGBV and HIV and promote realization of SRHR. Other relevant line ministries have also not prioritized any strategic interventions in their sector plans or allocated budget to complement the MoGSCP’s efforts.

There are several sociopolitical factors that pose a challenge to the eradication of GBV in Liberia. In addition to patriarchal norms that maintain the low social status of women and girls, the legacy of the Liberian Civil War has also contributed significantly to the perpetuation of gender-based violence and levels of violence against women, with rape being used as a weapon of war > remains. As a result, the nature and level of violence has, in an extent, been “normalized” in the now-post-conflict circumstances, "with a cultural acceptance of GBV that is often reinforced by religious leaders and community elders." According to the most recent Demographic and Health Survey (DHS) in 2013/16 show, for instance, that 41% of women aged 15–49 years reported having experienced sexual violence. Women dealing their wives if they burn the food, neglect the children, go out without telling them or refuse sex.

Child marriage, early initiation of sexual activity and teenage pregnancy are common in Liberia according to the DHS 2013, 24% of women age 25–49 had their first intercourse by the age of 15 and 78% by age 18, while 37% of women have had their first child by age 18. Statistics reveal that most girls were control by parents or guardians in rural areas compared to urban areas. Girls are more likely to live in rural communities than in urban areas. Because there is a strong correlation between child marriage and SRH (sexual reproductive health) outcomes, such as increased risk of HIV/AIDS infection, maternal mortality and morbidity, and health issues such as fistulas and uterine prolapse due to early pregnancy.

The most recent UNICEF data from 2008–2011 suggest that 44% of women and girls aged 15–19 are members of a society called Sandé. The Sandé society is led by traditional leaders called Zoës. The Sandé society is the trusted custodian of culture in much of Liberia and has been present in the region for centuries. Its purpose is to educate young girls about their roles as women, to prepare them for communal harmony and to prepare children for the rigors of adulthood. It also has a spiritual dimension. Girls are initiated into Sandé at puberty by Zoës. The initiates are called Sandé sisters (the male equivalent of Sandé) members are also adherents of Christianity or Islam. In order to join the Sandé society, which represents a rite of passage towards adulthood, women and girls need to undergo a series of rites of passage that include circumcision. Girls who do not wish to join the Sandé society are taken out of normal school and sent to "bush schools", compromising fulfillment of their right to education.

Regional and national consultations revealed that FGM is not the only HP in Liberia infringing on the rights of women and girls. Other HFs mentioned include forced confession by women to admit crimes they did not commit or face severe consequences; occurrence of women being witches and punishing women for witchcraft; and sexual abuse of women and girls.

Moreover, groups that are traditionally left behind are discriminated against, oppressed, stigmatized and abused, and live in constant fear of being attacked and beaten because of their condition, disability and/or sexual orientation. In addition, people with disability, including women and girls, are more at risk of violence and abuse than the general population. The lack of adequate infrastructure for capacity development and institutional change have yet to bear fruit and result in action, especially prevention of human rights violations against women and girls. Women and girls suffer from sexual freedom, social justice, social economic empowerment and access to health services; and suffer from denial of their fundamental rights to expression, equality and association. These have undergone HPs in Liberia.

Most cases of WAWG are reported by health facilities, NGOs and Liberia National Police (LNP)/Women and Child Protection Section (WCPS). In all cases reported by the GBV Unit of MGCP in 2017, medical treatment was provided to victims of sexual violence. The services provided include emergency obstetric care; immediate post-exposure prophylaxis for HIV; psychosocial support; and/or include treatment for sexually transmitted infections, post-exposure prophylaxis, tetanus toxoid vaccination, emergency contraceptives and psychosocial counseling. There is no specialised assistance nor services that are provided to women and girls with disability, refugees or displaced.

### Gender and climate change vulnerability

Availability of data concerning the differential impact of climate change on women and men in Liberia is limited. A Gender and Environment Index (GEI) case study of Liberia1 observed in 2015 that Liberia has made great progress in recognizing women's role in environmental decision making, as evidenced by number of women in leadership positions. However, gender inequalities remain pervasive in many customary social norms and practices continue to constrain women’s participation and representation. There is need for greater gender responsiveness in policy, and in the translation of policy into action, e.g., improved access to information and resources, and increased opportunities for women's empowerment in the pursuit of gender equality. These sorts of actions support women's greater decision-making and strengthen their resilience on climate change issues.

A recent study by the World Bank has demonstrated that women are more vulnerable to climate change2 in the sectors of forestry, Agriculture and fisheries has demonstrated that climate change affects women and men differently with women being affected mostly. The study has shown that even though both men and women rely on natural resources for their livelihoods, women have less access to land and other productive assets, which limits their ability to adapt to climate change and variation in the adaptive capacities of men and women. Women rely heavily on natural resources for their livelihood, the subsistence of their families, communities, education of their children, and health care.

Coastal erosion is causing the destruction of infrastructure, loss of property, displacement of people, decrease of income, rise of insecurity and of gender-based violence – all of which increase the vulnerability of women and men and exacerbate existing inequalities. Women are already facing:

Household-level small-scale enterprises, informal petty traders, in rural areas, in fisheries, breadwinners in households, educators in communities, women play important roles by constantly building resilient households and communities.

In the same manner as the Gender Agenda for Transformation3 of 2013, the new five-year national development plan, the National Development Plan (NDP) and the Pro-Poor Agenda for Prosperity and Development (PPAPD) charts a path towards accelerated, inclusive, and sustainable development for the Republic of Liberia. Under its Pillar I, “Power to the people” it also intends to improve the socio-economic and political status and capacity of women in all spheres of life. The NDP aims at reducing gender-based violence and harmful traditional practices; increase women’s participation in the community decision-making process; provide equal access for women and men to education and employment opportunities in agriculture, fisheries, Micro, Small and Medium Enterprises (MSMEs), and the formal sector. The PPAPD pillar “Economy and jobs” whose goal is to advance the macroeconomic environment enabling a vibrant private sector and job creation, and to ensure that growth translates into gains for the economy) pays a special attention to environmental issues and the management of natural resources. However, to make these goals a reality, the Government needs to highlight the different gender issues faced by women and men in vulnerable communities to enable transformative strategic interventions.

In the meantime, women and men have taken some empirical and pre-emptive actions to reduce the vulnerability and build the resilience of their households and communities against sea level rise. ## Other kinds of vulnerabilities

During the field visits and consultations for this proposal, children were identified as a vulnerable group. There is a rampant incidence of child labor, child street sellers, beggars and many school dropouts. Girls face more vulnerability especially within the family; they are at a higher risk of transactional sex, sexual and gender-based violence and forced early marriages. This explains the phenomenon of teen-age pregnancies as assessed above.


## Persons with Disabilities (PWDs) face discrimination in education and lack of user-friendly facilities and services; as well as in the job market as a result of social biases and stereotypes associated with disability. The National Action Plan for Inclusion of Persons with Disabilities in Liberia (2018 – 2022) stresses the need to promote full participation, inclusion and equal opportunities for PWDs. It states that “the right to full participation in national development of the country is critical for ensuring growing with equity. It is an expression or elaboration of the UN Convention on the Rights of Persons with Disabilities In practical terms it means that PWDs have access to education, employment and livelihood, health care, independent living and self-determination, and finally (j) access to justice and social protection.” The National Adaptation Plan (NAP) in Liberia recognizes the special needs of women with disabilities who are further discriminated against because they are women. Calls for special attention to be given to them.


## People Living with HIV/AIDS are also another vulnerable group. Stigma of HIV and communities exists as there is still insufficient understanding of issues related to HIV and AIDS and family members often abandon those living with HIV/AIDS need their support most. Women particularly bear the brunt of the burden of care for PLHIVs. The majority of women living with HIV/AIDS in Liberia have limited “safety nets” to assist and support them. There is a very small number of adolescent-friendly reproductive health services which are sparsely located in just few areas in Monrovia. This acute shortage of services has led to high rates of unintended pregnancies among young people, which also expose the risk of unwanted pregnancies which compound the challenges. Fear of negative effects, stigma and discrimination prevent many women from sharing their personal experiences, which has serious implications on accessing treatment and controlling HIV infections.


## Legal and Administrative Framework to address Gender inequality in Liberia


18


---
**Page 155**
---

The Constitution guarantees all persons, regardless of sex, the enjoyment of fundamental rights and freedom. Although there is no official definition of discrimination and discriminatory practices, Liberia is committed to the promotion of the rights of women through various national laws and policies. Significant progress has been made in this regard with the enactment of several laws that promote gender equality and women's empowerment. These include the Domestic Relations Law; the Inheritance Act of 1958 (which specifies Equal Rights in marriage and inheritance under Customary and Statutory Laws); the Rape Law of 2001, which criminalizes rape and provides for its punishment; the Child Marriage Prohibition Act of 2003; and the Anti-Human Trafficking Act of 2005, prohibiting trafficking in persons, although it is not explicit on trafficking of women and children for sexual purposes. All these policies and legislations affirm the Government's commitment to address gender inequality and to ensure that women are fully engaged in activities of their choice to benefit to the nation.

### Gender and women empowerment policy

The National Gender Policy (NGP) of 2017 is drafted in line with the Constitution of Liberia which is the supreme law of the land and takes precedence in establishing a framework for the Government to promote unity, liberty, stability, equality, justice and human rights with opportunities for social, economic and political advancement of the whole society, irrespective of gender. The policy document sets out the vision, mission, principles, goals, strategies and action plans of its strategic actions for 2018-2022 to be "Devlopng new or reviewing existing laws to eliminate gender insensitive provisions and to harmonize customary and civil laws in conformity with international human rights standards." **It is line with the National Development Plan (NDP) 2017-2035, which is a five-year plan for Prosperity and Development - promotes to harmonize the 2 systems of law in its short term and mid-term interventions.** Customary Law applies mainly to issues of marriage and inheritance and it is generally considered as discriminatory against women. It also prohibits divorce and Female Genital Mutilation (FGM). Although the statutory laws prohibit discriminatory practices, they make no specific provisions for protection against discrimination in the private or domestic sphere.

Even where some laws may exist (such as the Penal Code for Rape that has a provision for extensive prison term) women are still challenged with limited access to justice, particularly in the counties and rural areas. The lack of adequate infrastructure and resources in the justice sector has been exacerbated by the destabilization of courts during the conflict, inadequate staffing both in law enforcement and adjudication, inadequate capacity of the existing justice system to process cases, as well as limited knowledge and skills among women who are victims of gender-based violence (GBV). Women need to be capacitated to understand the law, know their rights, and be positioned to claim and exercise those rights. There is a pressing need for a comprehensive law review and a plan for reform and drafting of new laws that will address these gaps. The policy document sets out strategies to overcome some existing gaps, review of existing laws and the removal of any discriminatory and contradictory laws in the protection of women's fundamental freedom and rights as enshrined in the Constitution.

## Liberia Climate Change and gender Action Plan (coGAP)

In 2012, Liberia put in place a climate change Gender Action Plan (GAP) that is due for revision. Its aim is to ensure that gender equality is mainstreamed into climate change policies, programmes, and interventions. The plan identifies six hotspots where women are most vulnerable to the impacts of climate change; they are gaps to be filled. It stresses the importance of the fact that it is of utmost importance to deeply understand and analyse men and women's needs, priorities and challenges regarding climate change, and subsequently develop appropriate gender-sensitive policies.

Consultations with men and women have shown that around 70% of the population living in the identified six hotspots along the coastline of MAMF, without any further actions, sea level rise will affect a significant number them and will have a significant impact on their lives and livelihoods especially those dependent on fishing and agriculture.

The National Environmental Policy and Response Strategy on Climate change acknowledges the vital role that women play in conservation and the sustainable management of the environment, and aims to ensure their participation in decision making on the implementation of the environmental policy and its activities. Women are also recognized as a key stakeholder group and an integral part of the basic training, social development, environmental and natural resource management.

## The National Policy for Disaster Management

The Government of Liberia has recognized the importance of having measures for the prevention of disasters, or the mitigation of their effects, for preparedness and capacity building to effectively respond to any threatening disaster, and roles and responsibilities of ministries and agencies. To that

# Annex VIII Gender - Assessment & Action Plan

## GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL

effect, an Act establishing the National Disaster Management Agency and a The National Policy for Disaster Management was developed.[1] The policy establishes an institutional and operational framework that will drive successful implementation. Because DRM is interdisciplinarity and multi-dimensional in nature, it is important to ensure that all sectors are involved in building national and community resilience to disasters within the context of sustainable development. The National Disaster Management Agency (NDMA) is currently developing a National Disaster Risk Reduction Strategy (NDRRS). The NDRRS will be based on the DRM policy as a Guide for Emergency Response and Preparedness. Gender equality is a strong planning principle.

This policy recognizes the important role played by women in development and the burden they carry when suffering during disasters. Consequently, all activities implemented by the government and all its partners should take into account gender considerations. The policy also recognizes that the vulnerability of women and other vulnerable groups namely women, children, People Living With HIV/AIDS, the elderly, disabled and other vulnerable people at national, county, district and community/Chieftaindom level.

The policy further states that all DRM plans including contingency/preparedness plans shall have a gender strategy for emergency response informed by differentiated needs.

## The Proposed Actions are:

- **Strengthen Women's Security in Crisis:** Encourage women to take up representation and Leadership and Build their skills and confidence individually and within networks.
- **Promote Gender Equality in DRM** by incorporate gender analysis in the assessment of disaster risks.
- **Ensure Gender-Responsive Recovery** by providing equal economic opportunities for women including access to assets, such as land and credit, social protection and sustainable livelihoods. This will be achieved through the implementation of the National Policy on Women.
- **Develop Capacities and skills of men and women for Social Change before, during and after disasters reduce vulnerability to natural hazards, achieve equitable post-disaster reconstruction, build social cohesion and ease norms that stand against.

## IV - Lessons learned from climate resilience and gender programmes in Liberia

Liberia is still in the process of investing significant human and financial resources to both mitigation and adaptation. The country has made progress on several fronts including the National Climate Change Action Plan. In parallel, the New Revised Gender and PWD policies tackle gender inequality and social exclusion are led by the Ministry of Gender, Children and Social Protection (MoGCSP), which is relevant to disaster risk reduction and climate change adaptation.

These policies and their related programmes still need to integrate climate change adaptation programmes for the country's most vulnerable.

At the same time, financial support still be mobilized to address the climate change adaptation needs of vulnerable communities. The existing one more oriented in financing infrastructures development and research. There is also an urgent need for better coordination of stakeholders and

- **Capacity building of community members (men, women, youth) living in the MMA hotspots (including informal settlements):**
  - Motivation and awareness raising activities should continue for project ownership and success.
  - Communities should understand that capacity building and abstaining from developing aggressive coping strategies are key to achieving the overall objective of adaptation and disaster management strategies, rather than relief and response.
- **Capacity building of the institutions working on climate change adaptation should continue:**
  - The government (Ministry of Environment, Ministry of Disaster Management, etc.), local governments, NGOs, and other organizations should be supported in developing their capacity to manage funds and resources effectively. This includes training for staff at all levels (e.g., emergency funds and emergency plans and emergency response units) at the community level, and maintenance of community assets are essential to address immediate emergency needs.
  - Addressing gender inequality is critical; women's voices should be heard in decision-making processes because they have a different perspective on climate change. Women should be promoted because early response from external actors during disasters is most of the time inadequate or slow, and their strategies are not sustainable.
- **Partnerships between communities, government agencies, as well as between Government agencies, Non-Governmental Organizations, local authorities and community leaders should be reinforced:**
  - Ensuring that women's voices (including women, girls, youth, religious leaders, opinion leaders, minorities, and persons with disabilities in planning and implementing of climate resilience is critical.
- **Adaptive economic opportunities for year-round employment and earning opportunities for communities and women, especially for the youth, are critical for their awareness and livelihood adaptation:**
  - Livelihood support to vulnerable women, especially widows, single mothers, heads of households, and other family needs is necessary to be followed up ensure retention of assets and their productive use.
- **The social issues discussed during stakeholders' consultations also form development challenges that impact its support to initiatives that will improve the social well-being:**
  - From a gender perspective, women's voices should be heard in decision-making processes.
  - Ensure women's voices as well as men's voices are heard at all stages of adaptation and disaster management (from preparedness to recovery) planning.
- **Increase training for women and men on climate change adaptation and disaster risk reduction (flood/drought/salinity resilient (as appropriate in the area)):**
  - Maximize the diversification of livelihoods options to increase economic security.
  - Promote entrepreneurship for women and men to be the benefits and opportunities of the project.
  - Increase training in financial skills to plan for crisis and maximize marketing of goods before crisis.

- Ensure women’s safety in shelters, latrines and public spaces.

It has been proved that providing livelihood resilience support to poor women

- brings positive impact on their status within the household and in the community at large,
- helped to develop their confidence to move around the community and network with other women,
- yields greater levels of respect for women in the community, and

Therefore, in such a complex setting, the main challenge in this project design is to holistically address the different needs challenges and aspirations of women and men and capture them in the action plan.


## V - Recommendations

### 5.1 – Gender mainstreaming

In preparation of this project, UNDP conducted stakeholder consultations with a wide range of men and women in April 2013. These consultations were held in 22 coastal communities in MIMA and the focus group discussions are included as part of the feasibility study. One of the key aspects of the feasibility study was to identify the gendered vulnerabilities at household, individual, family and community levels; the gendered vulnerabilities to coastal hazards. This has clearly demonstrated that addressing these gendered vulnerabilities is critical for effective project implementation.

A compilation of the results of the 6 focus group discussion (FGD) findings form part of an additional annex to this proposal.

The different roles, priorities, aspirations and needs of men and women, and the ways in which both perspectives can be fully recognized and effectively utilized these gender in designing the project interventions. It is also important to recognize the invaluable role that women can play in resource mobilization and community development initiatives, as well as in shifting the perception from 'victims' to agents of change. By so doing, gender dimensions will be integrated for greater responsiveness in implementation and to obtain gender transformative results.

The gender analysis, through document review, stakeholder engagement, and consultation showed the need for:

- Engagement for the different stakeholders to consider gender Equality and women empowerment as a factor for success.
- Assessment of gender relations, roles and responsibilities, resource use and management, decision making and power relations in targeted communities in order to have relevant gender-

# Annex VIII Gender - Assessment & Action Plan

## Project Design

The project aims to propose some components and actions to create a paradigm shift that transforms vulnerable communities in the Mavrova Metropolitan Climate Resilience Project (MCMCRP) and contributes towards an inclusive, participatory and rights-based approach in its implementation.

### Key Components:

- **Gender mainstreaming**: This will be achieved by identifying needs and gaps in coping with climatic conditions as well as identifying gender-specific vulnerabilities.
- **Integration of sex and age-disaggregated data and indicators to establish a baseline by which progress can be measured**.
- **Consideration of both-gender and women specific interventions to incorporate into the coastal Gender Action Plan**.

## Project Design

The project will aim to propose some components and actions to create a paradigm shift that transforms vulnerable communities in the Mavrova Metropolitan Climate Resilience Project (MCMCRP) and contributes towards an inclusive, participatory and rights-based approach in its implementation. It addresses gender equality and women empowerment.

Men and women are targeted but including women as specific beneficiaries of the interventions will address their needs and vulnerabilities. The project will aim to improve women’s participation and women empowerment. It will contribute to change norms and behavior change through gender responsive training. The project also presents many advantages: ensuring equitable access to resources, services and infrastructure; increasing resilience in communities; and promoting sustainable livelihoods.

Furthermore, livelihood interventions supported by the project are not only climate resilient, chosen based on their resistance to increasingly SLR and vulnerability to extreme weather, but also allow to provide greater opportunities for women’s social empowerment. This will be achieved by helping women participate in the identification of needs and gaps in coping with climatic conditions as well as identifying gender-specific vulnerabilities.

From the gender analysis these are the key approaches that should be accounted for:

- **Creating an enabling environment for women's advancement**: The consultations have identified norms and social constraints which may contribute to more gendered vulnerability in a changing climate. By strengthening women’s voices and increasing their agency, the project can bring greater resilience dividends - not only for the women themselves, but also for their families and contributing towards the community as a whole.
- **Increasing women's voice and agency**: The project will attempt to improve women’s participation and provide more opportunities to their social empowerment. This will be achieved by helping women participate in the identification of needs and gaps in coping with climatic conditions as well as identifying gender-specific vulnerabilities.

By strengthening women’s voices and increasing their agency the project can bring greater resilience dividends - not only for the women themselves, but also for their families and contributing towards the community as a whole. The active participation of women and girls in decision-making processes and women and adolescent girls' active engagement will ensure the effectiveness of the programme.
